# Changelog

All notable changes to this project are documented in this file.

## Unreleased

### Fixed

- A click beside the box of four dialogs closed them, and what was typed into them was gone: the managers of credential profiles and of login sequences, Quick Connect and the confirmation of a multi-line paste. In a manager it closed the whole dialog, also from the form for a new or edited entry, without asking. A click was not needed for it either: a press in a field and a release beside the box is a click on what holds both, which is the overlay, so selecting a field's text with the pointer and letting go outside the box closed the dialog as well. The dialogs that 0.10.0 changed lost this then (see **Removed** there, "Close dialogues by clicking outside"); the credential manager and Quick Connect were left out, and the other two were written afterwards. None closes on a click beside it now: a manager closes by **Close** and its form by **Cancel**, Escape leaves the form first and then the manager, and the other two close by **Cancel** and Escape. `docs/DESIGN.md` §5.6 has the rule. With no dialog left that closes this way, the strip of a dialog's own messages no longer keeps its clicks from the overlay, which was for the two managers. Covered by tests in `src/components/DialogClickBeside.test.tsx`: a click beside each of the four; a press in a field with the release beside the box for both managers and Quick Connect, with what was typed still there; each of the four still closed by its button and by Escape, and a manager's form left by Escape before the manager; and, read from the source of every file in `src/components`, that no dialog's overlay has a handler for a click, a press or a release, so that a dialog written later does not bring the click back; a file has to have as many overlays that the test can read as it has dialogs, by their `<DialogKeys>`, so one written in a way it cannot read fails it too. The four with a click beside the box, the three with a press in a field and the one over the source fail without the change; the ones for the buttons and Escape pass either way, as does the one that tries the reading of the source on tags written for it. eslint, prettier, the type check and the frontend tests are clean. The test environment makes no click out of a press and a release, so the tests send the click a browser would. The change was confirmed by hand in the running app, on Windows; which of the four dialogs were tried there is not on record, and nothing was tried on macOS or Linux. The test over every component's overlay and the checks that each dialog still closes were asked for by the pre-commit review and audit of this change; see **Known issues** for what they found in these dialogs beside it.
- Smaller faults in the two managers and in Quick Connect, found by the pre-commit review and audit of the fix above and of these fixes:
  - **A save in a manager's form was sent as often as it was asked for.** A double click on **Save**, or Enter pressed twice, while the first save was under way created the credential profile or the login sequence twice. A form now sends one save at a time: a second submit is dropped, and **Save** is greyed out until the first has its answer. After a save that failed the form is as it was, with the reason, and can be saved again; the next try takes the reason away.
  - **A save's answer went to whatever form was open by then.** With a form left while its save was under way, by **Cancel** or Escape, and another one opened, the answer closed that other form with what had been typed into it, or put its error there. The answer is now for the form the save was sent from: that form closes, and one opened since stays. A failure of a save whose form was left is shown with the manager, in the strip of its messages, where it was shown in no place at all once the form was gone. A save is its form's own in the other direction too: a form opened while the save of one that was left is still under way has a **Save** of its own, and waits for nothing. What waits is the entry: a stored profile or sequence whose save is still under way has its **Edit** and **Delete** greyed out in the list until that save has its answer, also in a manager that was closed and opened again meanwhile: the store that sends the change keeps track of it, not the dialog. Of two saves of one entry the later could otherwise land first, and the earlier then put back what it held, an old password included, with nothing said.
  - **What was typed into a form while its save was under way was lost.** It was no part of the save, and went with the form when the save's answer closed it. While its save is under way a form now takes nothing: its fields, its **Browse…** and its **Save** are disabled, and a list of the type of a profile that is open closes. **Cancel** and Escape still leave it, as before.
  - **The focus was lost with a save that failed.** What is disabled while it has the focus loses it, so after a save that failed the keyboard had to find its way back into the form. The focus is given back to what had it when the save was sent, unless the user has moved it elsewhere meanwhile or another dialog lies over the manager by then.
  - **The stored password could replace one that was being typed.** Editing a password profile reads the stored password into the form, which takes a moment. One that came after a new password had been typed replaced it, and a save then kept the old password with nothing said. It also came into a form whose profile had been switched to a key meanwhile, and into a form of the same profile that had been opened since. It is now put into the form it was read for, and only while that form is a password profile's and its field is empty.
  - **A password was saved with a profile that is no password profile.** A profile switched from a password to a key or to keyboard-interactive in its form kept the password the form held, read or typed, and a save sent it along: for a new profile it was written to the keychain, which no connection with such a profile reads, and for a stored one it was written there again. A password is now sent with a password profile alone. See **Known issues** for the password that a stored profile has in the keychain when it is switched.
  - **A file dialog for a key that failed said nothing.** In the credential manager and in Quick Connect, a failure of the dialog behind **Browse…** went to the console as an unhandled rejection. The form now shows it, in the line its other errors are in, and the credential manager shows it with its messages once that form was left or saved; Quick Connect had no such line and has one. The message is the dialog plugin's own, in English. In the credential manager it goes when a file is chosen or the save is tried again, and in Quick Connect when **Browse…** or **Connect** is tried again or the port or the protocol is changed. In the credential manager the chosen file is also put into the form it was asked from, as that form is when the dialog closes. It went into the form as it was when the dialog opened: what was typed meanwhile was undone, and a form that was left meanwhile was opened again, over another one that had been opened since.
  - **Quick Connect passed any port on.** The field has the range 1 to 65535, which the webview holds a form to, but the form itself took whatever number it was given and left the refusal to the backend. It now refuses a port outside the range, or one that is no whole number, with the message the session dialog has for it, which goes when the port or the protocol is changed. An empty field is still the protocol's own port; a `0`, which counted as empty, is refused.
  - **Quick Connect said nothing of an empty host.** With no host or, for SSH, no user name, **Connect** did nothing. The two are required fields now: the webview does not send a form with one of them empty and says so at the field, in its own words and language, as it does for a port outside the field's range. A field with spaces alone is as empty, so it is emptied and the webview asked to say the same of it. The app has no message of its own for this, which would be a text in each of the 14 UI languages.
  - **Quick Connect's clearing of the password did nothing.** It set the field's state to empty when the dialog closed, which a component that is going away does not act on, under a comment that said it cleared the credential. The code and the comment are gone. The password is in the component's state while the dialog is open and is dropped with it, as before.
  - **The heading over a login sequence's steps was a label of no field.** It is a heading now, and the steps are a group that it names, as a screen reader announces it.

  Covered by tests in `src/components/DialogForms.test.tsx`. For each of the two managers: a save sent once for two submits with nothing rendered between them and for one that comes later, for a new entry and for a changed one; a save that can be sent again after one that failed and for another entry after one that went through, and a form without a name that sends none and holds up none; a form that takes nothing while its save is under way and can still be left, and the focus given back after a save that failed, or left where the user has gone; a form opened while a save was under way, left as it is by that save's answer, with a save of its own that the other neither holds up nor ends; a stored entry kept from another change and from being deleted until its save went through or failed, also in a manager that was closed and opened again, with the entry beside it left free, and in each store two changes of one entry recorded each for itself until it has its answer; and a failure shown with the manager once its form was left, for another form and for the list. For the stored password: read into the form of its profile, in a field that does not show it, and not over a password typed before it came, into a profile switched to a key, or into a form opened for the profile since. For the password a form holds: sent with a new password profile, with one that was switched away and back, and with a stored one whose password was changed, and not with a new or a stored profile that was switched to a key or to keyboard-interactive, under any name. For the list of a profile's type: closed when the form's save starts. For the file dialog: its failure shown in both forms, and with the manager once the form was saved; a chosen file put into a form with what was typed since, and none into a form that was left or into another opened since; and the reason of a failed save left in the form until a file is chosen. For Quick Connect: five ports that are refused, four that are passed on, Telnet's own for an empty field, and the message gone once the port or the protocol is changed; the host and, for SSH, the user name as required fields; a field of spaces alone emptied before the webview is asked; and both passed on without the spaces around them. And the steps as a named group, with no label in the form that is no field's.

  How many of these tests fail without the fixes, and which changes to the fixes were tried against them, is in the entry below, with the tests that came after. eslint, prettier, the type check and the frontend tests are clean. The app itself was not started for these, so none of it has been seen: that the forms lie as they did, now that their fields are inside a `fieldset` without a box of its own, in each of the three webviews; how a form looks while its fields are disabled, for the moment a save takes, and how the error line in Quick Connect looks; that the focus comes back in a webview, which takes it from a disabled field where the test environment leaves it; what each webview says at a required field that is empty, and that all three say something; and no file dialog was made to fail outside the tests. The answer for the form it was asked from, the stored password, the message that goes, and the five faults that were first listed under **Known issues** and fixed afterwards were asked for by the rounds of the pre-commit review and audit of these changes. See **Known issues** for what was found with these and kept.
- What the fixes above left open in the two managers and in the session dialog, listed under **Known issues** until now:
  - **A password profile that was switched to another type kept its password in the keychain.** A stored profile changed from a password to a key or to keyboard-interactive had its row changed and its password left in the OS keychain, where no connection with such a profile reads it, until the profile was deleted. The backend now deletes it with the change. A profile that is switched back to a password has none, and takes the one that is typed then. The row is changed first, so that a change that cannot be made, as one to a name that is taken, costs no password. Where the keychain then refuses the deletion, the row is put back as it was: nothing is changed, the save says so, and it can be tried again. Only where the row cannot be put back either is the profile changed with its password still there, which the save says as well. Beside that, the backend no longer stores a password that is sent with a profile of another type, for a new profile as for a changed one, whoever sends it (the form has not sent one since the fix above), nor an empty one in place of the password a profile has, which a change could before though the form never sent one. Deleting a profile that has no secret no longer logs a failure of the keychain. And after a change of a profile that failed, the manager's list is read again, since the backend may have made the change in part.
  - **Two changes of one entry could take their steps in turns.** A change of a credential profile is two steps, its row and its secret, and the commands ran side by side: two changes of one profile could leave the row of the one with the secret of the other, and the manager's read of the stored password could come between the two steps of a change. The window keeps a second change from being sent (see above), which a page that is loaded again forgets while a command of the old page may still run. The backend now takes one change or one deletion of an entry at a time, for credential profiles and for login sequences, and the manager's read of a profile's password waits for them. An entry waits for itself alone: a keychain that does not answer holds up the entry it was asked about and no other. A connection that reads a profile's password does not wait; see **Known issues**.
  - **A deletion that was under way was not kept track of.** An entry that was being deleted could be opened for a change, and a second confirmation that came before the first was rendered sent the deletion twice, the second one failing. A deletion is now recorded as a change is: the entry's **Edit** and **Delete** are greyed out until it has its answer, also in a manager that was closed and opened again, and it is sent once.
  - **Nothing said why an entry could not be changed.** The row of an entry whose change or deletion is under way now shows it: the text of its row is dimmed, with the pointer of something in progress over it, and it says to a screen reader that it is busy. It has no words for it, which would be a text in each of the 14 UI languages.
  - **The session dialog said nothing of an empty name or host.** **Save** did nothing. The two are required fields now, as the host and the user name of Quick Connect are (see above), with the same emptying of a field of spaces alone, and in the webview's own words.

  Covered in the backend by tests of what a change does to a profile's secret (stored for a password profile that is sent one and kept without one; deleted for a password profile that becomes another, also where a password is sent along; the keychain left alone for a profile that was none; and an empty secret taken for none), and of the lock of an entry (a second holder waits for the first, another entry does not wait, and an entry that is given up leaves no lock behind). One more test writes to the real OS keychain and deletes from it, and so runs only when asked for (`--ignored deleting_if_present`): an entry that is deleted is gone, and deleting one that is not there is no failure. It was run on Windows. The commands themselves, which need the keychain and a database, have no test: that a profile switched in the app has its password deleted was not tried. In the frontend, tests in `src/components/DialogForms.test.tsx` cover, for each manager: an entry that is being deleted kept from a change and from a second deletion, also in a manager that was closed and opened again, with its deletion sent once for two confirmations with nothing rendered between them; an entry free again after a deletion that failed, with the question gone; and the row of an entry that says it is busy, to a screen reader and by its look, while its change or its deletion is under way, and no other row. In each store a deletion is recorded as a change is, and the store of the profiles reads its list again after a change that failed, keeps the profile as one whose change is under way until it has, and tells of the failure also where the list cannot be read. `src/components/SessionDialog.test.tsx` covers the name and the host as required fields, a field of spaces alone emptied before the webview is asked, both emptied where both are, and both saved without the spaces around them.

  The two files have eighty-one tests, with those of the entry above. Fifty-five fail against the two managers, Quick Connect and the session dialog as they were without any of these fixes, with the stores as they are. The twenty-six that pass either way are, in each manager, the form without a name, the save after one that went through, the focus left where the user has gone and the entry free again after a deletion that failed; the seven of the stores, which those components do not use for this; and beside them the four ports that are passed on, Telnet's own port, the stored password read into its form, the password sent with a new profile, with one switched away and back and with a stored one that was changed, the host passed on without spaces, and the session saved without them. Twenty-five changes to the fixes were tried against the tests, each alone and with the rest in place, and each makes tests of what it takes away fail: the guard reading the rendered state and not the ref; every answer counted for the form that is open; a save closing its form without counting it as left; the stored password put into its form whatever that holds; the guard not given back when a form is opened or left; the fields left enabled while a save is under way; the focus not given back, and the focus given back wherever the user has gone; the end of any save taken for the open form's; a change that is under way not recorded by the store, and a deletion not recorded; what was under way left recorded after its answer; the end of one change taking every record of its entry; every entry kept while the change of one is under way; the password sent whatever the profile's type, and with every type but a key; the list of the type not told of a save; the webview asked before a field of spaces is emptied, in Quick Connect and in the session dialog; the host not required in Quick Connect, and the name not required in the session dialog; a deletion sent as often as it is confirmed; a row that does not say it is busy, and one that does not show it; and the list not read again after a change that failed. clippy, rustfmt, eslint, prettier, the type check and the frontend tests are clean. The app itself was not started: how a busy row looks, and what the webview says at the session dialog's fields, have not been seen. Listed under **Known issues** since the fixes above; the deletion of the password was decided when they were listed, and the row put back where the keychain refuses, the lock of an entry by itself and the empty password were asked for by the rounds of the pre-commit review and audit of this change. See **Known issues** for what remains.
- `npm audit` reported `minimatch` 10.2.5 (GHSA-g3hv-75wg-m249, a denial of service through a pattern), under `typescript-eslint`: development tooling, not in the shipped app, and listed under **Known issues** when 0.40.0 was released. `npm audit fix` moved it to 11.0.9 within the existing ranges, with `typescript-eslint` 8.60.1 → 8.73.0, and along with it `vite` 8.3.5 → 8.3.8, `rolldown` 1.2.16 → 1.2.19 and `@vitejs/plugin-react` 6.0.2 → 6.3.0, which build the frontend; only `package-lock.json` changes. `minimatch` 11 brings one new package, `@isaacs/nonrecursive-glob-match`, and `rolldown` 1.2.19 lists a WebAssembly binding as optional and for the `wasm32` CPU only, with five optional packages under it, two of them at a prerelease that the binding pins (`@emnapi/core` and `@emnapi/runtime` 2.0.0-beta.2); npm installs none of the six on the platforms the app is built on. Every package still comes from the npm registry, and none of the new ones has an install script. Built on Windows before and after, the frontend's four script files and its main stylesheet have the same content hashes in their names; eslint, the type check and the 604 frontend tests pass with the new versions. The app itself was not started.
- The session search did not look at folders, though `docs/DESIGN.md` §5.3 listed the folder path among what it covers. It matched a query against a session's name, host, user name and tags, so a search for a folder's name found only the sessions that had the word in one of those themselves, and nothing of the folder. A folder whose name has the query in it is now a result of the search, and lists what is in it.
  - **What is found.** A folder by its own name, by the rules a session is found by: the query anywhere in the name and taken as written, with `%`, `_` and `\` standing for themselves, cut at 200 bytes, and nothing for a query with a NUL in it. The names of the folders above a folder are not looked at, so `Production/EU` finds neither. A session is found by its own fields as before, and not by the name of its folder: it is listed under that folder when the folder is found. In PostgreSQL mode a search finds the folders the user can see, their own and other users' shared ones, as the tree has them.
  - **What the sidebar lists.** The folders that were found first, and then the sessions that were, as before. Under a folder is what is in it, as the tree shows it: its folders, open or closed as they are in the tree, and its sessions. The folder itself is open, however it is in the tree, until it is closed in the results, with its arrow, a double click or Enter. That is for the search and leaves the tree's own open folders as they are. A folder that was closed stays closed while the search is on, also when the query changes; once the field is cleared, the next search starts with every folder open again, and so does one after a refresh whose search failed, which shows the tree. A folder that was found has its menu in the results, and F2 and Delete act on it as in the tree. One that the tree does not show, because a folder above it is not among those the user can see, is not listed, as a session in such a folder was not.
  - **Where a folder is.** Two folders can have the same name, so a folder that was found has the folders above it beside its name: the two nearest, after an ellipsis where there are more (`… › Production › EU`), since the nearest folders are what tells two apart most often. The tooltip has more of the path, up to the eight nearest folders, and the tooltip of the name has the folder itself at its end. In PostgreSQL mode these names can be another user's, and a path is read for where a folder is, so a name in it is shown so that it cannot be read as another path:
    - The characters that change how text displays are written out as their code points, as **My Login** shows a name, and a name is cut at 64 characters, beside the folder at 24.
    - The `›` between the names and the `…` for the folders left out are written out as well where a name has one, so that one folder cannot read as two, one in the other, or as more folders than there are. A backslash in a name is doubled, so that the text `\u{203A}` in a name is told from the character written out.
    - Each name stands between isolates, in a path that goes from left to right. Two names in a right-to-left script next to each other would otherwise take the `›` between them into their direction and show in the other order, the folder before the one it is in.

    See **Known issues** for what a name can still look like.
  - **An item can be listed twice.** A session under its folder and as a result of its own. A folder that was found inside another that was is listed by its name under that one, where it is not opened, and with its content as a result of its own. Selecting one marks both. Nothing in the results can therefore be dragged to another place, as the tree's items can: the items under a folder that was found are not sortable, and each has a drag-and-drop ID of its own.
  - **A search lists no more than what it finds and what is in that.** Four things kept the results from costing more than their length, which matters in PostgreSQL mode, where another user can share any number of folders, as deep as they like and with names as long as they like, with a common letter in their names. The tree went through every folder and every session for each folder it listed, which would have been every folder a search finds: the folders and sessions are now sorted by the folder they are in once, for the tree and the results alike. A path has the eight nearest folders and no more, where a chain of folders that are all found would have had a path as long as the chain put together for each of them. A folder that was found is opened as a result of its own only, where such a chain, open in the tree, would have been listed once for every folder of it. And a name is cut by going through its start only: the cut counted the characters of the whole name first, which the app's commands hold to 255 bytes and a row written past them does not, and a path shows the same name once for each folder below it. That cut is the one **My Login** and **My Highlighting** show a name, a host and a user name by, and they take the same from it. What a search lists is still as long as what it finds, as the tree is as long as what is open in it.
  - **The backend.** `session_search` returns the folders with the sessions, as `{ folders, sessions }`, from two statements that run one after the other, so a search still holds one of the app's database connections at a time. They are two reads and not one view of the database, as a rule (see **Fixed**, on the limit of a search's statements): a change someone makes between them shows in one and not the other until the next search. The command is the same, so nothing changes in the app's manifest, and the database gets no migration. A search is now two reads where it was one; the entry on the pace of the search below has what limits how often it runs.
  - **Enter at a session that was found connects once.** A row of a session in the results takes Enter itself and passed it on to the list as well, which connects to the selected session on Enter. After a click on the row that is the same session, so the Enter asked for two connections. The row now keeps the key. This was read from the code while the list was moved into a component of its own (`src/components/SearchResults.tsx`), and not tried in the running app before the change.

  Tests cover, in the backend: a folder found by its name at any depth, in the order the folders are listed in, and not by a session in it; the backslash, the wildcards and a NUL in a query; the cut of a long query, for a folder and a session; and what `session_search` puts together. One more runs against a real server (`--ignored search_finds`, verified against PostgreSQL 18): a role finds its own folders and sessions and another user's shared ones, and not that user's personal ones. In the frontend, for the list: the path of a folder, also where a folder above is missing or the folders make a loop, and that it has eight folders at the end of a chain of five thousand; how a path is shown, with a separator, an ellipsis, a backslash, a bidi override or right-to-left letters in a name, and with a name cut inside what is written out or between the halves of a character; a name of eight megabytes shown two thousand times within two seconds; the tree sorted once, and listed as before; the order of the list; a session listed twice; a folder that was found inside another, by its name there and not opened; a chain of forty folders that are all found and open in the tree, listed in seventy-nine rows; a folder closed and opened in the results without a change to the tree; a folder inside it opened as in the tree; the menu and the double click; the Enter that connects once; that a press starts a drag at an item of the tree and at nothing in the results; and that the copies of an item have drag-and-drop IDs of their own. For the sidebar, from the field to the list: what a search lists, without a folder that the tree does not show; Enter at a folder that was found, in the results and not in the tree; a folder that stays closed for another query and is open again after the field was cleared; and a refresh after someone else's change, which lists what the search finds then. The tests of the Enter and of the drag fail without the change they are for. The app itself was not started: the list was tried in these tests only, so how the path beside a name fits the sidebar's width, and how a tooltip shows a path, have not been looked at. Listed under **Known issues** since 0.40.0. What a flood or a chain of another user's folders costs, the names in a path and the weak spots of the first tests were found by the two rounds of the pre-commit review and audit of this change.
- Nothing limited how often the session search ran, listed under **Known issues** until now. The field searched at every change of its text, and each search reads every folder and every session the user can see, in PostgreSQL mode with two statements against the shared database. Searches that were asked for faster than a slow database answered them ran side by side: they could take all ten of the app's database connections, so that the user's other changes and connects failed for want of one, and an answer that came late, to an earlier query, replaced the results of the query the field showed. Five things limit it now, all but the fourth in both database modes.
  - **The field waits for a pause.** A text is searched for once it has stood for 250 ms. A word that is typed without a pause that long is one search, where it was one for each of its characters. The field shows each character at once, and the list follows when the typing pauses. A text that is cleared within the pause is not searched for.
  - **The window sends one search at a time.** While a search is under way the next one waits for its answer, and is then one search for the text the field has by then, however often it changed meanwhile. A text that is typed while a search is under way still has its pause first. The search that a refresh brings, after an edit or when the data changed elsewhere, takes its turn with the others: it is sent when that turn comes, without a pause, and in place of the search a pause was running for. The refresh no longer waits for that search, so an edit is done when the folders and sessions are read again, and the results follow when the search has its answer; for that moment the list can still show what the edit took away, as it could before, while the refresh's search ran. An answer is shown only if the field still has the text it was asked with.
  - **The backend runs one search at a time.** `session_search` waits for the search that is running, without a database connection. A search that waits when a later one asks is answered with an error at once, so no more than one waits, however many ask, and what waits holds its query as it is searched for, which is its first 200 bytes and as far beyond as the character at that place goes. The window, which sends one search at a time, gets no such answer from its own searches. This is for what the window does not keep to, such as a page that was loaded again while a search of the old page is still running.
  - **A search's statements have 10 s.** In PostgreSQL mode each of the two gives up after 10 s, where the app's other statements have a minute (see the lock timeouts under **Fixed** of 0.40.0): what a search finds is for a list in the sidebar, and one that takes longer keeps the next search waiting and loads the shared server for nothing. The two statements are one transaction now, on one connection, for the limit alone, which is set for that transaction and ends with it: they are still two reads, each of the database as it is when it runs, at PostgreSQL's default isolation level; where the server or the role sets a stricter one, both read the database as it was when the transaction began to read. A limit of 10 s or less that is in effect stays, and a longer one, or none, gives way to the 10 s for the search, also one that the server sets, which the app keeps for everything else. A statement that waits for a lock another session holds gives up before that as a rule, after the 5 s of the app's lock timeout, as before; where the server sets a lock timeout of its own, which the app keeps, it gives up at that, and at the 10 s at the latest, as it does where a connection pooler drops the session's settings. The price is statements: a search is six to the server where it was two, with the transaction's begin and end and the two that read and set its limit, or five where a shorter limit stays. In round trips that is eight where it was six: the pool checks a connection when it is taken and when it goes back, and a search takes one connection now where it took two, one after the other. Those other steps do not have the 10 s, but the limit of the app's other statements: a minute, or what the server sets, and none where a connection pooler drops the session's settings. And the transaction keeps the lock that the read of `folders` takes until it ends, so through the read of `sessions`, where each statement let its lock go when it was done: what waits for every use of that table to end, as a change to its definition by the owning role's setup does, waits for a search that much longer. A transaction that takes both tables for itself, `sessions` first, as a migration that changes both may, or a role's own `LOCK TABLE`, can now meet a search the other way round, each waiting for the table the other has: the server then ends one of the two, the search, which fails like any other, or the other one; a migration that is ended is tried again at the next start of the owning role. A statement that runs into the limit fails the search, which is treated as any other that fails, so where the folders or the sessions a user can see cannot be read within 10 s each, that user's searches fail every time (see **Known issues**). In SQLite mode, where the database is the user's own file, a search has no such limit.
  - **A search gives up its turn after 60 s.** In PostgreSQL mode a search waits up to 5 s for a connection and its two statements run up to 10 s each, so one that the server answers is done well before that as a rule. Not always: the other steps of its transaction have the limit of the app's other statements, a minute as a rule, and the read of the local database that follows has no limit, so a search can outlast its turn on a server that is slow over those steps as well. One without an answer by then, as on a connection that has gone silent without being closed, which brings none of the server's limits to the app, is answered with an error, and the next search may run; its statement is left to end by itself, on the connection it has. A search is refused while three that were given up have not ended. In PostgreSQL mode searching therefore takes one of the app's ten connections at a time, and up to three while searches that were given up have not ended.

  The command's name and parameter are the same, so nothing changes in the app's manifest. A search that fails is treated as before: the list keeps what it showed, and after a refresh it shows the tree (see **Known issues**). Where a search's task ends without an answer, as by a panic, the window is told that the search failed and the application log has the reason. A search that is under way is still not called off, so on a slow database the next one waits for it; see **Known issues** for that and for what a single search costs.

  Covered in the backend by seven tests of the gate. Of three searches that ask while one runs, all but the last are answered as replaced when the next one asks, while the first still runs, the last one runs when the first is done, and one that asks while that one runs waits for it and runs. Of forty-nine that ask while one runs, one is left waiting, and it alone runs, with none beside another. A search that is replaced in the moment the turn comes free does not take it, tried sixty-four times over. A search keeps its turn until it has its answer, with the next one answered after it, and one that waits when a later one asks is told so and is not run. A search is answered with what it found or with its own error, and with an error that has nothing of the panic in it where it panics, which holds up no later search. One without an answer is given up when its limit is up, the next one runs beside it, and it still goes on to its end once it is answered. And with three given up, the next is refused and not run, until one of them has ended. Seven changes to the gate were tried against these tests, each alone, and each fails at least one. The command itself, which needs the app's state, has no test, and the turn's limit was tried at 20 and 50 ms, not at its 60 s. The limit of a search's statements is part of the test that runs against a real server (`--ignored search_finds`, verified against PostgreSQL 18): with the folders locked by another session, on a connection that gives up on no lock, a search fails as a statement that took too long after 10 s, where no limit is in effect and where one of a minute is, as the app's own connections have it, and after 3 s where a limit of 3 s is in effect; with a lock timeout of 2 s, where the app's connections have 5 s, it fails after that, as a lock it did not get; the limit that was in effect is back after each search, none where there was none, and the next search finds what it should, as the role it was. A search that has not failed after 30 s fails the test. Three changes to the limit were tried against it, each alone, and each fails it: no limit set for the search, the limit set over a shorter one that is in effect, and a limit of 20 s. That test does not run with the others, and takes some 25 s. In the frontend, `src/stores/sessionStore.search.test.ts` has twenty-five tests of the pace, with a clock the test moves: the pause of 250 ms; one search for three changes that each come within the pause of the one before, and none before the pause is over; one search at a time, the next one with the later of two texts, and the answer for a text the field no longer has not shown; the text sent as it is when the turn comes, and not once more after its pause; a text left its pause where nothing had asked for a search while one was under way; an answer shown for a text the field has again, with the one more search that the last change's pause brings; a text with a space more taken for another text and sent as it is; no search for a text that is cleared within the pause, for one that an empty text follows or for spaces alone, and a pause of its own for a text typed right after a clear; the answer of a search dropped whose field was cleared; and the results kept at a search that fails, with the next text searched for. For a refresh: a search sent with it and not waited for; none without a text; one search for two refreshes while a search is under way, and one for a refresh and a text typed meanwhile, with that text; a search of its own for a refresh that comes while the one after an earlier refresh is under way; its answer not shown for a text the field no longer has; one search where a refresh comes within a pause; the results taken away when its search fails, and not when the next search of the field does; the results kept when a search of the field that was under way fails, and taken away when the refresh's own does, also where that one is shared with a text typed meanwhile; and nothing left behind by a refresh whose field was cleared before its turn came, or that had no text to search for. Seventeen changes to the pace were tried against them, each alone: sixteen fail at least one test, and the seventeenth, a pause left running after the field was cleared, changes nothing that can be seen, since the pause then finds an empty field and sends nothing. `SessionSidebar.search.test.tsx` has one more test, of the field itself: three changes send no search, and with a fourth they are one search, for which the list waits. The app itself was not started for this, and no test ran against a slow server or one that does not answer: how the pause feels while typing, and what the sidebar shows while a search waits for a slow one, have not been seen. The limit of a search's statements came after the rest, and with it the turn went from 150 s to 60 s. The pre-commit review and audit of the first change, in three rounds, found what the gate and the window did not hold to at first, and the weak spots of the first tests: every search that asked waited for its turn before it was told that it was replaced, with the whole query it was sent; nothing ended a search without an answer, which kept every later search waiting, and then nothing limited how many such searches were left behind; a refresh, and with it an edit, waited for searches asked for after it; and the text of a panic went to the window. Those of the statements' limit, in three rounds as well, found that a search without its limit kept its test waiting where it should have failed it; that a search which had found was failed by an end of its transaction that did not go through; and what the first account of the limit left out: the lock timeout that comes before it, the steps that do not have it, the lock kept between the two reads, and that another role can keep a search from ever finishing.

### Known issues

- The two managers, Quick Connect, the session dialog and the paste confirmation have smaller faults that were found with the click beside a dialog and left as they are when the others were fixed (see **Fixed**). A login sequence's response field shows what is typed, a password included, and neither it nor the pattern field switches off the webview's spell check and autofill. Editing a password profile reads the stored password into the form, masked; when that read fails the field is empty and nothing says so, which leaves the stored password as it is at a save. A profile that is switched to a password without one being typed is a password profile without a password; a connection with it fails and says so. So is one whose new password the keychain refuses to store: the other changes to it are made by then and are not undone, and the save says that it failed. Where the keychain refuses to delete the password of a profile that is switched to another type and the row can then not be put back either, the profile is changed with its password still in the keychain, as one that was switched before this version is. A password profile that was switched to a key or to keyboard-interactive before the switch deleted its password (see **Fixed**) still has it in the keychain, until the profile is deleted, or switched to a password and away again. A passphrase for a key is not kept for a profile, and a secret that is sent with a key profile is no longer stored. A form whose save never has its answer stays disabled until it is left by **Cancel** or Escape; the save then goes on, whatever it does in the end is shown with the manager only if it fails, and the entry it is for, if it is a stored one, shows as busy and cannot be changed or deleted until the window's page is loaded again, as a restart of the app does. The backend takes one change of an entry at a time, within one running app: a second instance of the app is not held to it, it does not say in which order two changes arrive, and a connection that reads a profile's password does not wait for a change of it, so it can find the password of a profile gone or not yet there for a moment, and fails then. The word for a field that is required and empty, in Quick Connect and in the session dialog, is the webview's own, in the webview's language and not the one chosen for the app, and has not been seen in the app. The paste confirmation shows the text as it is, so invisible characters and those that reorder text are not written out, and of a paste longer than 16384 characters it shows the start and says so. Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit review and audit of the click beside a dialog and of the fixes that followed it, and kept by decision.
- A session search that fails says nothing, and leaves the results of the search before under the new query. The backend returns the database's own message (`Failed to search sessions: …`, or `folders`), as for a lock that is held or a connection that is gone in PostgreSQL mode, or one of its own for a search it gave up or did not run (see **Fixed**), and the sidebar drops it: the field shows what was typed and the list what an earlier query found, with nothing to tell the two apart. A search that fails when the sidebar refreshes, after an edit or when the data changed elsewhere, drops it as well and shows the whole tree instead, with the query still in the field. The message itself is the database's, in English and with whatever the server put in it, and would have to be worded by the app before it is shown, as the errors of a start are. Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit review and audit of the search field's menu.
- The session search matches a session's tags as the text they are stored as, and treats upper and lower case differently on the two backends, in a folder's name as in a session's fields. Tags are kept as a JSON list (`["core","lab"]`), and the query is looked for anywhere in that text, so `"`, `[` or `,` finds nearly every session, and a query can match across two tags' quotation marks. SQLite's `LIKE` ignores case for the letters of ASCII only, where PostgreSQL's `ILIKE` goes by the database's locale, so `zürich` can find `ZÜRICH` in PostgreSQL mode and does not in SQLite mode. Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit review and audit of the search field's menu.
- A session search that is under way is not called off, and nothing shows that one is. sqlx cannot cancel a statement, and a query that the app gives up on goes on running on the server, with its connection waiting for the answer. So the search for a later text waits for the one under way (see **Fixed**): in PostgreSQL mode for up to 10 s for each of its two statements on a shared database that is slow, and up to the 60 s a search keeps its turn where the server does not answer at all. The list shows the tree or what an earlier text found until then, with nothing to tell that from results for the text in the field. What a single search costs is unchanged apart from that limit: it reads every session and every folder the user can see, since its pattern begins with a wildcard, which no index serves, so each running app, which is each user's as a rule, can have one such statement running on a shared database at a time, and up to three while searches that were given up after 60 s have not ended. The limit has a price of its own: where the folders or the sessions a user can see cannot be read within 10 s each, that user's searches fail, every time, where they had a minute. That goes by what the database holds, to which every role adds with what it shares. It is also so while another role holds a lock on `folders` or `sessions`, at which a search gives up after 5 s as a rule, as it did before, and after 10 s at the latest. So a role that shares very many folders or sessions, or holds such a lock, can keep every user from searching, without any of them being told: nothing limits how much a role may share, no limit of the app binds a role that sends its own statements (§4.5.3 of `docs/DESIGN.md`), and with 10 s it takes less data than with the minute a search's statements had before. A search that fails this way is sent again with the next text, one at a time, and says nothing (see the entry above). Against a server that has gone silent, three are given up in about three minutes, and searching is then refused until one of them ends, which is when the operating system gives its connection up, if it does, since the app has no keepalive on its database connections, or until the app is restarted. The window ends no search itself: it sends the next one when the backend has answered the one under way, and goes by the backend's limit for that. Left open when the pace of the search was limited (see **Fixed**); the entry on that pace was pre-existing and found by the pre-commit review and audit of the search field's menu.
- A paste into the sidebar's search field takes invisible characters and those that change the direction of text as they are, from the field's menu (see **Fixed** of 0.40.0) as from the keyboard: bidi embeddings, overrides and isolates, zero-width characters and the line and paragraph separators, which the import drops from a name, and the tag characters, which it leaves. The field then shows a query that reads as another, and the search finds nothing or something unexpected. They go no further than the field and the search's parameter, so this is confusing and not more; the menu's paste drops line breaks, the other control characters of C0, DEL and C1, and unpaired surrogates only, and turns each run of them into a space. The names and hosts the search finds are shown as the session tree shows them, those of other users' shared sessions and folders included (see the entry on names and hosts that another user wrote). The folders above a folder that was found are shown with such characters written out (see **Fixed**), by the list the import goes by. A name there can still look like another by letters of another script, as anywhere, can hold a character that looks like the `›` between the names without being it (`>`, `❯`, `»`), and can be made of invisible characters that are not on that list, such as the tag characters or a blank Braille pattern, so that a part of a path looks empty. The folder that was found is itself shown as the tree shows it: by its name and, for another user's, the icon of a shared folder, without saying whose it is. A path that does not fit beside the name is cut off at its end, which is where the nearest folder is, and the tooltip has it; how often that happens at the sidebar's usual width has not been looked at in the app. Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit review and audit of the search field's menu.
- In the search results the keys go by the selection, and the arrow keys by the whole tree and not by what is listed. Up and Down select the item before or after the selected one in the order of the tree, with the folders that are closed there left out, so the selection moves to something the results do not show, or to an item some rows away. Left and Right open and close a folder in the tree, also one that was found, which the results show open or closed by a state of their own (see **Fixed**). Enter, F2 and Delete act on the selected item, which stays selected when the query changes and it is no longer listed: Enter then connects to a session, or opens or closes a folder, that the list does not show, and Delete asks about it, a session by its name and a folder by the number of its sessions only. An item that is listed twice is selected in both places, and where the sidebar brings the selected item into view, as after the search is cleared or an item is deleted, it is the first of the two. A folder that is listed by its name inside another that was found does nothing on a double click there, and is announced like an item with nothing in it. Pre-existing for the sessions a search found; the part about folders came with them (see **Fixed**). Found while folders were added to the results, and by the pre-commit review and audit of that change.
- A chain of folders tens of thousands deep can keep the sidebar from showing anything. To tell which folders the tree shows, the sidebar goes up from each folder to the top by a function that calls itself once for each folder on the way, and a script engine gives up on one that calls itself too often: the sidebar would then fail as a whole, tree and search alike. Tried with that function by itself, outside the app: a chain of 20,000 fails where the list has the deepest folder before the ones above it, which the order by name allows, one of 5,000 does not, and a list with the top folder first does not at any depth. Nothing limits how deep folders are nested, except in the mRemoteNG import. In PostgreSQL mode another user can make such a chain of their own folders and share it, and every user who can see it would then be without a sidebar until the chain is gone. A walk that does not call itself would close it. Pre-existing, and not tried in the app; found by the pre-commit audit of the folders in the search results, whose own paths are held to eight folders (see **Fixed**).
- A search does not find another user's shared session whose folder the user cannot see. The tree lists such sessions under **Shared**; the results leave out every session whose folder the tree does not show, and a folder likewise, so in PostgreSQL mode a session that its owner shares out of a personal folder can be connected to from the tree and is never found. Pre-existing; found while folders were added to the results.
- A release build still compiles and signs whatever is in the runner's cargo home, and cargo does not check that against the lock file (see **Changed** of 0.40.0). Two things put crates there that the build did not download itself.
  - **The runner.** The runners run on their hosts and not in containers, so `~/.cargo` holds what every earlier job on the machine left: earlier releases, a run for a throwaway tag, and any other repository's jobs if the runner serves more than this one.
  - **The cache.** A build restores the cache for its own `Cargo.lock`, and an entry is the one the first run for that lock file saved, with what its build and the install scripts of its npm packages wrote into the cargo home. A later change of the npm packages does not change the key. How Forgejo keeps one repository's or one tag's cache from another's was not looked into.

  Someone who can push a tag, or run a job on the same runner, could thus leave a changed crate for the next release, which would be built in and signed with nothing in that release's log to show for it, also after the tag is gone. Whoever can push a tag can reach the signing key more directly, so for them this adds a way to outlast the tag and not a way in; for a job of another repository on a shared runner it would be a way in. A build that starts from an empty cargo home of its own, without the cache, and downloads its crates at every release would close both. That changes how cargo runs on all three platforms and needs a run on the runners to try, so it was left for a change of its own. Kept by decision when the fallback was removed; found by the pre-commit audit of that change.
- What the steps of a release job can reach beyond what the workflow file gives them has not been looked at on the runners. The runner Forgejo's is built on is said to hand each step the job's own token in its environment (`GITHUB_TOKEN`), together with the token for the cache and the artifacts; if the runners in use do, the install scripts of the npm packages and the build scripts of the crates can read both, whatever the checkout leaves in the git config (see **Changed** of 0.40.0), and with the second could change a cache or an artifact that a later job publishes. What the job's token may do is set on the forge, not in the workflow. A step that lists the names of its environment variables, run once with a throwaway tag, would tell. Not verified; raised by the pre-commit audit of the checkout credentials.
- `cargo audit` still reports advisories that no update can fix yet: `glib` 0.18 (unsound `VariantStrIter`) and `proc-macro-error` (unmaintained), both in Tauri's GTK3 stack on Linux, which only a Tauri release can replace; and `zvariant`, `zvariant_derive` and `zbus_names` (unmaintained since their merge into `zbus` 6), through `tauri-plugin-opener`, whose current release still uses `zbus` 5, and through `keyring`'s `secret-service`, which this app's features do not build. Left open after the dependency update under **Fixed** of 0.40.0. Three more that it reports are open as well: `chrono` 0.4.45 (unmaintained, RUSTSEC-2026-0543), which the app and sqlx use and which has no version to move to; `rustix` 1.1.4 (RUSTSEC-2026-0486, its xattr functions are unsound for a buffer of no length), which 1.1.6 fixes; and `async-executor` 1.14.0, under `zbus`, which was yanked. The last two take an update of crates that the patch releases under **Fixed** of 0.40.0 did not include, and were not looked into.
- `npm audit` still reports `picomatch` 4.0.7 (GHSA-fc5q-39xc-vjpf, a pattern with repeated extglobs can take very long to match), which has no fixed version yet. It is in development tooling and not in the shipped app (`npm audit --omit=dev` finds none): under `vite` and `vitest`, and under `typescript-eslint` as well, through `tinyglobby`, which was so before the update under **Fixed** too, where `npm audit` listed those packages for `minimatch`. That update took the findings from 13 to 12 and the advisories from two to one. The tools run on a developer's machine and on the release runners, over the project's own files and configuration.
- In PostgreSQL mode the app's database connection takes from the environment what the app has no setting for: `PGOPTIONS`, `PGAPPNAME` and the certificate files `PGSSLROOTCERT`, `PGSSLCERT` and `PGSSLKEY`. sqlx reads them as libpq does, and has no call that takes one back. None decides where the connection goes (`PGHOSTADDR` did; see **Fixed** of 0.40.0), but `PGOPTIONS` sets run-time parameters for the app's sessions: a search path, or a lock or statement timeout, which the app then keeps as one the server set, in place of its own 5 s and 60 s. Someone who has the variable set for `psql` has it for ShellStation too, without a word in the log. It is the user's own environment and reaches that user's sessions only. Taking the variables out of the app's environment at startup, as is done for `PGHOSTADDR`, would close it, and would end the one way there is to give the app a client certificate; which of the two is wanted is not decided. `docs/DESIGN.md` §4.3 says what is read.
- A settings import can still set the other settings either way, so a file from someone else can make the app awkward to use: terminal text in the background colour, toasts that close after a second, a UI language the user does not read, a local shell at every start, a refresh interval of 5 s, or the shortest connect timeout and no keepalive. Each shows itself and is undone in Settings, while restricting them would keep a user's own settings from moving to a new machine. Accepted; found by the pre-commit audit of the import direction fix.
- Some quits still leave the SSH and Telnet connections for the operating system to close, with no `SSH_MSG_DISCONNECT` (see **Fixed** of 0.40.0), and are not handled yet. These are quitting from the macOS Dock or at logout, and a system shutdown, which do not go through the app's own quit. A connection still being set up when the app quits, restarts from Settings or installs an update is left to the operating system as well, and so is a session still closing when the 4 s are up, or one that a page load, a new page's announcement or the closing for an update took in the instant before the quit began: those are closed in the background, and the quit, finding nothing to close, does not wait for them. Found by the pre-commit audit of the connection clean-up fix.
- On Windows a session log on a network share is still only as private as its directory there. What the app creates on a share gets its access list from the directory above, as before (see **Fixed** of 0.40.0), so whoever the share lets read that directory can read what a session showed, passwords echoed by a device included. The same goes for the app's own files when the user's profile is kept on a share. A list of the log's own would have to name the user by a SID the server knows, which it does in a domain but not when it is reached with another account, and the app cannot tell which it is. Naming the owner in the list instead (the OWNER RIGHTS SID) might serve both; it was held back because whatever is later created in a new `.ssh` would inherit that entry, and OpenSSH may refuse a key with an entry it does not know. Kept by decision when the access list was fixed.
- On Windows, the application log's file of the day on a network share is appended to whoever owns it. Elsewhere a file that is already at the day's name is appended to only if it belongs to the user or the administrators (see **Fixed** of 0.40.0); on a share the owner is not looked at, because a server reached with another account names the user's own files by an owner the app does not know, and every start would then begin a new file. Someone who can create files in a log directory on a share, but cannot read the files of others there, can therefore put a file of their own at the next day's name and read what the app appends to it. A link there and a file with a second name are still refused. A new file on a share gets its access list from the directory above, as before, so in most shares whoever can write to the directory can read the log in any case. Comparing the owner with that of a file the app has just created there would tell the user's files from others' on any server. Found by the pre-commit audit of the application log's files, and left open with the share exception for session logs (above).
- A dialog does not take the focus when it opens. What is behind it keeps it and gets nothing typed (see **Fixed** of 0.40.0), but still shows as focused, and a field in the dialog takes a Tab or a click first, unless the dialog puts the focus into one of its fields itself. What an input method composes at a terminal behind a dialog is kept from the terminal's remote host, but is not taken out of the terminal's own input field, and none of it has been tried with an input method. Narrowed by the fix under **Fixed** of 0.40.0.
- Nothing limits the log directory as a whole. The limits on a session log (see **Fixed** of 0.40.0) are for each log by itself: up to 300 sessions can be logged at a time, each with up to 100 MiB by default and up to 12.5 GiB with both limits at their largest, or twice that with files that cannot be deleted, a reconnect begins a new log, and the logs of sessions that have ended are never deleted by the app. Several hosts, or one that the user keeps reconnecting to, can therefore still fill the volume the log directory is on, as a user's own logs can over time. Writes then fail, for the logs and for whatever else writes there; a log is given up at its first write that fails, and its session's tab says so (see **Fixed** of 0.40.0). A floor on the free space of the volume, below which a log is given up, would close this. Left out by decision when the limits were added.
- In PostgreSQL mode, a per-user mapping for a shared session that was saved before mappings had an owner (see **Fixed** of 0.40.0) is claimed by its User Identity alone. Before that fix any user could rewrite any mapping, so one that was tampered with then is carried over as it is, and its user's next connection to that session sends the profile it names. Until its user's ShellStation has claimed it, any other user who can see the session can still read it, which gives away the ID of one of that user's profiles, and can claim or delete it first, so that the user's connection falls back to the session's own profile. A ShellStation older than 0.40.0 does not look at the owner, so it also still connects with a placeholder. A placeholder nobody claims, such as one of a user who has left, stays until an admin deletes it. An admin can rule all of this out by deleting the placeholders after the owning role's first start of 0.40.0 and before the users' (`docs/ADMIN_GUIDE.md` §5); the users then assign their profiles again. Kept by decision, so that an update does not drop every user's assignments; found by the pre-commit audit of the per-user mapping fix.
- In PostgreSQL mode, the check of the row-level security setup at each start (see **Fixed** of 0.40.0) tells and does nothing else. The app goes on working against a database whose setup is missing or is another version's, and the notice can be dismissed, until the next start or the next time the window's page is loaded. What is missing is still not in effect until a start of the owning role gets through, and a user who holds a lock on a table while the owning role starts a new release can still make the setup leave that table (`docs/ADMIN_GUIDE.md` §7); every user is now told. The check is a start's answer: a setup that is taken away or completed while the app runs shows at the next start. It goes by names where the setup does, so it does not read what a policy of the setup's name says, and it looks for no policy or trigger that someone has added beside the setup's; the owning role can add either, and a user of Option B a trigger. A version older than the one the owning role last started is told that the trigger functions are not its own, with the same notice as for a setup that is behind. The app only warns, by decision when the check was added.
- In PostgreSQL mode, what a user assigns to another user's shared session is used only for what the user confirmed the session names (see **Fixed** of 0.40.0). What that does not cover:
  - **What answers under a name.** What is confirmed is a host as written, not an address or a machine. The name is looked up by the last jump host, or by the user's machine for a direct connection, and whoever controls that lookup, or the machine behind the name, gets what the connection sends. For SSH the host key check stands against that, with a first connection still accepted by Enter; for Telnet nothing does. Neither is different for a session of the user's own.
  - **A login sequence** types what its steps hold, each when the host prints what the step waits for. The confirmed host therefore decides which steps it is sent, a password written into a step included, as a Telnet sequence has it, there being no profile to take it from.
  - **What is assigned.** What is confirmed is the session, not which profile or sequence is used for it. In the app's window only **My Login** assigns to another user's session, and it confirms first; the folder commands no longer reach such a session (see **Fixed** of 0.40.0). But the command that saves a mapping for one session does not ask whether the session was confirmed. A mapping that gets to a confirmed session in any other way is therefore used without asking again: by a statement of the user's own, by anything else in the app's window that could call that command, or by the claim of a mapping from before mappings had an owner (see the entry on those below).
  - **Private addresses.** A session can name a host on the user's own network unless **Restrict private IPs** is on, which it is not by default. The user sees the host and confirms it.
  - **Confirming.** A save confirms what the dialog shows, of the session and of every jump host listed, whether the user has read it or not.
  - **The file.** Whoever can write `session-logins.json` can confirm for the user; it is as private as the config directory. It is kept by session alone, not by database or role, so two roles used from one account share what was confirmed. What was confirmed of a jump host counts for every confirmed session that goes through it, and confirming it again in the dialog of one of them confirms it for all; what was confirmed of it as a session connected to keeps counting for it as a jump host beside that. A machine or account without it asks for each session once more. A file that is not what this version writes, a later version's among them, counts as nothing confirmed and is written over by the next save. Two instances of the app each write the whole file, so one can drop what the other has just confirmed, which is then asked for again. And an entry is never removed: one for a session that is gone stays, at some 200 bytes, and one for a session whose assignment was cleared counts again when something is assigned to it anew.
  - **Option B.** All of this goes by a row's owner. A user who can write another owner into a row of their own, as an Option B user can, gets past it as past the other per-user rules.

  Found by the pre-commit audit of **My Login**, which found what the pin now closes as well.
- **My Login** shows what another user wrote within these limits. The owner's role name is the first word of the dialog's sentence, cut at 64 characters: where an administrator has created a role with a name that reads as text, or under Option B, where the owner column takes anything, it can read as part of the sentence. In a name, only the characters of the import's list are written out, so a name can still look blank or like another one with characters outside that list, and one that holds the text `\u{202E}` as written looks like one with that character written out. Marks stacked on a letter of a name are cut off at the edge of the text they are in, and can still cover that text. A host in ASCII can still look like another, an `rn` like an `m`. A session that cannot be confirmed shows in the dialog like any other, and only the save says so. What has changed since it was confirmed is told by colour and by being crossed out, which a screen reader does not say, and the dialog's fields for protocol, host, port and user name are announced as live regions, which they are not. And the session tree still takes Enter and F2 behind the dialogs that apply a profile or sequence to a folder, bulk-edit a folder or move an item, as it did before; behind **My Login** it does not. Found by the pre-commit review and audit of **My Login** and left as they are.
- In PostgreSQL mode, a credential profile or login sequence cannot be assigned to all sessions of another user's shared folder at once. **Apply Credential Profile…** and **Apply Login Sequence…** are for a folder's owner; for another user's sessions there is **My Login** (see **Fixed** of 0.40.0), one session at a time. The statements behind the two commands take no such folder, and no session of another user's (see **Fixed** of 0.40.0). Taking one would mean covering whatever is in the folder and below it at the moment they run, which its owner decides, without showing a session or a host; replacing what the user has assigned to single sessions there; and leaving out jump hosts that are not in the folder. Offering this takes a dialog that lists the sessions and hosts it covers and assigns to exactly those. Held back by the pre-commit audit of **My Login**.
- In PostgreSQL mode, **Sort Alphabetically** on a folder goes into every folder below it that the user can see, other users' shared ones included, and runs two transactions for each, though it orders only the user's own items. The menu offers it for a folder of the user's own, below which another user's folders can be from before a folder took only its owner's items, or under Option B; the command behind it also takes the top level, with everything that anyone shares below it. A large tree of another user's there makes the sort take as long as going through it takes, and it cannot be cancelled. Its statements lock the rows they order with `FOR UPDATE`, which waits for a lock that a foreign key holds on one of them (DESIGN.md §4.5.3) and gives up after 5 seconds, and each folder is sorted in transactions of its own, so a sort that fails part of the way leaves the folders before it sorted. Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit audit of the fix that has the sort go into a folder once (see **Fixed** of 0.40.0).
- Names and hosts that another user wrote are shown as they are in most of the app: in the session tree, in the messages of a failed connect, which quote session names, and in the host key prompt. **My Login** writes out the characters that change how text displays (see **Fixed** of 0.40.0), and the notice of a session without a log drops them. In PostgreSQL mode the owner of a shared session can therefore still give it a name or a host with a bidi override or invisible characters in it, which reads as something else: the commands that save a session check the length of a name and a host, and only the import drops such characters. Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit audit of **My Login**.
- In PostgreSQL mode, the session dialog does not say whether it could save the user's choice of credential profile and login sequence: a failure, such as a user identity that is not set, is dropped, and the dialog closes as after a save. It also shows what is assigned only once that has been read, and replaces a choice made before then. **My Login** has neither (see **Fixed** of 0.40.0). Pre-existing; found while writing **My Login**.
- In PostgreSQL mode, `_sqlx_migrations`, the table by which a start decides whether it has migrations to apply, is left to its owner from the owning role's first start of the version that does so (see **Fixed** of 0.40.0), and what remains is this. What was written to it before is not undone and cannot be told from it. A row for a migration that no build carries yet is only logged as a warning, at each start and until that release comes, after which it counts as applied; a version that is behind the database logs the same, so the warning says something only in the log of the newest version's start. `docs/ADMIN_GUIDE.md` §2.3 has a query for the rows. Until that first start has got through, a user who can still write to the table can lock all of it, and the owning role's start then stops before its setup, until an admin ends that session. A table of that name that belongs to another role than the tables' owner is reported at each start and stays that role's to write, until a superuser gives it back. And the app names the table without its schema, as it does its other tables, so what it reads is the one the connection's search path finds first. Found by the pre-commit review and audit of the fix.
- In PostgreSQL mode, a role that may create objects in the schema `public` can put operators and functions there that other users' statements run in place of the built-in ones, as those users. Every role may before PostgreSQL 15, and in a database upgraded from then, unless that is revoked. `docs/ADMIN_GUIDE.md` §1 now does so for a new database and §5 says how for an existing one; the users of the withdrawn permission model Option B were granted it, and keep it until it is taken back (see **Fixed** of 0.40.0). PostgreSQL finds `pg_catalog` first but prefers an exact match of the argument types elsewhere on the search path to a built-in that needs a cast. The row-level security setup, which runs as the owning role, makes no comparison that such an operator could take (see **Fixed** of 0.40.0). The app's other statements, which run as each user, have not been gone through for this one by one; in those that were looked at, `pg_catalog` has an operator for exactly the two types compared, text with text and text with the name `current_user`. Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit audit of the trigger function fix.
- In PostgreSQL mode, highlight profiles are each user's own from 0.40.0 on (see **Fixed** of 0.40.0), and what remains is this.
  - **What is taken over.** The profiles the shared table holds at a user's first start of 0.40.0 or a later version are copied as they are, whoever wrote them, within the limits (see **Fixed** of 0.40.0), with the profile each of the user's own sessions names. Before, any user could rewrite any profile, so one that was tampered with is carried over, a pattern that the backtracking check does not catch included, and so is its name as written; from then on it is the user's own, to edit or delete in Settings. Nothing in the window says that a take-over happened or what it brought: the application log has the counts, as a warning where something did not come over. A profile past a limit is not read, and not at a later start either. So a user is without a profile that had more than 500 rules, as a SecureCRT highlight import could store one, until they import that file again; and without one that someone has made longer than 1 MiB, or that the limits were filled before, with profiles that the user's own sessions name none of. Before, the same role could have deleted it. A session whose owner is still the placeholder `'local'` counts as no one's own, so no choice comes over for it. And a shared table that someone has filled with so many rows that going through it takes more than 10 seconds is not taken over at all, and holds every start up for those 10 seconds, until an admin has emptied it of them. As long as the table holds anything, the same goes for every later first start: of a new user, of a user on another machine or account, or of one whose local database was lost. An admin can rule both out by looking the table over before the users update and emptying it once they all have (`docs/ADMIN_GUIDE.md` §5). A lock that someone holds on the table keeps a start from taking it over, for 5 seconds at each start until it gets through.
  - **Older versions.** A version from before 0.40.0 still reads and writes the shared table and shows each session with the profile its row names, so its users are as exposed as before. It does not see what a user of 0.40.0 or a later version chooses, and that version does not see what it writes after the take-over.
  - **Other users' shared sessions** are shown without highlighting after the update, until the user chooses a profile for each.
  - **One database.** That the take-over has happened is noted in the local database, whichever shared database it was. A shared database that the app is pointed at later is not taken over.
  - **One machine.** Profiles and choices are in the local database of one machine and account. A user's second machine starts with what the shared table held at its own first start. A profile moves as a file, with **Data Export / Import**; the choice of a profile for a session does not move with it, since an import brings sessions in without one.
  - **One at a time.** For another user's shared sessions a profile is chosen session by session; Bulk Edit covers the user's own.
  - **Left-over rows.** A choice for a session that someone else deleted, or that a shared database's own cascade removed, stays in the local database as a row that nothing reads, at some 80 bytes.
