So does one for a session made while the database could not be reached, which is gone with that start.
  - **The check itself.** The check that refuses patterns prone to catastrophic backtracking still goes by a pattern's shape and does not catch every one. What it now guards against is a pattern of the user's own, or one in a file the user imports: a SecureCRT highlight file or a ShellStation export from someone else can carry such a pattern, and a session shown with it holds up the window. Pre-existing.

  Kept by decision, so that an update does not drop every user's profiles; the rest is what per-user means.
- In PostgreSQL mode, the central database has the tables `credentials`, `credential_profiles` and `login_sequences` without row-level security; the app keeps these in each user's local database and never reads the central ones. `highlight_profiles` is another from 0.40.0 on, read once at a user's first start (see the entry above). Pre-existing; found while fixing the per-user mappings.
- In PostgreSQL mode, a user can still hold database locks that other users' changes need (see **Fixed** of 0.40.0 for what changed). PostgreSQL lets anyone who may change a table lock all of it, which the app's users need to; a session also sets its own time limits, so none that the app or the server sets binds a user who sends their own statements. With a whole table locked, nothing in it can be read or changed, by anyone and whoever owns it: the session tree does not load, changes fail after 5 seconds, and a start goes on without the row-level security setup; its window says what is then missing from the database (see **Fixed** of 0.40.0). A row a user can lock only where it is their own (see **Fixed** of 0.40.0), which holds up what refers to it, such as another user's mapping for a shared session of theirs. Through a foreign key, a row of a user's own that refers to another user's session keeps that session from being deleted, though not from being changed, while the transaction that wrote it is open. Such a row is a mapping for it, or a row in the central `credentials` table, which the app does not use and which has no row-level security. A session that names the other as its jump host is such a row only if it named it before that was refused (see **Fixed** of 0.40.0), or where the trigger that refuses it is not in place or does not hold the user (Option B). The shared table of highlight profiles is no longer used (see **Fixed** of 0.40.0); a lock on it only keeps a first start from taking the profiles over. An admin finds and ends the session that holds it (`docs/ADMIN_GUIDE.md` §7). Not something the app can prevent; found by the pre-commit audit of the shared-row fix.
- In PostgreSQL mode, a session that named another user's session as its jump host before that was refused (see **Fixed** of 0.40.0) keeps it until its owner picks one of their own. Such a session is not connected to, but nothing keeps the jump host from being deleted: by its owner, alone or along with its folder, or by an admin who removes that user's sessions. The foreign key then takes it from the session that named it, which connects directly from then on, and nobody is told. The app does not point these sessions out to their owners: the session dialog shows the jump host that is set, the refusal at connecting names it, and `docs/ADMIN_GUIDE.md` §5 has a query for a superuser that lists them, with what to tell their owners. The fix has no step for it: refusing the deletion would let one user's session keep another user from deleting their own, and whether to mark such sessions instead is undecided. Left over from the jump host fix; found by its pre-commit audit.
- In PostgreSQL mode, the primary key of `folders` and of `sessions` is checked without row-level security, as every unique constraint is. A role that adds a row under an ID that is taken is refused by it, also where the row of that ID is one the role cannot see, so the refusal tells whether a folder or session with a given ID exists. The foreign key of the central `credentials` table, which the app does not use and which has no row-level security, gives the same answer for a session to a role that adds a row there. IDs are random, so this confirms an ID someone already has and finds none. The jump host fix closed the same answer where the foreign key of a jump host gave it. Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit audit of the jump host fix.
- In PostgreSQL mode, a change that fails because of a held lock shows PostgreSQL's own message, such as `Failed to rename folder: … canceling statement due to lock timeout`, in English and without saying that it can be tried again. Left as it is for now; found while fixing the lock waits.
- A first connection's host key is still accepted with Enter, as in OpenSSH's yes/no prompt; only a changed, other-type, address-changed or unchecked key takes a click (see **Fixed** of 0.40.0). A man in the middle on a first connection presents exactly such a new key, so an Enter pressed while focus is outside a terminal would trust it. Enter typed into a terminal never reaches the prompt. Kept by decision; found by the pre-commit audit of the host key prompt.
- On Windows, any user of the machine who can read the system-wide `known_hosts` can still hide its revocations from a user whose ShellStation has nothing stored from it (see **Fixed** of 0.40.0). That is the case when:
  - the user's ShellStation has never read the file whole, such as for a new user, or
  - nothing could be stored: there is no local data directory, or writing failed, both of which are logged.

  To hide them, a local user holds the file open without sharing it for reading, from before the victim's first connection on. They would also have to do that while a network attacker presents the revoked key, which the victim had already trusted in their own `known_hosts`. For a user whose ShellStation has read the file whole and stored what it read, a hold keeps the revocations last read, and a change since leaves the key unchecked.

  "Unchecked" is a warning, not a refusal. While the file is held after an administrator has changed it, every connection that would not already be refused or warned about warns, first connections and keys the user's own `known_hosts` trusts alike, and accepting takes a click. A user who clicks through accepts a key that the new revocation might name. The hold can keep these warnings up until it ends, for every user of the machine, but only after an administrator's edit, which the hold also keeps every ShellStation from reading. The alternatives would be trusting the key, which would hide the revocation, or refusing every connection, which would let the hold shut users out.

  Three smaller limits remain:
  - **Revocations removed since.** A revocation that an administrator has taken out still refuses its key while the file is held, until it can be read again.
  - **Edits too close together.** Two edits within the same tick of the clock, by default about 16 ms on Windows (not measured here), leave the same last-written time. If ShellStation read the file between them, and the second keeps its size, the second goes unnoticed.
  - **Entries other than revocations.** While the file is held, a key on record in it neither trusts the key nor makes the prompt a warning, as before.

  Rejected alternatives:
  - **Leaving the key unchecked whenever the file is held and nothing is stored.** This would let any user put a warning on the connections of every user whose ShellStation has not read the file.
  - **Keeping the file open while the app runs.** This would stop administrators' `Set-Content` and `move /y` on the file, and would not help a user whose ShellStation has not read it yet.

  Found by the pre-commit audit of the system-wide `known_hosts` change, and narrowed by the fix under **Fixed** of 0.40.0.

## 0.40.0 - 2026-11-18

### Added

- **Settings → Data Export / Import** (previously "Database — Export / Import") now also covers the client settings and the command bar buttons, which live in the webview's localStorage and were lost when a database was moved to another machine. **Export Data** opens a dialog with one checkbox each for **Database**, **Settings** and **Command buttons**, and writes the chosen ones into a single JSON file. **Import Data** reads the file first and offers only the sections it actually holds, so a full move to a new machine is a single import. Database import keeps its existing behaviour (rows that already exist are skipped). Settings overwrite the current values, each going through the same setter as the Settings dialog, so an invalid or unknown value is ignored rather than stored; an empty language ("follow the system") leaves the importing machine's language alone. Five safety settings — blocking private addresses, confirming multi-line pastes, confirming before quitting with open sessions and before closing a tab, and checking for updates — can be switched on by an import but never off, and three settings that make something happen with less care can be switched off but never on: paste on right-click, which types into a live session; copy on selection, which puts what the remote host printed on the clipboard for the next paste; and closing a tab when its session ends, which throws away what it showed. So a file from someone else cannot quietly weaken them; changing one the other way stays a deliberate change in Settings, even when the file is this machine's own export. Command buttons are added after the existing ones and never replace them: a button with the same id (in any letter case), or the same name and command, is skipped. Since a button types its command into a live session on one click, the import dialog lists every button the file would add with its command as written (runs of spaces collapsed, so padding cannot push the rest of a command out of view), says what a click does, and imports only on a click of **Import** — not on Enter, so a stray keystroke cannot skip the list. A button whose name or command holds control or format characters (zero-width, text-direction, tag characters), line separators, blank-looking characters or unusual spaces is refused, so it cannot look different from what it sends. Names keep emoji sequences and icon-font (private use) glyphs; commands, which never need them, also refuse private-use characters and variation selectors. Settings that belong to one machine (database connection, session and application logging) are not exported. Sections are imported independently, so a failed database import is reported without stopping the settings and buttons. The new strings, and the section's updated help text, are translated into all 14 UI languages.
- The new file is a versioned envelope (`format: "shellstation-export"`, `version: 1`, `app_version`, `exported_at`, and optional `database`, `settings` and `command_buttons` sections) written by the new `data_export_file` command, which replaces `db_export_file`. The command shows the save dialog itself instead of taking a path from the webview, so the file it writes is always one the user picked; only one export runs at a time, and the dialog title the webview supplies is capped. It writes through a temporary file that is then renamed into place, so a failed export never leaves a half-written file and a symlink at the target is replaced rather than followed, and it does the write on a blocking thread rather than an async worker. The backend checks the frontend-supplied sections before writing: settings must be a flat map of scalars (at most 64 entries, strings up to 256 characters) and buttons must match the command bar's own limits, with no id used twice. The import side applies the same entry limits before anything is imported. Database-only files written by earlier versions still import as before. A file from a newer format version is refused with a message to update instead of being half-read.
- The connection timeout, keepalive interval and keepalive count setters now accept only whole numbers within range (1–300 s, 0–3600 s and 1–100). The backend reads them as integers, so a fraction or an oversized value would otherwise be refused there; the Settings dialog only ever offered valid values. The backend now keeps these settings and checks the same ranges (see **Fixed**); it used to cap the timeout at 300 s silently, so a value above that, which only an imported file could set, is now ignored instead.
- **Settings → Import SecureCRT** now also brings over the buttons of SecureCRT's button bars and Command Manager, which users who move from SecureCRT had to build again by hand. The file is read first, by the new `inspect_securecrt` command, and a dialog like the one behind **Import Data** offers **Sessions**, **Button bars** and **Command Manager**. A part is offered only when the file has something in it that can be imported, and every button is listed with its command before anything is imported. Sessions are imported as before.
  - **Where the buttons go.** They are added to the command bar after the existing ones. A button already there with the same name and command is skipped, so one that is both on a bar and in the Command Manager (VanDyke's own way to move bars into the Command Manager copies them) is added once. All bars go into the one command bar. Buttons get the default color and pass the same hidden-character check as buttons from a ShellStation export file.
  - **What carries over.** Only Send String buttons, since a command bar button only sends text. A command carries over as SecureCRT wrote it: its send-string escapes (`\r`, `\n`, `\t`, `\b`, `\e`, `\p`, `\v`, `\\`) are the command bar's own, and so, from this version, are its three-digit octal codes for control characters (see **Changed**), so a Ctrl+C button (`\003`) keeps working. A printable character written as a code (`\101`) is written out as that character, so the import dialog shows the text a button types rather than digits that type it. Every converted command is read back the way the command bar reads it and compared with what SecureCRT sends; a button whose two differ is skipped. A comma in a command or label is written `\,` in the export and read as a comma. In a label, `&&` becomes `&` and a single `&`, which marks a Windows access key, is dropped. A label longer than 32 characters is shortened.
  - **What is skipped, with a warning after the import:** a button with another function (a menu function, a script, …) or with no label, one that sends nothing, one with an octal code above `\177`, which SecureCRT sends as a single raw byte and a command bar button, sending text, cannot, one whose codes, written out, would run into the characters around them (in `\0\061\062`, `\0` is no code, but `\012` would be a line feed), and one whose command is longer than 1024 characters, which is not shortened, since that would change what it sends. At most 1000 entries are taken from the button bars, and as many from the Command Manager; unassigned entries do not count. An entry over 64 KiB, far more than any importable button needs, is skipped before it is taken apart. A warning quotes at most 64 characters of a bar name, label or function, so a crafted file cannot multiply a long name into every warning on its bar.
  - The section picker of the data export and import dialogs is now shared with this dialog. A section can carry a second sentence as a line of its own, which the checkbox's description includes, and the button list shows a name cut short by the layout in full on hover.
- Tests: 25 Rust unit tests in `import/securecrt.rs` for the SecureCRT buttons and the parser fixes below (the export layout of SecureCRT 9.7 and 8.x, files without some of the blocks, Command Manager folders and their nesting limit, blocks nested or repeated, cut-off files, a `<string>` inside a `<string>`, the entry limit and unassigned entries, oversized entries, warnings quoting long bar, key and session names, field and label escaping, octal codes, the name and command limits, and each reason to skip a button), and `src/secureCrtImport.test.ts` and `src/components/SecureCrtImportDialog.test.tsx` (15 tests: ids, color and the hidden-character check, importing only the picked parts without duplicates or changes to existing buttons, and the dialog's sections, empty blocks, button lists and keyboard handling). Two tests in `src/components/CommandBar.test.tsx` cover the octal codes the command bar reads and those it sends as written, on the same edge cases as the import's tests, so the two scanners cannot drift apart unnoticed.
- Tests: 18 Rust unit tests in `commands/data_transfer.rs` (settings and button validation including duplicate ids, the empty-selection check, section omission, the database section reading back as `db_import` input, the temp-file write, the dialog title check and the one-export-at-a-time guard), and `src/dataTransfer.test.ts`, `src/stores/settingsStore.test.ts` and `src/components/DataTransferDialog.test.tsx` (87 tests: file parsing including legacy, malformed and oversized files, settings and button import rules including the direction each guarded setting may move in, letter case in ids and hidden characters, store reloads after a database import, per-section failure isolation, the button list, and the dialog's keyboard handling). `getExportableSettings` is tested against the store's actual persisted keys, and `SETTING_IMPORTERS` is typed to cover all of them, so a new setting cannot silently be left out of exports.

### Fixed

- SSH and Telnet connections could stay open after the app had stopped showing them, so a remote device went on treating the session as in use: one that allows a single session, or a single configuration session, could then refuse a new one, or changes made from it. russh does not close a connection when its handle is dropped while a channel is still held, and SSH keepalives kept such a connection, and every jump host under it, alive indefinitely. There were five ways to get there:
  - **A session that ended on its own** (the device closed the shell, `exit`, an idle timeout) left its SSH connection and its jump host connections open until the tab was closed. The reader task now disconnects the target and then each jump host as soon as the session ends. Telnet was unaffected, because its I/O task owns the socket.
  - **Reconnecting a disconnected tab** (Enter) never closed the old session, which stayed open until the app quit and counted toward the 100-session cap. A reconnect now closes the old session first and waits until it is closed, so the device has seen it end before the new connection arrives. If the tab is closed while that happens, the reconnect stops there.
  - **Reloading the window** (F5, Ctrl+R) threw away every tab but left all their sessions connected, out of sight and with no way to close them until the app quit. A page load in the main window now closes every session from before it, local shells included. A connect still in flight during the reload is refused when it tries to register, and closed, and one waiting on a host-key or keyboard-interactive prompt that no longer has a dialog ends at once rather than at the prompt's timeout.
  - **A connection refused at the final registration**, because a concurrent connect took the 100th slot, was dropped without stopping its reader task, which kept the channel or socket and with it the whole connection open. It also went on reading output and wrote it to a session log. `SshSession` and `TelnetSession` now stop their reader task when dropped, and a dropped SSH session also disconnects its chain in the background.
  - **After a reconnect, the tab kept the protocol it was opened with.** If the saved session had been changed from SSH to Telnet (or back) in between, the tab sent input to, and closed, the new session with the other protocol's commands, which fail silently, so closing the tab never closed the new connection. The tab now takes the protocol of the session it reconnected to.

  Closing a tab now sends `SSH_MSG_DISCONNECT` to the target and then to each jump host, and waits until they have closed, up to 3 s for the whole chain. A Telnet tab waits for its socket to close, and a local shell tab for the shell to exit. No close holds the session manager's lock while it waits, so a slow server or a stuck shell cannot hold up input to the other tabs. A local shell whose kill fails is no longer waited for, since the wait could block for good, and closing a local shell tab no longer blocks the main thread while the shell exits. An SSH connection that fails after the TCP connection is up (authentication, opening the shell) is now disconnected explicitly rather than left to close once russh notices the dropped handles. So is a jump chain that fails part way (a jump host refusing the login, a tunnel that cannot be opened), down to every hop already connected. Every connection of a chain is held by a guard from the moment it connects until the session is open, so this also holds for a connect that is abandoned part way, and an SSH reader that stops unexpectedly still closes its chain. Covered by tests in `ssh/mod.rs` against an in-process russh server. One shows that a dropped handle leaves the connection open while a task still holds its channel, and that stopping the task closes it. Others check that `SshConnection::close` returns only after the target and the jump host are both closed, that a second close returns at once, and that a jump chain dropped part way disconnects its hops while a finished one does not. Tests in `telnet/mod.rs` check that a refused, removed, dropped or closed session stops its I/O task. `TerminalTabs.test.tsx` covers the reconnect order, a tab closed before or during the reconnect, a failed teardown of the old session, and the protocol carried over. The end of a session on its own and the page-load cleanup need a running app and are not covered by automated tests. Pre-existing.
- **Quitting the app left its SSH and Telnet connections for the operating system to close,** so a device saw the TCP connection end but received no `SSH_MSG_DISCONNECT`. Quitting now closes every session as closing its tab does: each SSH connection, its jump hosts included, is sent `SSH_MSG_DISCONNECT` and waited for, each Telnet socket is closed, each local shell killed and each session log closed and written. The sessions close side by side, each SSH chain giving up after 3 s, and the app exits after 4 s whatever is still closing. While SSH or Telnet sessions close, a **Closing sessions…** notice with a progress bar covers the window, in all 14 languages. It has no buttons, since the quit cannot be called off; it takes the focus from the terminal and keeps every key from the app until it exits.
  - **Every way of quitting closes the sessions first.** Ctrl+Q and the window's close button go through the new `app_quit` command, after the confirmation where the settings ask for one; the window's close button without a confirmation used to close the window, and with it the app, directly. The quit returns at once and closes the sessions on a task of its own, so a second request does nothing, a panic there still ends in the exit, and a connect that finishes while the app quits is refused, as one finishing during a reload is. Should `app_quit` fail, the frontend closes the window as before, and should that fail too, the next quit tries again. Should the app still be running 10 s after the quit began, the frontend closes the window itself.
  - **The menu's quit item** (**File → Exit** on Windows, **Quit ShellStation** with Cmd+Q on macOS; Linux has no such menu) was Tauri's predefined one, which ends the process on the spot with no event the app could hold it on. It is now the app's own, with the same label and shortcut, and quits from the backend directly, so it still works when the webview is hung. Like the predefined item, it does not ask to confirm, since the confirmation lives in the webview; on macOS this includes Cmd+Q, which the menu takes before the webview sees it. The Settings hint that the confirmation applies to Cmd+Q was already wrong before this change and still is.
  - The quit logic moved from `App.tsx` to `src/hooks/useAppQuit.ts`, covered by `useAppQuit.test.ts` (the close button and Ctrl+Q / Cmd+Q, the confirmation and its setting, ended tabs, quitting once, a quit the backend began, the notice, a failed `app_quit`, a failed fallback and the 10 s fallback) and `QuittingOverlay.test.tsx` (focus, keys, no buttons). Two tests in `commands/mod.rs` cover the refused connect and that the quit begins only once. The closing itself is the tab close covered above. The backend's quit task and the menu item need a running app and are not covered by automated tests. The page-load cleanup and the quit now share the code that takes the sessions out of their managers and kills the local shells. The quit confirmation's session count now follows tabs that end while it is open. Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit audit of the connection clean-up fix.
- **Installing an update left the SSH and Telnet connections for the operating system to close,** as quitting did before the fix above: a device saw the TCP connection end but received no `SSH_MSG_DISCONNECT`, and one that allows a single session could go on counting it. The update dialog called the updater plugin's `downloadAndInstall`, which on Windows starts the installer and exits the process itself, and elsewhere was followed by `relaunch()`; neither is a quit the app can hold. The dialog now downloads and installs in two steps and has the backend close every session in between, with the new `update_install_begin` command. The sessions are closed as a quit closes them: each SSH connection, its jump hosts included, is sent `SSH_MSG_DISCONNECT` and waited for, each Telnet socket is closed, each local shell killed and each session log closed and written, side by side and within 4 s.
  - **Only once the update is downloaded.** The download's signature is checked by then, so a download that fails costs no session, as before.
  - **No session is opened from then on,** so that a connect still on its way does not outlast the close; it is refused with "An update is being installed". Should the install or the restart fail after that, the dialog tells the backend with the new `update_install_failed` command and the app connects again. The sessions that were closed stay closed: once they have closed, the backend tells each tab, so the tabs show them as ended and offer the reconnect, and the dialog says that the update failed, once the backend has been told. After a failed install, the next try closes what was opened since.
  - **A closing that fails does not stop the install,** since a version that cannot close its sessions must still be able to replace itself; the reason goes to the console, and to the application log where the backend refused, and the connections are left to the operating system as before. The same goes for sessions still closing after the 4 s, which go on closing while the update installs, and for a local shell behind a lock that does not come free within 2 s, as at a change of page.
  - **Permissions.** The main window is now granted `updater:allow-download` and `updater:allow-install` in place of `updater:allow-download-and-install`, which leaves no moment between the two. The design document used to rule the two separate ones out as having no use; it now says what they are for, and what they add for a script in the window: downloads that are kept in memory without being installed. The plugin checks the signature in both calls.
  - **The dialog stays from the end of the download on.** Escape used to close it while the update was being installed, which left the window usable until the restart. **Help → Check for Updates…** is ignored from the click on **Install and restart** until the install has failed; it used to take the dialog away and then offer the update for a second install beside the first, as a second click on the button could. The download itself is given back when the install fails; the plugin keeps it otherwise. Nothing limits how long the install may take, since it can wait for the user; should it never return, the dialog stays until the app is quit, which still works.
  - **What is left.** A window reloaded while an update installs can connect again, since the refusal goes with the page that asked for it and the page that comes would never take it back; those sessions end with the process. No shortcut reloads the window under a dialog. A quit that begins while the sessions are closing for an update does not wait for them (see **Known issues**).
  - Covered by six tests in `commands/mod.rs` (a connect refused while an update installs and allowed again after a failure, the next page not held by the page before, the closing being for the announced page and once, the log of a connect under way, a quit that has begun, and a held shell lock), five in `src/page.test.ts`, and `src/components/UpdateDialog.test.tsx` (16 tests: the order of download, close, install and restart, no install before the close has returned, no close for a failed download, an install despite a failed close, the app connecting again after a failed install or restart, also when the close had failed and before the failure is shown, only the generic error shown, one install for two clicks, a check from the menu during an install and after a failed one, and Escape in each phase). The quit and the update now share the code that closes the SSH and Telnet sessions and waits for them. The Rust tests run with no session in the managers, so the closing of a real session for an update, and the notice to its tab, are covered only as far as the quit's shared code is. The command itself, the plugin's two calls and an install on any platform need a running app with an update to offer, and were not tried. The check from the menu, the second click, the late notice to the tabs and the wording of several sentences here were found by the pre-commit review and audit of this change.
- **The restart that Settings offers after a change of database left the SSH and Telnet connections for the operating system to close,** as quitting and installing an update did before the two fixes above. **Save & Restart** called `app_restart`, which restarted the process on the spot with every session in it. It is now a quit that ends in a new start: the backend closes every session first, as for a quit (each SSH connection and its jump hosts sent `SSH_MSG_DISCONNECT` and waited for, each Telnet socket closed, each local shell killed, each session log written), and asks for the restart within 4 s. While SSH or Telnet sessions close, the window shows the same **Closing sessions…** notice as for a quit, whose text says that ShellStation closes; it then starts again. From the click on, every connect is refused, and a second click does nothing.
  - **A quit is kept to, where it reaches the backend.** A quit that arrives while the sessions close for a restart makes the app exit and not start again; a restart asked for while the app quits changes nothing. The menu's quit item arrives throughout. Ctrl+Q and the window's close button arrive only until the **Closing sessions…** notice shows, which takes every key and holds every further quit, as it does during a quit; with only local shells open there is no notice, the shells are killed at once and the restart follows at once, and the two still arrive in the moment before it. Once the sessions are closed the restart is settled, and a quit in that last instant changes nothing.
  - The restart is asked of Tauri with `request_restart`, not `restart`, which off the main thread never returns and would keep the thread it is called on. The frontend is unchanged.
  - Covered by three new tests in `commands/mod.rs` (a restart refusing a connect under way and a change of page as a quit does, a quit kept to over a restart, either way round, and a restart settled once the sessions are closed); the test that a quit begins only once now also checks how it ends. The task that closes the sessions and then ends the app is not covered, nor is what the window does meanwhile. The restart itself needs a running app and was not tried, on any platform. Listed under **Known issues** until now; found while fixing the update install. The quit in the last instant and what the window passes on were found by the pre-commit review and audit of this change.
- **A connect sent in the same instant as a window reload could stay connected without a tab.** The backend told the sessions of the old page from those of the new one by a count of page loads, which a connect command read when it started running. A request the old page had already sent could start running after the count went up, and passed for one of the new page. Each page now makes up a token when it loads (`src/page.ts`), announces it with the new `page_announce` command, and passes it as `page` to `pty_spawn`, `ssh_connect`, `telnet_connect` and `session_connect`. The backend opens a session only for the page announced last, so a connect of an earlier page is refused whenever it arrives. It is now refused before it reaches the remote host as well; it used to connect and be closed at registration, which a device still saw as a session.
  - **How the page changes.** A page load closes every session, as before, and leaves no page announced. A token other than the announced one is a new page: its announcement closes every session still there and makes it the announced page. One change is made at a time, and an announcement returns only when the change is complete, local shells included, so a session the page opens after it is never taken by a change still under way. Once the app is quitting, the page no longer changes, since the quit closes the sessions itself. Since every connect now waits for the change of page before it, the change waits at most 2 s for the local shells' lock, which a stuck write to a shell can hold; after that it leaves the old page's local shells running, without a tab, until the next change of page or the quit, so that SSH and Telnet can still connect. The quit waits for that lock as before, within its own 4 s.
  - **In the frontend,** the four connect commands are called only through `invokeConnect`, which announces the page and adds the token, so no call can go out without one; an ESLint rule refuses a direct `invoke` of any of the four. Announcing again changes nothing while the page is the same. The page also announces itself as it starts, so that what an earlier page left behind is closed then rather than at its first connect, and tries once more after a second if that fails. The token is built from `crypto.getRandomValues`, since it is made as the module loads and older WebKit lacks `crypto.randomUUID`.
  - **Two side effects of the count are gone.** A connect the new page started before the count went up was refused, and a local shell it started before the old shells were taken was closed with them. The load is now counted as the webview reports it, and the task that closes the sessions does nothing once the page that load brought has announced itself, since that announcement has closed them.
  - **A local shell that cannot be opened now says so** in a toast, as a failed quick connect does. It used to fail silently, which a refused connect would have as well.
  - **What is left.** An announcement of the old page that is itself on its way during the reload makes it the announced page again until the new page starts, and a second connect it sent in that same moment is then opened. The new page's announcement at startup closes it. Should that announcement of the old page arrive later still, after the new page's, it closes the new page's sessions instead, and the new page's next connect closes what the old page opened.
  - Covered by twelve tests in `commands/mod.rs` (a page load during a connect, a connect of an earlier page before and after the new page's announcement, a connect before any announcement, announcing again, the page-load task running after the new page's announcement, a late announcement of the old page, an announcement waiting for a change under way, a change not waiting for a held shell lock, the quit waiting for it, an abandoned wait taking no shells later, no change once the app is quitting, and the token check), which replace the two for the count; by `src/page.test.ts` (14 tests: each connect command going out after the announcement and with its token, a failed announcement, the token's form and that it needs no `randomUUID`, one token per page load, and the announcement at startup with its retry); and by five tests in `TerminalTabs.test.tsx` (a reconnect, a local shell and a saved session each announcing first and passing the token, and a toast when the announcement or the shell fails). The page load itself needs a running app and is not covered by automated tests. Found by the pre-commit audit of the connection clean-up fix.
- **A session log could be written into a file someone else had put in its place, and was briefly open to others.** A log's name is predictable (session name and minute), and the logger opened it with a call that also opens what is already there, following a symbolic link. Someone able to write to the log directory could therefore have terminal output appended to a file elsewhere. On Unix the file, and a log directory the logger had to create, were restricted to their owner only after they were created, so both could be opened in the moment between. The logger now creates each log with a call that fails when anything holds the name (a file, a directory, or a symbolic link, dangling or not, which is not followed), and as 0600 on Unix as part of that call. A missing log directory is created as 0700 the same way, missing parents included.
  - **A name that is taken is passed over.** The log then gets `_1`, `_2`, … up to `_999` before its extension, as it already did for a name in use, but this is now settled when the file is created rather than when the session connects. Two sessions with the same name that connected in the same minute were both given the same name, since neither had a file yet, and wrote into one file; each now gets its own.
  - **A log whose file cannot be created is given up.** The session goes on without a log and the reason is logged once; the session's tab says so as well (see the entry on the toast, below). The logger used to try again on every output, with a warning each time; with the names now tried one by one, that would also have meant up to a thousand failed creates per output where every name is taken.
  - **Session names that got no log, or an odd one.** A session name with two dots in a row, or ending in a dot before the format's own, was refused as a path traversal, so the session was not logged at all; a name starting with a dot gave a hidden file on Unix; an empty name gave a file called `.log`; and a very long name gave a filename the file system refuses. A dot in a name is now kept only between other characters and never next to another dot, an empty name becomes `session`, and a name is cut at 120 bytes. Found by the review of this change.
  - **What is unchanged.** On Windows a log file got its access list from the log directory, as before, and only the exclusive create applied there; that is fixed by an entry of its own, below. A log directory that already exists is still restricted to its owner afterwards on Unix, as far as it can be. Someone able to write to the log directory can still delete or rename a log, and can keep a session from being logged by taking all thousand names for its minute, which the session's tab now reports (below). Someone able to replace the log directory itself, or a directory above it, with a link still decides where the logs go; the create protects only the log's own name.
  - The checks that compared the resolved path of the log with the log directory, before and after opening it, are gone. The log's name holds no path separator, so the one before compared the log directory with itself; the one after ran once the file was open and could only delete it. The new `create_private_append_file` in `private_fs.rs` shares its create with `create_private_file`. On Windows that create now reports a name held by a directory as already existing, as on Unix, where Windows itself says "access denied".
  - Covered by eight tests in `session_logger.rs` (no file before the first output, a file each for two sessions with the same name, a file or directory already there left alone, a link to an existing file and a dangling one not written through, a log given up when it cannot be created and when all thousand names are taken, the session names above, and the modes of a new log and its directory) and two in `private_fs.rs` (appending after a truncation with a taken name refused, and links not followed). The link tests need the right to create symbolic links, which the Windows machine they were written on lacks, so there they say so and end early; they and the mode test ran on Linux, where both source files' tests pass, and where putting back the plain create, or leaving out the mode, makes the corresponding tests fail. The full Rust suite passes on Windows; the app itself was not started. Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit audit of the connection clean-up fix.
- The connect commands left a session log entry and a terminal-ready signal behind on several early returns: the 100-session cap refusing a connection after the log was opened (`telnet_connect`, `session_connect`), the final registration failing (`ssh_connect`, `telnet_connect`, `session_connect`), a login sequence failing to resolve, and `pty_spawn` failing. A new `ConnectSetup` guard in `commands/mod.rs` opens the log, stores the ready signal and undoes both on every early return, until the command disarms it once the session is registered. It replaces the cleanup each command repeated for a failed connection. The cap is now checked before the log is opened. Closing a tab before its terminal attached also left the ready signal behind; `ssh_disconnect`, `telnet_disconnect` and `pty_kill` now drop it, and close the session log even when the session is already gone. `ssh_connect` and `telnet_connect` now cap the connection timeout (300 s), keepalive interval (3600 s) and keepalive count (100) as `session_connect` already did. Two tests in `commands/mod.rs` cover the guard and its disarming. Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit audit of the Telnet dead-session fix.
- File imports in **Settings** (SecureCRT, mRemoteNG, database JSON, highlight profiles) silently did nothing on Windows: no import, no toast, no log line. The shared file-picker helper treated "window regained focus and no file is present one animation frame later" as a cancel, but in WebView2 the window regains focus as the native dialog closes while the chosen file reaches the page over a separate IPC hop, so `change` routinely arrived after the helper had already resolved `null` and removed its input. Introduced in 0.12.0 with the helper itself. The helper moved to `src/pickFile.ts` and now settles only on the input's own `change` or `cancel` events. For webviews that never dispatch `cancel` (WebKit before Safari 16.4), a pick still pending when the next one starts is settled as cancelled then — the native picker is modal, so it can only be a cancel that went unreported — which bounds the leftover to one hidden element. Covered by `src/pickFile.test.ts`, whose focus-before-change test fails against the old helper with the file resolving as `null`.
- SecureCRT import dropped entity and character references inside element text: a username stored as `dom&amp;user` imported as `domuser`, `&#x2D;` vanished from a hostname. quick-xml reports references as separate `GeneralRef` events between the surrounding text chunks, and `read_text` ignored them. Character references and the five predefined XML entities are now resolved; anything else (undeclared entities, invalid character references) is kept verbatim instead of being lost. Session and folder names were unaffected, since they come from attributes, which quick-xml resolves itself; the mRemoteNG importer reads attributes only and was likewise unaffected.
- Imported usernames now go through the same control-character stripping and 255-character cap as imported names and hostnames. With character references resolved, a reference such as `&#x1b;` could otherwise place a raw control character into a username, which flows into SSH authentication and the `user@host` session log name.
- The import sanitizer (`sanitize_imported_name`) now also drops characters that change how a name _displays_ rather than what it contains, which `char::is_control` does not catch: the bidi embedding, override and isolate controls (U+202A–U+202E, U+2066–U+2069) that can make a crafted hostname or username render as a different one, the line and paragraph separators U+2028/U+2029, and invisible characters with no place in a host, user or session name (U+200B, U+FEFF, soft hyphen U+00AD, U+2060–U+2064, U+FFF9–U+FFFB, and the standalone Hangul fillers U+3164 / U+FFA0). Directional marks (U+200E, U+200F, U+061C) and joiners (U+200C, U+200D) are kept because right-to-left and Indic names and emoji sequences depend on them, and marks cannot reorder text the way overrides can. Applies to imported folder names, session names, hostnames, usernames and jump-host references.
- Import warnings and parse errors are now sanitized before they reach the UI. Parser warnings quote raw file content (`Skipped "<name>" — no hostname`, `unsupported protocol: <value>`, `In "<key>": …`) and quick-xml errors can quote XML fragments, so a crafted export could push control or bidi-override characters into the toasts that display them. Both now go through the same character filter as names, capped at 500 characters each. The number of warnings returned is also capped at 20 plus a final "…and N more warnings" entry: each warning becomes its own toast, and a file full of bad entries previously produced one toast per entry with no limit. The app-log summary line still reports the full warning count.
- The one import error that bypassed this — failing to create the top-level "SecureCRT Import" / "mRemoteNG Import" folder — now goes through the same message sanitizer, and is logged at `warn`. Previously the import aborted with a log that showed it starting and nothing after. The text is a database error rather than file content, so the log line carries it whole.
- SecureCRT and mRemoteNG imports could link a session to the wrong jump host. References were resolved by bare session name with the last session of that name winning, so when two imported sessions shared a name (`bastion` in both `EU` and `US`), every session using that jump host went through whichever came last in the file. A reference that matches several sessions in the file is now left unset with a warning naming the session, and so is a session that names itself. Sessions that were not imported count toward that match — whether the parser dropped them (unsupported protocol, no hostname; both parsers now report these by name and folder) or the import did (invalid port, database error) — so a duplicate that did not make it in cannot leave the other copy looking unique. A reference can also be a folder path ending in the session name (`EU/bastion`), relative to the import root, which picks out one of several sessions that share a name. The index tracks only the references that appear in the file, each with a match count and the first match, and builds paths only up to the 255-character name cap, so its size is bounded by the references rather than by the number of sessions. References are sanitized with one character more than names, so a reference longer than any name can be no longer matches whichever session shares its first 255 characters after both were truncated. The not-found and failed-to-set warnings now name the session they concern.
- SecureCRT import dropped every `<key>` named `Default`, `Default_LocalShell`, `Default_RDP` or `Default_Serial` at any depth, with everything under it and without a warning. The rule is meant for SecureCRT's built-in session templates directly under `Sessions`, and now applies only there, and only to a template's own session settings: the same name inside a folder, or a key of that name directly under `Sessions` that has child keys, is the user's own session or folder and is imported. Two other silent drops, in hand-made or crafted files only — a SecureCRT `<key>` without a `name`, and an mRemoteNG `<Node>` whose `Type` is neither `Container` nor `Connection` — now produce a warning that says how many sessions, if any, went with them, and those sessions count toward jump host ambiguity by name, like other dropped sessions: a reference to `bastion` no longer links `Other/bastion` as if it were unique while a second `bastion` sat in a dropped subtree. The template rule has since been checked against an export of SecureCRT 9.7.3 (a folder with two sessions, a session beside it, button bars and the Command Manager): its four templates sit directly under `Sessions`, between the user's own keys, each with `Is Session` set and no child keys, and the parser returns the folder and the three sessions, no folder or session named after a template, and no warning. A test in `import/securecrt.rs` has that layout, cut down to the properties the parser reads. It is one export of one version, taken on Windows with the templates as installed; none of its sessions has a jump host or is a Telnet session, so the form of a `Firewall Name` that names a session and the Telnet `Port` are still as the parser assumes them. Was listed under **Known issues**.
- A SecureCRT or mRemoteNG file cut off part way (an interrupted copy or download) imported everything before the cut without a word, because quick-xml does not report elements still open at the end of the input. Both parsers now fail with "Unexpected end of XML" instead. The SecureCRT parser reads the whole file, since the button bars and the Command Manager come after the sessions (see **Added**), so a file cut off anywhere in it is refused.
- A crafted SecureCRT file could make the import run out of memory. Each nameless `<key>` or `<string>` inside a key is a warning that quotes the key's name, and the parse allows up to 50,000 warnings, so a key with a name of a few megabytes and many such elements built warnings totalling terabytes. Warnings now quote at most 64 characters of a key, session or protocol name, with `…` where it was cut.
- A `<string>` element inside another `<string>` in a SecureCRT file ended the outer one's text at its own end tag. The outer end tag was then taken for the end of the element around it. Elements nested in a string are now skipped whole.
- An imported session whose hostname consisted only of control or formatting characters was stored with an empty hostname, since the parsers check for an empty hostname before sanitizing. It is now skipped with a warning, like a session with an invalid port.
- **Settings → Highlighting → Import Profile** read the chosen `.ini` completely into webview memory and sent it over IPC before the backend's 10 MB limit could reject it; the other three imports already checked the size first. It now checks `file.size` against the same 10 MB up front, with a new `highlighting.importTooLarge` string in all 14 locales.
- `cargo clippy -- -D warnings` failed with 42 `double_must_use` errors under clippy 1.99: `async-trait` 0.1.91 added a bare `#[must_use]` to every generated method, whose boxed-future return type is already must-use. `async-trait` 0.1.92 stops emitting it; lockfile-only bump, no source change.
- `just lint` (`cargo clippy --all-targets --all-features -- -D clippy::pedantic -D warnings`) failed with 16 errors under clippy 1.99. `assert_is_empty` (10, all in tests): the assertions now compare against an empty `Vec` with `assert_eq!`, so a failure prints the unexpected warnings instead of just `false`. `items_after_statements` (3): a `use` and two `const`s moved to the top of `restrict_windows_acl` and `persist_import`. `unused_async_trait_impl`: `SshHandler::data` is removed; it did the same as russh's default no-op, and channel data still arrives through `channel.wait()` in the reader task. `borrow_as_ptr`: `GetStartupInfoW` now gets `&raw mut info`, which needs Rust 1.82, well below the 1.94 the dependencies require (see **Changed**). `doc_markdown`: one doc comment. No behavior change. Checked on Windows only, so code that builds only on Linux or macOS has not been checked against the pedantic lints.
- The app no longer panics at startup when application file logging is on and the log file cannot be opened, for example because the directory is not writable or the disk is full. `init_tracing` built the rolling file appender with `.expect`. It now falls back to stdout-only logging with a message on stderr, as it already did when the directory itself could not be created. The file setup moved into a helper, `open_app_log_file`, so the stdout-only fallback is written once instead of at each way the setup can fail. Four tests in `lib.rs` cover it. One checks that the configured directory opens. The others check that a relative directory, a directory that cannot be created, and a day's file name taken by a directory each make it fall back. Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit audit of the clippy pedantic fix.
- A database import could change existing sessions it reported as skipped, and a crafted file could reach keychain secrets it never contained. A session in the file that matches an existing one (same folder, hostname and port) is skipped, but its jump host was still written to the existing session, so a file could route that session through a bastion the file adds. A credential the file gave for such a session was stored for the existing session. At the next startup, `migrate_legacy_credentials` then relinked that session to a new profile with the file's username and auth type. A credential for a session id not in the file at all was worse: it was stored as given, including its keychain reference. At the next startup the migration read the secret that reference names into a new credential profile, linked the given session to that profile, and deleted the keychain entry. The entry could be another profile's secret or the saved PostgreSQL password. For a `publickey` row, the migration stored that secret in the database as the profile's key path. An import now sets jump hosts and writes credentials only for sessions it created. Each credential gets a fresh id and its new session's `session-<id>` keychain reference, and the import summary counts the credentials it skipped. The migration also drops any legacy row whose reference is not `session-<its own session id>`, without reading or deleting the entry and without linking its session. That is the only form a legacy writer ever produced, so this also covers such a row left by an import in an earlier version. A row an earlier import wrote for a matched session carries that session's own reference, and still migrates once, as before. Covered by tests of `credentials_to_import` and `jump_host_links` in `commands/database.rs`. `migrate_legacy.rs` has tests of the ownership check and of a foreign row being dropped from an in-memory credential store; the latter fails with a profile created when the check is removed. Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit audit of the clippy pedantic fix.
- **PostgreSQL admins: start this version once with the role that owns the tables, normally `shellstation_admin`, for this fix to take effect (details below).** In PostgreSQL mode, any user could delete another user's shared folders and sessions, or take them over or out of everyone else's folder tree, despite the rule that only a shared row's owner can delete it. The `*_shared_update` policies let anyone update a shared row, and PostgreSQL accepts an updated row that passes any permissive policy. That left two ways in:
  - **Taking ownership.** `UPDATE … SET owner = current_user` passed `*_owner_all` and made the row theirs, to delete or make personal. A `WITH CHECK` cannot prevent this, because it sees only the new row.
  - **Moving the row.** Moving a shared session or folder under a folder of their own (`folder_id`, `parent_id`) took it out of everyone else's folder tree. Deleting that folder then deleted the row with it, because the foreign keys cascade and a cascade is not subject to RLS.

  `setup_postgres_rls` now installs two trigger functions:
  - **`shellstation_keep_owner`** (as `folders_keep_owner` and `sessions_keep_owner`) refuses an owner change unless it comes from a superuser (who reassigns a removed user's rows) or the table owner (the admin role, or a member of it), or claims a placeholder `'local'` row for oneself, as the startup fix-up does.
  - **`shellstation_keep_place`** (as `folders_keep_place` and `sessions_keep_place`) refuses a move by anyone other than the row's owner, the table owner or a superuser. The app's own move commands already required the user to own the row.

  Both refuse with `insufficient_privilege`, and editing a shared row's other columns works as before; the last entry of this section closes that as well, and adds a third function. Both run as the updating user, with their `search_path` pinned to `pg_catalog, pg_temp` (temporary schema last) and their catalog lookups schema-qualified, so a temporary table named `pg_class` cannot stand in for the catalog. They are replaced on every run, so a later fix reaches existing databases. This holds under the admin guide's Option A. Option B users hold `TRIGGER` and `TRUNCATE` on the tables, and both reach past row-level security anyway.

  Two smaller changes: the policy list loses an unused fifth field, and the startup warning when the RLS setup is skipped no longer calls that "expected for DML-only users". It now says that only the role that owns the tables can apply the setup.

  **Admins:** for that reason the triggers are installed the first time an instance of this version starts with the owning role's credentials, normally `shellstation_admin`. Do that once after updating, under either permission model; until then both holes stay open. Doc changes:
  - `docs/ADMIN_GUIDE.md` §5 now says so, shows how to check that the triggers exist and are enabled, and says that under Option B they do not stop users holding `TRIGGER` or `TRUNCATE`.
  - §4 notes that reassigning a removed user's rows has to run as a superuser, and adds a check that none are left.
  - §2 warns against a role named `local` and against granting the admin role to regular users. Its Option B description now says that those users' `TRIGGER` and `TRUNCATE` privileges reach past row-level security.
  - `docs/DESIGN.md` §4.5.1 describes the triggers and their limits. §4.5.2 adds the same Option B note.

  Covered by a new ignored test, `shared_rows_keep_their_owner`. It runs the migrations and `setup_postgres_rls` against a throwaway server named in `SHELLSTATION_TEST_PG_URL`, using the admin guide's roles, and drops what it created even when a check fails. It checks that a non-owner:
  - can still edit a shared folder but cannot delete it
  - cannot take the folder or a session over, nor the folder through a temporary `pg_class`
  - cannot move the session or a subfolder under a folder of their own, so deleting that folder leaves them in place

  It also checks that the owner cannot hand the folder to someone else but can move their session between their own folders, and that the admin can reassign the folder. Each refusal is matched to the trigger that gives it. Verified against PostgreSQL 17. The test fails with the move check disabled, and with the catalog lookup unqualified and its `search_path` unpinned. Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit audit of the clippy pedantic fix and its review.
- Restricting the permissions of `~/.ssh` and `known_hosts`, done before a newly trusted host key is saved, could name the wrong Windows account and hid its failures:
  - **Windows:** the `icacls` grant named the user by `%USERNAME%`. On a domain-joined machine with a local account of the same name, that could resolve to the local account. The same call removes inherited permissions, so the user could be locked out of their own `.ssh` directory. It now grants to the user's SID, read once from `whoami /user`. `icacls` and `whoami` run by full path from the system directory, whenever `SystemRoot` is set, as it is on any normal Windows install.
  - **Linux and macOS:** a new directory and file used to be created with the default umask and restricted afterwards. They are now created as 0700 and 0600, through a new `private_fs` module, so they are never briefly open to others. An existing `~/.ssh` is still restricted afterwards, as before.
  - **Logging:** a failed `icacls`, `whoami` or `chmod` used to be ignored without a trace. It is now logged at `warn`.

  Tests cover the `whoami` parser, the SID lookup on Windows, and the private create helpers, including their modes on Unix. `private_fs.rs` was type-checked for Linux, but none of the Unix code has run on Linux or macOS yet. Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit audit of the clippy pedantic fix.
- Imports now check the values they store against what the app can use. They are never stricter than what the app itself stores, so an export always imports again:
  - **JSON database import (Data Export / Import):**
    - It refuses a file in which a session has a protocol other than `ssh` or `telnet`. It also refuses an auth method other than `profile`, `none` (Telnet sessions from before credential profiles), `password`, `publickey` or `keyboard-interactive`. Before, such a session was stored and then failed on every connect.
    - It caps session icons at 128 characters, the limit for editing a session in the app.
    - Highlight profile rules must parse as a list of rules. Login sequence steps must keep to the app's limits on how many there are and how long each pattern and response is. Both used to be stored unchecked.
    - Neither gets the full checks of creating them in the app. A SecureCRT highlight import stores more rules than the editor's limit. Compiling every pattern of a large file would also stall the import, so patterns are still compiled when a session uses them.
  - **SecureCRT and mRemoteNG imports:** a session with an unsupported protocol is skipped with a warning. Their parsers only produce `ssh` and `telnet`, so this guards against a later parser change.

  Every protocol and auth method that the code writing sessions has stored, in any version in the history, still passes. Covered by tests in `commands/database.rs`, including a Telnet session with `none`, a username longer than the old cap, and a profile with more rules than the editor allows. The SecureCRT and mRemoteNG check has no test of its own: the parsers cannot produce another protocol, and `persist_import` needs a running app. Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit audit of the clippy pedantic fix.
- On Linux and macOS, ShellStation's own files are now owner-only:
  - **Config directory:** it holds the config, the local database and, by default, the logs. It used to be created with the default umask, which usually leaves it readable by every user on the machine. It is now created as 0700, and an existing one is restricted to 0700 at startup. With application logging on, the default log directory inside it is treated the same way; either way, it sits inside the owner-only config directory.
  - **`config.json`:** it was written with the default umask and restricted afterwards, in place. It is now written to a new 0600 file beside it, which is then renamed over it. It is never open to others, and an interrupted save leaves the previous config whole instead of a truncated one. A symlink at its path is replaced rather than written through.
  - **Custom log directory:** a directory the user chose for the application log was at first created if missing, but otherwise left alone. It is now treated as the default one is (see **Changed**).
  - **Failures:** a file system without Unix modes can refuse the restriction. That is reported, but the directory is still used. Failing to create the config directory, or to write the default config at first start, used to leave no trace. Both are now reported. A config directory problem also goes to the app log when application logging is on.

  The new `private_fs` helpers have tests, including their modes on Unix. They were type-checked for Linux, but the Unix code has not run on Linux or macOS. Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit audit of the clippy pedantic fix.
- At startup in PostgreSQL mode, the app decides whether migrations need to run by comparing how many were applied with how many the build carries. A database with the same number applied but a different set, such as another build's, counted as up to date, and the app skipped migrating. The check now confirms that every one of the build's migrations is applied. As before, a migration recorded as failed does not count. Covered by a new ignored test, `migrations_check_compares_versions`, run against a throwaway server. It checks that a failed migration and a migration from another build each leave the database not up to date, and it fails with the old count comparison. Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit audit of the clippy pedantic fix.
- **Settings → Database** now refuses a custom SQLite path with `..` components when it is saved, the same rules startup applies. Before, such a path was saved, and on the next start replaced with the default database, leaving only an error message. The check that the parent directory exists no longer canonicalizes it first. Its comment claimed that this prevented traversal, but the path was saved as typed. The shared rules have a test now. Two cleanups with no change in behaviour:
  - `UpdateBuilder::set_optional_uuid` reuses `push`, and its module docs no longer count the columns.
  - The import's progress events say why a lost one is ignored.

  Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit audit of the clippy pedantic fix.
- An SSH tab whose session had ended could keep looking alive: no "process exited" line, typed input silently dropped, and Ctrl+W and Enter sent to the dead session instead of closing or reconnecting the tab. A tab learned that its session had ended only from the backend's `terminal-exit-<id>` event, and `ssh_write` kept accepting writes for a session that was still registered after its connection was gone — the send runs on a detached task, so the command reported success either way. Seen once on Windows after `exit` was sent from a command button: the log showed the exit event emitted, then two Ctrl+W and an Enter reaching the backend over the next 8 seconds, so the frontend had not handled the event, for reasons the log does not show. `ssh_write` now fails with `Session closed` once the session's reader task has stopped or its connection is closed, and a terminal that gets that error — from typed input, a command button or the command bar, including a `\v` clipboard segment, whose write errors were previously swallowed along with clipboard errors — runs the same exit handling as the event. That handling runs once per terminal, whichever signal comes first, and never after unmount. Both sides match the string exactly (`SESSION_CLOSED_ERROR` in `src-tauri/src/ssh/mod.rs` and `src/terminalRegistry.ts`); a test in `src/terminalRegistry.test.ts` reads the Rust constant and fails if the two drift apart. Telnet tabs had the same gap — `telnet_write` hands data to the session's I/O task over a channel from a detached task — and `telnet_write` now fails the same way once that task has ended and closed the channel. Its warning never logged the data, but now names the session.
- `ssh_write` logged the unsent data when a write failed because the connection had closed: russh's `Handle::data` hands the payload back as its error, and the warning printed it with `{e:?}`. Input typed into a session after its connection dropped — including a password typed at what still looked like a prompt — reached the app log in plain text. The warning now carries the session id only, neither the data nor its length.
- Pressing Enter twice on a disconnected saved-session tab, or holding it, before the first reconnect finished opened a second connection that no tab could claim. It stayed connected, with nothing able to show or close it, until the app quit. The tab stays marked exited until `session_connect` resolves, so each Enter started another. A reconnect is now ignored while one is in flight for that tab. A reconnect that resolves after its tab was closed, or while the tab is still being torn down, now disconnects the new session — in the second case the closing tab was previously kept alive on it instead. The teardown uses the saved session's protocol, which is what `session_connect` connected with, rather than the tab's.
- With more tabs open than fit the tab bar, opening another session switched to it but left its tab out of sight past the right edge, so it looked as if the new session had not been focused until the tab bar was scrolled by hand. The tab bar now scrolls the active tab into view whenever the active tab changes: a newly opened tab, a neighbour that takes over when the active tab is closed, or a clicked tab that was only partly visible. A tab already fully in view stays where it is. It uses `scrollIntoView` rather than computing `scrollLeft`, because the tab bar's UI-scale `zoom` skews `getBoundingClientRect` differently per webview engine. `src/test/setup.ts` stubs `scrollIntoView`, which jsdom lacks, and a test in `src/components/TerminalTabs.test.tsx` that fails without the fix checks that opening a tab scrolls that tab into view.
- **Restrict private IPs**, the connection timeout and the keepalive settings reached the backend as arguments of every connect command, so a compromised webview could connect with the check switched off. The backend now keeps them, in a new `connection` section of `config.json`, and `ssh_connect`, `telnet_connect` and `session_connect` read them from there; the four arguments are gone.
  - Three new commands change them. `connection_save_config` takes the timeout and keepalive values (each optional, so two changes at once cannot overwrite each other) and checks their ranges. `connection_set_restrict_private_ips` switches the check on without asking. To switch it off, the backend opens a native warning dialog, words it itself, and saves only on a click of **Stop blocking**. The dialog is in the operating system's language: the first of the user's preferred languages that one of the 14 UI locales covers, or English if none does. It is not in the UI language, which the webview names, so a compromised webview could pick one the user does not read.
  - The webview picks the moment that dialog appears, such as while the user is typing in a terminal, so its first button is **Keep blocking**: Windows and macOS make that the default for Enter, and GTK sets no default, so Enter there only presses a focused button. A third button, **Cancel**, is what closing the dialog counts as: with two buttons, the dialog plugin reports Escape as a click on the second, which would then have had to be the safe one.
  - Only one such dialog is open at a time, until it has closed. After **Keep blocking** or **Cancel**, a request to switch the check off is refused without asking for 30 s, doubling with each further refusal up to an hour, so a compromised webview cannot keep reopening the dialog until a click lands on the wrong button. A **Stop blocking** starts the count again, and the app log records how many refusals there have been. The cooldown is checked once the one-dialog guard is held, so a request already on its way cannot slip in as a dialog closes.
  - The main window may no longer open message, ask or confirm dialogs of its own (`dialog:default` is narrowed to `dialog:allow-open` and `dialog:allow-save`, the two it uses), so it cannot show look-alikes of this one either.
  - The Settings dialog shows the backend's values and follows the answer: unticking the box leaves it ticked until the user confirms. The box stays disabled until the backend's value has loaded, so a default is never shown as the real state. A load or save the backend fails shows a toast.
  - Settings are now stored as version 1. These four values are neither written to localStorage nor read back from it. On the first start, values an older version left there move to the backend: the check only ever moves as "on", and timeouts only within range. A connect timeout above 300 s, which older versions stored but connected with as 300 s, moves as 300 s. They are kept until the backend has taken them, so a failed first start tries again on the next one. A value set in this version in the meantime, in Settings or by an import, is no longer handed over, so the older one cannot overwrite it; that includes switching the check off, once confirmed in the dialog. If that start cannot switch an older "on" for the check on (it could not write `config.json`, say) and the check is off, a notice says so in plain words: connections to private and loopback addresses are not blocked, it is tried again at the next start, and where in Settings to switch the check on now. Unlike other toasts, the notice does not close on its own: it stays until it is dismissed, or until the check is switched on, when it would no longer be true. Toasts that stay are shown once however often they are asked for, so they cannot pile up. The backend's error goes to the app log instead of the notice; any other failure of the move shows a toast with the error, as before. If localStorage refuses to keep them, that start still hands over the copy it read. If what it kept there cannot be read, the check is switched on, as the backend does for a damaged `config.json`, and nothing else is handed over. A data import still only switches the check on, and now reports a setting the backend failed to save.
  - Saving the database settings now keeps the rest of the config under the same lock that writes it, so switching the check on while that save runs is no longer undone.
  - A value out of range in a hand-edited `config.json` gets its default, with a warning in the app log naming it; the check itself is left as it is. A `config.json` that does not parse as a whole (one that is not UTF-8 included), or cannot be read, now fails closed for the check: it keeps the file's own value if that much can still be read, and is on otherwise. Before, every setting fell back to its default, the check included, and the next save wrote the defaults back. The next save still replaces such a file with the defaults, so a copy of it is now kept beside it as `config.json.bad` (owner-only), with a line in the app log. A missing file, as on a first start, still leaves the check off.

  Tests: 17 Rust unit tests (the new section's defaults for an older or partial `config.json`, round trip, range limits and reset, the check failing closed for a damaged or unreadable file but not a missing one, and the copy of a damaged file; a dialog text for every UI locale, with three distinct buttons, the "keep" button named in the message, no broken line continuation, the lookup by language, and the first of the preferred languages with a text winning; the one-dialog-at-a-time guard, the cooldown, its doubling and its reset) and 35 new tests in `src/stores/settingsStore.test.ts` (the four values are neither written to nor read from localStorage, the store follows the dialog's answer, each setter saves only its own value and only in range, the move from localStorage never switches the check off, carries a long timeout over as 300 s, skips values out of range, is retried after a failure without overwriting a value set since, in Settings, by an import or while the hand-over runs (a confirmed "off" included, but not an "off" for a check that was off already), survives localStorage refusing to keep them and switches the check on when what it kept cannot be read unless this launch still holds its own copy, reports the check as off when the backend could not switch it on, but not when it is on anyway, and reports other failures with their error, and an import reports a failed save after applying the rest). `src/privateIpCheckNotice.test.ts` checks that the notice names the setting in the real English text, is not shown when the check is on, goes once the check is on or when taken down, and stops watching once dismissed; `src/components/ToastContainer.test.tsx` that a toast closes at its time and a sticky one stays until dismissed; and `src/stores/toastStore.test.ts` that the same sticky toast is shown once. The notice's string is translated into all 14 UI languages. The fake backend in those tests refuses out-of-range values the way the real commands do. The dialog itself was not opened in this change: it needs the running app. Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit audit of the connection clean-up fix.
- With **Restrict private IPs** on, most private IPv6 addresses got through. `is_restricted_ip` (`ssh/mod.rs`) compared the whole first group of an IPv6 address, so it caught only addresses whose first group was exactly `fe80`, `fc00` or `fd00`, not the rest of `fe80::/10` or `fc00::/7`: `fd12:3456::1` passed. It also ignored the IPv4 address an IPv6 address can carry, so `::ffff:10.0.0.1` passed and the connection went to 10.0.0.1. The check now:
  - matches link-local (`fe80::/10`), unique local (`fc00::/7`) and the deprecated site-local (`fec0::/10`) addresses by prefix;
  - checks the IPv4 address inside IPv4-mapped (`::ffff:0:0/96`), IPv4-translated (`::ffff:0:0:0/96`) and IPv4-compatible (`::/96`) addresses, NAT64 addresses under the well-known prefix (`64:ff9b::/96`), 6to4 addresses (`2002::/16`) and Teredo addresses (`2001::/32`, both its server and its client), since a connection to one of these can reach that IPv4 address;
  - blocks the local-use NAT64 prefix (`64:ff9b:1::/48`) as a whole, since only the local network knows which IPv4 addresses it stands for;
  - blocks all of `0.0.0.0/8` on IPv4, not just `0.0.0.0`.

  A NAT64 network-specific prefix looks like any other address and is not covered. Three new tests in `ssh/mod.rs` cover the edges of the ranges matched by prefix, and each kind of embedded address with a private and with a public IPv4 inside; the prefix test and the private-IPv4 test fail against the old check. The SSH and Telnet connections share the check. Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit audit of moving the connection settings to the backend.
- With **Restrict private IPs** on, the check and the connection resolved a hostname separately. `validate_ssh_target` resolved it and checked every address, then the Telnet `TcpStream::connect` and the SSH `client::connect` (target, or first jump host) resolved it again, so a DNS answer that changed in between (DNS rebinding) could still reach a restricted address. Behind a jump host, the bastion was sent the name and resolved it itself. The check is now `resolve_unrestricted` (`ssh/mod.rs`), which returns the addresses it checked, and the connection goes to exactly those:
  - SSH (target, or first jump host) and Telnet connect to the checked addresses instead of the name;
