  - a jump host is sent a checked address for the next hop or the target instead of the name, each in turn until the bastion opens the tunnel, so it has nothing left to resolve. At most 8 addresses are tried, so a name with a long list of addresses cannot make the bastion try them all;
  - every host in a chain is still checked before the first connection, and a host missing from the checked addresses fails the connection rather than being connected to by name. The lookups of the whole chain and the first connection now share the connection timeout, as the lookup and the connection already did with the check off. Before, a lookup had no time limit;
  - the lookups no longer run on the async runtime's worker threads, and at most 16 run at once. A lookup keeps its thread until the resolver answers, even after its connection has timed out, so it keeps its place until then too: names that never answer cannot tie up the threads the rest of the app shares.

  A refused host's message no longer names the restricted address it resolved to, and a failed lookup's message no longer quotes the resolver's error: both go to the log, so a compromised webview cannot read DNS answers through them.

  With the check on, a host behind a jump host already had to resolve on this machine; what changes is a name the bastion resolves to a different address. The tunnel now goes to the address seen here, so a split-DNS setup that relies on the bastion's own answer gets the local one. With the check off, the bastion still resolves the name. The bastion is trusted to connect to the address it is sent. Thirteen new tests in `ssh/mod.rs`: a public literal comes back as given; restricted literals and `localhost` are refused without the message naming the address, and a failed lookup without the resolver's error; a lookup its caller gave up on keeps its place until the resolver answers; the chain's checked addresses run out into an error, never into the name; a connection goes to the checked addresses and never resolves the name, or goes by name when nothing was checked; and an in-process bastion is sent the checked addresses in order and never the name, or the name when nothing was checked. The tunnel fails once every checked address is refused, sends no further address after a timeout, gives all its attempts one timeout between them, and tries no more than 8. Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit audit of the Telnet dead-session fix.
- A tunnel a jump host would not open was reported with russh's error for it, shown as is in the error toast. For a refusal reason russh has no name for, that error carries the reason text the jump host sent. The message now gives the reason in words of its own (the jump host does not allow it, could not connect, does not support tunnels, is out of resources, or refused it), and any other error goes through the same filter as the SSH connection errors; the jump host's text goes to the log. One new test in `ssh/mod.rs`. Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit audit of the DNS rebinding fix above.
- With **Restrict private IPs** on, multicast (`224.0.0.0/4`, `ff00::/8`), reserved (`240.0.0.0/4`) and special-purpose (`192.0.0.0/24`, `198.18.0.0/15`) addresses got through. None of them is a single host on the internet, and `198.18.0.0/15` is also where a local proxy in "fake-IP" mode answers the names it forwards itself, so a name that resolves there can reach a service on this machine. `is_restricted_ip` now refuses them, also inside an IPv6 address that carries one. Two new tests in `ssh/mod.rs`, and the test of allowed addresses gains the edges of each new range. Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit audit of the DNS rebinding fix above.
- Dependencies with RustSec advisories are updated, except `keyring`, whose update is left for a change of its own:
  - **Tauri's HTTP client** (the updater's downloads): `rustls` 0.23.42 → 0.23.46 (four advisories, among them TLS 1.3 handshake messages accepted at the wrong encryption level and a `ServerHello` downgrade), `rustls-webpki` 0.103.16 and `webpki-root-certs` 1.0.11 (distrusted intermediates accepted), and `tokio` 1.54.1 (unsound `AsyncFd` constructors).
  - **The PostgreSQL driver:** `webpki-roots` 1.0.11 and `ctutils` 0.4.7 (non-constant-time comparisons).
  - **russh:** `crypto-bigint` 0.7.10 (timing variability in constant-time operations; 0.7.5 was also yanked), and the yanked `chacha20` and `wnaf`.
  - **The updater's `zip`** 4.6.1 → 9.1.3 (three extraction advisories) came only with `tauri-plugin-updater` 2.14, which needs Tauri 2.12. Tauri and its plugins therefore move to their current releases, crates and npm packages alike, so the two stay on the same versions: `tauri` 2.12.5, `@tauri-apps/api` and `@tauri-apps/cli` 2.12.4, updater 2.14.1, dialog 2.8.4, clipboard manager 2.5.0 (whose yanked `clipboard-win` 5.4.1 is now 5.4.3), opener 2.7.2 and process 2.4.0. `tauri-utils` no longer uses the unmaintained `unic-*` crates, which drop out.

  The dialog plugin's message dialog, and the `rfd` code behind it, are unchanged on Windows, macOS and GTK apart from how Windows waits for the answer, so the **Restrict private IPs** dialog keeps its buttons, their order and its default. These updates change only the lockfiles and `package.json`; the advisories still open are listed under **Known issues**. The full Rust and frontend test suites pass; the app itself was not started.
- `russh` 0.62.4 → 0.65.1, for client-side fixes from its GitHub security advisories, which RustSec does not list yet. A malicious server could have the client run channel callbacks for a channel it never opened (GHSA-47hw-gvq5-r2gm), panic the session task by getting a cipher that needs a MAC negotiated without one (GHSA-p8qx-h547-fjw9), grow the client's memory without limit with `EXT_INFO` messages (GHSA-4p9f-7p9m-5vfg), hang a channel by announcing a maximum packet size of zero (GHSA-fw5c-8223-6h42), or overflow the stack with a deeply nested certificate in the host key it sends during key exchange (GHSA-3hm4-p7h4-rvwp). The agent and Pageant advisories in the same releases do not apply, since the app has no agent support. Two more fixes reach the app: a keepalive that fell due before authentication had finished made the session task spin a CPU core (keepalives are on by default every 15 seconds, and the host key prompt alone can wait 60), and the channel window was miscounted on a window adjust.
  - russh 0.63 lets a server present a host certificate instead of a plain key, so `check_server_key` now receives either. The app asks for none: a certificate is trusted through a certificate authority, and the app has no list of those. `base_preferred`, which `legacy_preferred` builds on, therefore leaves `host_key_certificates` empty, and should a certificate arrive anyway, `check_server_key` refuses it rather than checking the plain key inside it against known_hosts. The default algorithm lists are otherwise unchanged, so the same algorithms are negotiated as before.
  - 0.65.1 is taken for two fixes to connecting, and not for its two advisories, neither of which reaches the app: one is in the agent (GHSA-736f-c93q-9fwx), and the other is about a `kex_done` callback that was handed the key exchange's shared secret before the host key was checked (GHSA-9264-jvgf-6w2f), which the app does not have. russh 0.65.0 took the shortest packet of `3des-cbc` with an encrypt-then-MAC MAC for too short and ended the connection there. `3des-cbc` is offered only with the **Legacy algorithms** switch on, and no release had the fault: 0.30.0 has russh 0.62.4, which did not make that check. And a host key signature whose two ECDSA numbers a device pads with leading zeros, as Huawei VRP does, is now read as OpenSSH reads it, where russh refused it and the connection with it. `untrusted` 0.7.1 drops out of the dependency tree. Only the lockfile changes.
  - One new test in `ssh/mod.rs`: neither algorithm set asks for host certificates.
- More crates move to their current patch releases, two of them for RustSec advisories, after which `cargo audit` reports no vulnerability:
  - **russh** 0.65.1 → 0.65.2, which was left out above. It takes `rsa` 0.10.0 where it pinned a release candidate, and with it `ssh-key` 0.7.0-rc.12 and the final releases of `pkcs1`, `argon2` and `blake2`; its own code changes only to follow the new names in `rsa`. `ssh-key` now refuses a certificate whose signing key is itself a certificate, which does not reach the app, since it asks for no host certificates. `rsa` 0.10.0 is the first version without RUSTSEC-2023-0071 (Marvin Attack), which was under **Known issues** as not reachable from a client. `Cargo.toml` now asks for russh 0.65.2 at least. A login with an RSA key (`rsa-sha2-512`) against OpenSSH 9.7 was tried by hand with these versions; no test covers one.
  - **sqlx** 0.9.0 → 0.9.1, and with it `libsqlite3-sys` 0.37.0 → 0.40.0 for a CVE in the bundled SQLite (RUSTSEC-2026-0459), which sqlx 0.9.0 did not allow. Of its fixes these reach the app: a statement whose cached plan a change to its table made stale ("cached plan must not change result type", as after a migration that another client applied) is prepared and run again, or inside a transaction fails once and is prepared again the next time, a transaction cancelled during its `BEGIN` is rolled back, the pool's maintenance no longer spins a CPU core after a miscount of idle connections, and SQLite's transaction depth follows a rollback that SQLite made itself. The PostgreSQL driver now uses more of `whoami`, which on macOS brings one new crate into the build, `objc2-system-configuration` 0.3.2; that build was not tried here.
  - **`PGHOSTADDR` no longer decides where the database connection goes.** sqlx fills its connection options from libpq's `PG*` environment variables, and the app sets host, port, database, user, password and SSL mode over them. Up to sqlx 0.9.0 `PGHOSTADDR` was one more source of the host, so the host from Settings replaced it. From 0.9.1 it is an address of its own, as in libpq: the connection goes there in place of the host, unless the host is the directory of a Unix socket, and nothing in the options takes it back. With the variable set, as by someone who uses it for `psql`, the app would have connected to that address and logged in there, with the host from Settings only as the TLS server name, which none of the app's SSL modes checks a certificate against. The app now takes the variable out of its own environment at startup, before it starts a thread, and the application log has a warning when it was set. A local shell tab is still started with the variable, which is the user's; an instance the app starts itself, at the restart from Settings or after an update, is started without it, and so are its local shells. No release had sqlx 0.9.1. What the app does not set is read from the environment as before (`PGSSLROOTCERT`, `PGSSLCERT`, `PGSSLKEY`, `PGAPPNAME`, `PGOPTIONS`); none of these decides where the connection goes, and `docs/DESIGN.md` §4.3 now says what they do (see **Known issues**). A password file (`PGPASSFILE`) may be read as well; the password from Settings replaces what it gives.
  - **tauri** 2.12.5 → 2.12.6, with `tauri-build` 2.7.5, `tauri-utils` 2.10.3, `tauri-runtime-wry` 2.12.4, `wry` 0.57.2 and `tauri-plugin-opener` 2.7.3; the crates need each other's new versions, so `cargo update -p tauri` alone moves none of them. Emitting an event to the window no longer holds the locks on the listeners and the webviews while it waits for the main thread, where a main thread that wanted one of them at that moment hung. On macOS the restart from Settings and after an update now starts the new instance with `open -n -g`, which does not bring it to the foreground; that was not tried. Its third change, to the proxy for a development server on a phone, is in no desktop build. `wry` asks for `dom_query` 0.29 on Android only, so the lockfile lists it and the HTML parser it brings (`html5ever` 0.40 and fourteen more crates) beside the 0.28 of `tauri-utils`, and no build of the app compiles them. The npm packages stay at 2.12.4, on the same 2.12 line.
  - `uuid` 1.29.0 → 1.29.3, `regex` 1.13.1 → 1.13.2, `zeroize` 1.9.1 → 1.9.2 and `libc` 0.2.189 → 0.2.191. `libc` 0.2.190 removed items it had long deprecated. Nothing in the tree uses one: the crates that depend on `libc` and need no C compiler were type-checked for Linux and macOS with the new lockfile, `nix` 0.28 under `portable-pty` among them.
  - `cargo update -p` again moved edges of crates it was not asked about to older versions: `windows-sys` 0.61.2 to 0.60.2 in thirteen crates, and `getrandom` 0.4.3 to 0.3.4 in `tempfile`. Those edges are put back to what they were before, as was done for the russh update above; cargo takes the lockfile as it is with `--locked`.
  - The Rust test suite passes with these versions on Windows, the PostgreSQL tests included; the app itself was not started. Nothing asks for more than Rust 1.94.
  - One new test in `config.rs`: with `PGHOSTADDR` set, the connection options name no address once the variable is taken, and a local shell is still given it. It runs itself once more in a process of its own, since the environment is the whole process's.
  - The doc comment on `base_preferred` made `just lint` fail (`known_hosts` without backticks); fixed with the `known_hosts` changes below.
- `npm audit` reported 13 advisories (9 high), all in development tooling, none in the shipped app. The ones that matter most are in `vite`'s dev server, which `tauri dev` runs: its editor launcher could be made to run a command (some editors, and on Windows through unescaped `%`), a page from another origin could run script through custom middleware, and files outside `server.fs.allow` could be served (on Linux and macOS, when project paths match system paths). The rest are in test and lint tooling: `vitest`'s mocker (a path traversal and unauthenticated mock registration), `undici` (through `jsdom`), and denial-of-service fixes in `brace-expansion`, `browserslist`, `js-yaml`, `nanoid`, `postcss`, `source-map-js`, `zod`, `baseline-browser-mapping` and `@babel/core`. `npm audit fix` moved them to fixed versions within the existing ranges (`vite` 8.0.16 → 8.3.5, `vitest` 4.1.8 → 4.1.12 and others); only `package-lock.json` changes, and `npm audit` now finds none. The production bundle built with the updated `vite` and `rolldown` has the same files as before, and its differences are minifier output only. The type check, lint, formatting check and 179 frontend tests pass; the app itself was not started.
- The webview was granted the opener plugin's default permissions, which no part of it uses: only the **Help** menu opens links, and it does so from Rust, which needs no permission. A compromised webview could therefore have opened any `http`, `https`, `mailto` or `tel` link in its default application, and shown any file on disk in the file manager, since revealing a file has no path scope. The grant is removed. The plugin is also no longer told to inject its link-click handler into the webview, which opened links through that permission when they had `target="_blank"` or were clicked with Ctrl or Shift held; the app has no links. The unused `@tauri-apps/plugin-opener` npm package is removed as well, so importing it in the webview now fails at build time rather than when it is called; the built bundle is byte-for-byte the same. One new test in `lib.rs`: no capability file grants an `opener:` permission. Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit audit of the dependency updates above.
- The host key prompt looked the same for a first connection, for a host whose key had changed, and for a `known_hosts` that could not be checked, and Enter accepted all three. A changed key, the classic sign of a man in the middle, was only a line in the log. ShellStation now reads `known_hosts` itself instead of through russh, and the prompt says what it found:
  - **New host:** as before, and Enter accepts. Enter with **Reject** focused now rejects; it used to accept.
  - **Key changed:** a different key of the same type is on record. The title and the offered fingerprint are red, and the recorded fingerprint is shown above it. Accepting takes a click on **Accept Anyway**. Enter clicks a focused button in the dialog and otherwise does nothing, so a keystroke meant for something else cannot trust the key. When several prompts wait in turn, the next one no longer inherits the last one's focused **Accept** button. Accepting replaces the recorded key, as before.
  - **Key type changed:** only keys of other types are on record. A man in the middle could offer another type so as not to look like a changed key, and russh treated this as a new host. It gets the same warning and click as a changed key.
  - **Not checked:** a `known_hosts` file could not be read, or the host's entry in it could not be parsed. Same warning and click.
  - **Revoked:** a key listed under `@revoked` for the host is refused without asking, as in OpenSSH. russh ignored these lines. A revocation's names may be patterns such as `*`. A revocation for the host that cannot be parsed might be of the offered key, so that key is then "not checked" rather than trusted. Revocations are never removed, so this happens on every connection until the line is fixed.

  Before, one line russh could not read made it discard the whole file: a single non-UTF-8 byte anywhere, or an entry for the host with a key it could not parse. Every host was then prompted as new, with only a `debug` line in the log, which the default `info` level drops. The new reader skips such a line, logs it at `warn` with its line number, and checks the rest. It reads a line as OpenSSH does, up to a NUL byte if it has one, with the key type matching the key. It trusts a key only for an exact name, or a hashed one in the form OpenSSH writes. Names are looked up as OpenSSH writes them: in lower case, matching a plain name in either case, and with an IPv6 address out of any brackets it was given in. Both used to be taken as typed, so `[::1]` or `Example.com` missed an entry or a revocation that OpenSSH would find, and a key saved for them was written under a name OpenSSH does not look up. A pattern such as `*.example.com` never trusts a key, and a negated pattern that matches (`!example.com`) takes a line out. Matching a pattern takes at most the pattern's length times the host name's, never exponential time. `@cert-authority` lines are skipped, since no host certificate is accepted. A `known_hosts` linked to `/dev/null` counts as empty, as in OpenSSH. A directory in its place, a file over 64 MB, or one that cannot be read gives the "not checked" prompt, with the reason in the log, and a key accepted then is not saved. The file is read through one handle with that limit, and the prompt shows up to eight keys on record of each kind, however many the file holds.

  Rust tests cover trusted, changed, other-type, unchecked and revoked keys, patterns and bad lines. One test checks that whatever the reader counts for a host, saving a new key for it replaces. Frontend tests check that Enter accepts only a first connection's key, never clicks a button outside the dialog, and is not given a focused button from the previous prompt; also that Escape rejects. The new strings are translated into all 14 UI languages.
- A host name could add markup to the host key prompt. The prompt's text is filled in by react-i18next's `Trans`, which puts the host name into the translation before it reads the translation's markup, and the app turns i18next's escaping off. A name such as `x</1><br><strong>VERIFIED</strong>` could therefore break the line, add bold text, or close the bold host name early; no script or attribute could get in. The values are now escaped for `Trans` and unescaped in the text, so a host name shows as typed. A test renders all four prompts with the real English translations and such a name. Host names holding `<` or `>` are now refused anyway (see below). Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit audit of the prompt above.
- Once a host key was trusted, the host's name was written into `known_hosts` as given, and nothing but its length had been checked. With a comma in the name, such as `x,example.com`, the key became trusted for `example.com` as well. A space or a line break could add any key for any host, including `*`, which OpenSSH, sharing `~/.ssh/known_hosts`, reads as every host. This took someone who can set a session's host name and jump host (any user of a shared session in PostgreSQL mode, a session imported from someone else's file, or a compromised webview), and a user who accepts the host key. SSH connections now refuse a host or jump host name that is empty or holds whitespace, a control character or any of `, * ? ! [ ] # @ |` before connecting to anything. No host name or address contains those. The one exception is brackets around a whole IPv6 address, such as `[::1]` or `[fe80::1%eth0]`, which Windows resolves and which name no other host in `known_hosts`. Since the host key prompt shows the name, it also refuses the characters OpenSSH's own host name check refuses (`' " $ \ ; & < > ( ) { }` and the backtick), and invisible characters with which a name could pass for another, such as bidi controls, zero-width and filler characters, soft hyphens, variation selectors and tag characters. The error quotes the name with control and invisible characters escaped. The code that saves a key refuses such a name as well. Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit audit of the first change in this release to the host key check, which logged an unreadable `known_hosts` at `warn` and grew into the host key prompt entry above.
- Saving a newly trusted host key to `known_hosts` had three problems:
  - **Hashed entries stayed.** Accepting a changed key left the old entry in place when it was hashed (`|1|…`), as OpenSSH writes entries with `HashKnownHosts`, the default on Debian and Ubuntu. The new key was then reported as changed on every later connection. Old entries are now found by hashed names too, with `hmac` and `sha1` as new direct dependencies at the versions russh already uses. A line that names other hosts as well, often the host's address, loses only this host's name: the others keep the old key, so connecting by address still reports a changed key rather than a new host. The whole line used to go. A line goes whole when taking the host's name out would leave a name first that makes OpenSSH read it differently, such as a hashed name or `@cert-authority`. Names are read as OpenSSH reads them, and as the new `known_hosts` reader does, except that hashed names are matched more leniently, so that no entry for the host is left behind however it was written. Comments, `@revoked` and `@cert-authority` lines, line endings and lines that are not UTF-8 are kept byte for byte. CRLF line endings used to become LF. A file that was not valid UTF-8 used to keep all its old entries, so once its bad bytes were fixed, the new key was reported as changed in the same way.
  - **The file was rewritten in place.** It was truncated and written again, so a crash part way lost every entry, and a failed write went unnoticed. Taking out the old entries and adding the new one is now a single atomic replace, through the same owner-only helper as `config.json`, and a failure is logged at `warn`. It replaces russh's `learn_known_hosts_path`, and runs off the async runtime. A symbolic link at `~/.ssh/known_hosts`, say into a dotfiles repository, is still followed: the file it points to is replaced, and ends up owner-only, and the link is kept. Of the directories, only `~/.ssh` is restricted, not the one the link points into. A link that points nowhere is an error, and so is anything but a regular file, such as a link to `/dev/null`, which switches `known_hosts` off. In both cases the key is not saved.
  - **Concurrent saves lost keys.** Two connections whose keys were accepted at the same moment could each read the file before the other had written it, and the later write dropped the other key. A lock now covers the read and the write. It covers one running ShellStation, not a second instance or OpenSSH writing at the same moment.

  Tests in `ssh/mod.rs` use hashed names written by OpenSSH's `ssh-keygen -H`. They check through the new `known_hosts` reader that a saved key is trusted, that the old one is reported as changed or revoked, and that an address sharing the old key keeps it. They also check that every other kind of line is kept as it was. A further test saves keys for eight hosts at once; with the lock taken out, it failed in each of three runs. The symlink test skips itself on Windows without the privilege to create links, and the next test's checks of a link to `/dev/null` and of a link that points nowhere only run on Unix. None of these symlink checks have run here. Their steps were run on Linux as standalone programs, but the tests themselves have not. Pre-existing; found by the same audit.
- Host keys were looked up only under the host's name, and only in `~/.ssh/known_hosts`. A key an administrator had listed in the system-wide file was asked about as for a new host, and one revoked there could be accepted with a keystroke. ShellStation now reads what OpenSSH reads by default:
  - **More files:** `~/.ssh/known_hosts2` beside the user's own, and the system-wide `ssh_known_hosts` and `ssh_known_hosts2` in `/etc/ssh`, or on Windows in `%ProgramData%\ssh`. What they hold counts together, as in OpenSSH: a key trusted in one is not reported as changed because another holds a different key, and a key revoked in any is refused. A trusted key is still saved only to `~/.ssh/known_hosts`. A file that is missing counts as empty. One that cannot be read leaves the key unchecked, since it might revoke it, but `known_hosts2`, which nothing writes any more, is skipped then, as OpenSSH skips it. A system-wide file is skipped too, with the reason in the log, unless only an administrator can have caused it: a directory in its place, or a file over the 64 MB limit. Anything else that keeps it from being read is either another user, who on Windows can hold it open or lock part of it, or the administrator keeping it from this user; neither may turn every prompt into a warning. What a file that another user holds or locks counts for now, and when such a hold can put a warning on a connection after all, is in the next entry, on files another user holds or locks.
  - **Only files no one but an administrator can change are read.** Any user of a Windows machine can create a folder in `%ProgramData%`, and a folder an administrator makes there by hand lets every user add files to it. Another user of the machine could otherwise put a file there that trusts their own key for any host. A system-wide file is now read only when no one but an administrator can change it or its directory:
    - **On Windows,** both must be owned by SYSTEM, the Administrators group or TrustedInstaller. Their access lists must allow no one else to write or append to the file, add files to the directory or remove them, delete or rename either, change their times or other attributes, or change their permissions or owner. The times are part of this since the next entry, on files another user holds or locks. Entries that only deny rights, or only apply to what is created inside, are of no matter, and nor are those for OWNER RIGHTS, which here is an administrator. An entry of a kind the check does not read fails it, as does one too short for the SID it names, and so does having no access list at all, which lets everyone in. The directory must not be a link: a junction, which any user can make, could be pointed elsewhere once checked. So it is opened without following a link, refused if it is one, and otherwise checked through that one handle. `%ProgramData%\ssh` as Windows' OpenSSH sets it up passes. One made by hand does not, until the right to add files that Users inherit from `%ProgramData%` is taken away.
    - **On Linux and macOS,** both must be owned by `root` and writable by no one else.
    - **Checked through the handle it is read from.** The file is checked through the handle it is then read from, so it cannot be swapped for another between the check and the read. A symbolic link is followed, as in OpenSSH: the file it leads to is what is checked, and the directory checked is the one holding the link, so only an administrator can have placed it. The directory of the file it leads to is not checked, since NixOS links `/etc/ssh/ssh_known_hosts` into `/nix/store`, which a group can write to.
    - **Untrusted files count for nothing.** A file someone other than an administrator could have changed counts for nothing, not even its revocations, so that one user cannot refuse another's connections.
    - **The folder comes from Windows, not the environment.** The `ProgramData` folder is asked of Windows with `SHGetKnownFolderPath`, rather than read from `%ProgramData%`. Whoever starts the app could point that variable at a network share that reports any owner. Since Windows may fill in the path from `%SystemDrive%`, it is used only on a fixed drive of the machine; otherwise `C:\ProgramData` is used.
    - **Hand-written FFI.** The owner and access list are read through a few lines of FFI in the new `ssh/windows_security.rs`, as for `GetStartupInfoW` in `lib.rs`, rather than the `windows-sys` crate.
  - **The address is looked up too, without adding prompts.** For a direct connection, the entries under the address connected to count as well, as OpenSSH's `CheckHostIP` does. OpenSSH has had that off by default since 8.5: addresses change hands, and it warned each time one did. So only the part that cannot cause such warnings is taken. A key revoked under the address is refused. When the host's name is not on record but the address is, with another key, the first connection's prompt becomes a warning, **Host Address Has Another Key**, that takes a click. The address's entries never trust a key, a host trusted by name is never asked about because of its address (a different key there is logged at `warn`), and no address is written to `known_hosts`. An entry under the address that cannot be read leaves a new host unchecked. When one under the host's name cannot be read, the prompt says the key could not be checked rather than that only the address is on record, since that entry might be of the key. Loopback, link-local and unspecified addresses, a host given as an address, and a host reached through a jump host, which resolves its name, are looked up by name only. The prompt shows the keys on record for the address, but not the address, as the webview is not told what a name resolves to. To learn the address, `connect_checked` now opens the TCP connection itself, as russh's `client::connect` did, and builds the handler once it is up.

  The prompt also shows a status the webview does not know as a key that could not be checked, rather than as a first connection; Enter did not accept one even before.

  Rust tests cover:
  - files counting together;
  - what a file that cannot be read costs, by whose file it is, given what was read from each so that the result does not depend on who runs the test;
  - a missing file, a directory, and a system-wide file others can change, read from disk;
  - the owner and access-list rules, including the Users' right to add files that a folder made by hand inherits;
  - entries under the address: other keys, the same key, a hashed entry, a name trusted or changed, a pattern, another port, a damaged entry under the address or under the name, and revocations, hashed ones too;
  - how addresses are named;
  - that the handler is told the address reached, both for checked addresses and by name.

  In manual runs, taking out any one of these failed exactly the matching tests: the access-list check, the checks of a system-wide file and its directory, the skipping of an unreadable `known_hosts2`, the order of the prompts, the hand-over of the address, or the lookup under it.

  Windows-only tests:
  - Windows' own `hosts` file is read as a system-wide file, unless the user running the tests may change it.
  - A test file's owner and access list are read through its handle, and its directory's through a handle opened as the check opens one.
  - Access-list entries that `icacls` adds are read by kind: a denial, a grant only for what is created inside, and a grant to Users to add files. Reading a denial as a grant, or ignoring that a grant is only for what is created inside, each failed this test in manual runs.
  - A junction in a directory's place is refused.
  - Only a fixed drive counts as one of the machine's own.
  - The `ProgramData` folder Windows reports is checked against `%ProgramData%`.

  The Unix branch of the check was compiled and linted for Linux, but not run. Its rules are tested on every platform, but its test against `/etc/passwd` has not run.

  Frontend tests check that an unknown status, including one named like a property every object has, gets the unchecked warning and is not accepted with Enter.

  The new prompt has not been tried in the running app. Its strings are translated into all 14 UI languages; the 13 besides English have not been reviewed by a native speaker.

  Found as a known issue of the host key prompt (see the entries above). The access lists, the handle, the `ProgramData` folder and what an unreadable file costs were found by the pre-commit review and audit of this change.
- On Windows, another user of the machine could have the `@revoked` lines in the system-wide `known_hosts` ignored. Anyone who can read a file can hold it open without sharing it, or lock part of it, and ShellStation then skipped the file with only a `warn` in the log. To use this, a local user had to hold the file while a network attacker presented a revoked key that the victim had already trusted in their own `known_hosts`. Changes:
  - **A locked part no longer stops the read.** When part of a system-wide file is locked, it is read through a read-only mapping instead, which such a lock does not stop. The mapping is copied with `ReadProcessMemory`, so a page that cannot be read, say on an I/O error of the drive, fails the read rather than end the process. A locked file over the 64 MB limit is not mapped. It counts as too large, as an unlocked one does, and leaves the key unchecked; before, the lock made it skipped. The FFI is in `ssh/windows_security.rs`: the declarations of the six functions it calls, and `read_mapped`.
  - **What a file held is remembered.** Each time a system-wide file is read whole, after it and its directory have passed the check that only an administrator can change them, ShellStation stores its `@revoked` lines as `ssh_known_hosts.revoked` and `ssh_known_hosts2.revoked`. They go in `%LOCALAPPDATA%\shellstation`, which a roaming profile does not carry to another machine. A comment line goes first, with the file's size and the times it was created and last written, taken before the read.
    - The stored file is written only when any of that changes. It is kept even when the file has no revocations, so that a file read without any can be told from one never read.
    - Every SSH connection reads the system-wide files, so while a file is there, what is stored follows the administrator's changes. A file that is deleted, or no longer trusted, leaves it as it is, which can only refuse more.
    - Only on Windows, since no other platform lets another user keep a file from being read.
    - Only in the platform's local data directory. When there is none, nothing is stored, rather than in a directory relative to where the app was started, and a `warn` says so.
  - **A held file keeps its revocations.** When another process holds a system-wide file open without sharing it for reading, or has locked part of it and a mapping cannot read it either, the revocations stored from it count.
    - **The file itself is still checked.** It must pass the check that only an administrator can change it, as when it is read. A hold keeps it from being opened to read, so it is checked through a handle that reads no more than its attributes, owner and access list, which a hold does not stop. A file that fails counts for nothing, stored revocations included.
    - **A change since is noticed.** The file's size and times can still be read while it is held. When they are not what was stored, the file has changed and might revoke the key now. The key is then left unchecked, which takes a click, unless a stored revocation already refuses it. A hold that shares writing but not reading lets an administrator's in-place write through, as a manual try with `[IO.File]::WriteAllText` showed, so this is how a revocation added during a hold shows.
    - **Only an administrator may change the times.** Setting them back would hide an edit that keeps the size, such as revoking another key of the same length in place of one. So the check that only an administrator can change a system-wide file, and its directory, now also counts the right to change their times or other attributes. On this machine, `%ProgramData%\ssh` gives SYSTEM and Administrators full control and Authenticated Users read and execute, which still passes, as does Windows' own `hosts` file.
    - **A file never read is skipped,** as before and as OpenSSH skips it, when nothing was stored because ShellStation has never read it whole. Leaving the key unchecked then would let a hold alone put a warning on the connections of every user whose ShellStation has not read the file.
    - When the stored file is there but cannot be read, the key is left unchecked, since it might be revoked. So is a stored file larger than one can be: 64 MB and a kilobyte.
    - Nothing else in the held file counts, as before: a key on record there neither trusts the key nor makes the prompt a warning, so a warning it would have given becomes a first connection's prompt. Only `@revoked` lines are taken from the stored file, whatever else is put there.
    - Within one ShellStation, reading the stored file and replacing it take the same lock. Between two, the replace is a rename, which a read sees whole or not at all.
    - On Windows the stored file is kept from other users by the permissions of the user's own profile, which `%LOCALAPPDATA%` is part of.
  - **Logging**, at `warn` unless said otherwise:
    - A file held by another process is now told apart from one an administrator keeps from the user. The message says that the file is held, and then one of three things:
      - how many stored revocations counted, and whether the file has changed since;
      - that nothing is remembered from it;
      - that what was stored could not be read.
    - A locked part read through a mapping is logged, since nothing should lock the file, and so is a lock that a mapping cannot get past either.
    - Failing to store or to read what is remembered is logged, and so is having no local data directory to store it in, once. Storing it is logged at `debug`.

  A hold can still hide a revocation from a ShellStation that has never read the file whole (see **Known issues**). Keeping the file open while the app runs, so that no one could take it for themselves afterwards, was tried by hand and left out. While a handle that shares reading, writing and deleting was open on the file, PowerShell's `Set-Content` and `move /y` failed on it. An administrator could then not have used them while any user had ShellStation running. Writing the file in place, `Copy-Item`, `copy /y` and `Move-Item` still worked.

  `config.rs` gains two functions:
  - `app_config_dir` works out the app's config directory, which `run` uses as before.
  - `local_app_dir` gives the local data directory for the stored revocations. It has no fallback to the working directory.

  Tests in `ssh/mod.rs`, run on Windows:
  - Only `@revoked` lines are stored, after the file's size and times.
  - A held file's stored revocations refuse a key that the user trusts, under the host's name and under the address.
  - A held file read before without revocations, or never, is skipped as before.
  - A held file that has changed since leaves the key unchecked, though a stored revocation still refuses it.
  - A stored line naming the key, or another key, for the host is ignored, and the prompt stays a first connection's. A stored revocation that cannot be parsed, and a stored file that cannot be read, leave the key unchecked.
  - Revocations are stored from a trusted read, but not from an untrusted file, the user's own or a missing one, nor with remembering off. A trusted read without revocations stores the size and times alone. Read again unchanged, nothing is written again.
  - An edit that keeps the size counts as a change by its time. With the time set back, it would not, which is what the new access-list rule guards against. An edit that changes the size counts as a change even with the time set back.
  - A file counts as changed when its stored size and times are missing or not as written, or when its present ones are not given.
  - The access-list rule refuses a right for someone else to change the times.
  - Windows-only tests lock a file through one handle, or hold one through another, as another process would:
    - A plain read of a file with a few bytes locked fails as held by another process, and the mapping reads it whole.
    - A mapping of an empty file reads nothing, and one longer than the file is an error. A locked file over the limit counts as too large.
    - A file held without sharing is still checked, through a handle that reads its access list, and counts for nothing when the check fails. Trusted, it gives the stored revocations, and the key is refused. The user's own file held so is unreadable, as before.
    - A file held without sharing reading can still be written to. The revocation added then leaves the key unchecked while the hold lasts, and refuses it once the file can be read again.

  Not tested:
  - a lock that a mapping cannot get past, and with it the comparison that follows, against the file as its open handle then reports it;
  - a page that `ReadProcessMemory` cannot read;
  - that the size and times are taken before the read rather than after it;
  - the stored file's own size limit, which a system-wide file near 64 MB would need;
  - the SSH handler's part: storing only on Windows and only in `local_app_dir`, and logging once when there is none;
  - the log messages.

  The first three could not be brought about in a test. The check that the stored file is owner-only runs on Unix only, and so has not run.

  In manual runs, taking out any one of these failed exactly the matching tests:
  - the mapping, or the size limit before it;
  - the stored revocations of a held file, or telling a held file from a skipped one;
  - checking a held file itself, or checking it through a plain open, which the hold stops;
  - taking only revocations from the stored file;
  - telling a stored file that cannot be read from one never stored, or leaving the key unchecked then;
  - noticing a change, leaving the key unchecked then, the size among what is compared, or storing the size and times when there are no revocations;
  - skipping a file never read, rather than leaving the key unchecked;
  - storing revocations at all, leaving them as they are for a missing file, or not writing them again unchanged;
  - counting the right to change the times in the access-list rule.

  `cargo fmt`, clippy with the project's lints and the full Rust test suite pass on Windows. On Linux, clippy 1.95 passes with the project's lints, but the tests have not run there. The app itself was not started. Found by the pre-commit audit of the system-wide `known_hosts` change, which listed it as a known issue. The pre-commit review and audit of this change found eight more problems, all fixed above:
  - the crash on a page that cannot be read;
  - the unreadable stored file;
  - storing the revocations relative to the working directory;
  - a hold that lets an administrator's edits through;
  - times that someone short of an administrator could set back;
  - a store that would follow a roaming profile to another machine;
  - a stored file that could be a little larger than the limit it was read with;
  - a held file whose own access list went unchecked.
- The webview chose where the app writes files. It could set any absolute folder as the session log directory and any name through the filename format, and the session logger appends terminal output there. A compromised webview could therefore have output, which the remote host controls, appended to a file such as `x.bat` in a Startup folder. The application log directory and the SQLite database path had the same gap, the database path taking effect at the next start. Changes:
  - **Paths are chosen in backend dialogs.** **Browse…** next to each log directory, and **Browse…** and **Create New** for the SQLite file, now open a dialog from the backend (`logging_pick_directory`, `app_logging_pick_directory`, `db_pick_sqlite_path`). The backend words its title itself, in the operating system's language, as it does for the private-address prompt. It starts in the configured location or the default, never in one the webview names. Only one such dialog opens at a time.
  - **Saving accepts only three paths:** the default, the path already configured, or the user's choice from the last ten minutes. Any other path is refused, and the choice is forgotten after a successful save, so a choice the user never saved cannot be saved later by the webview.
  - **The fields are read-only.** A new **Use Default** button puts the default back.
  - **The webview may no longer open save dialogs** (`dialog:allow-save` is gone from its capabilities), since it no longer uses them.
  - **The log filename format** allows only letters, digits, spaces, `- _ . ( )` and the placeholders. It may not start with a dot or a space, and must end in `.log` or `.txt`. A format already in `config.json` that breaks these rules is replaced by the default when the app starts, with a warning in the app log. As a backstop, the logger also refuses a generated name with a `:`, which on Windows could name a drive (`C:x`) or a stream (`x:y`).
  - **Device names get a `_` in front.** A session named like a Windows device (`CON`, `NUL`, `COM1`, …) would have had its output written to that device rather than a file.
  - **Settings change in memory only after `config.json` is written**, for both logs, so a failed save no longer leaves the app with settings the file does not hold.

  A log directory or database path saved before this version is kept as it is, since nothing can tell whether the user chose it. If in doubt, check them in **Settings**. The webview can still open file dialogs of its own, which the SSH key pickers need, but a path from one of those is never accepted by these save commands. The language lookup the private-address prompt uses moved to `commands/system_language.rs`, so both dialogs share it. The pick lifetime, the format rules, the device names and the titles for every UI locale have unit tests. The dialogs themselves have not been clicked through in the running app. Pre-existing; found by the audit of the **Data Export / Import** change and the audit of this one.
- The app's own commands were not listed in an app manifest, so the capability file did not limit which webviews may call them: any webview the app opened could have called every one, from connecting a session to reading a saved credential. Tauri checks an app's own commands against the capabilities only when the app has a manifest. The app opens only its main window, with a strict CSP and no remote content, so nothing could use this today; a second window added later would have had every command without anyone granting it. The commands are now listed in `src-tauri/permissions/app-commands.toml`, as one permission, `app-commands`, which the capability file grants to the main window alone. Any other window or webview is refused, and so is a page that is not the app's own, as before. Nothing changes in the main window. A new command has to be added to that file as well as to `generate_handler!`, or the main window is refused it; a test in `lib.rs` resolves the manifest and capabilities the build compiles in, as Tauri does, and fails when the two lists differ, when a command is granted to anything but the main window's local pages, or when the manifest is gone. Checked once by hand against Tauri's mock runtime with the app's real configuration: the main window reached a command, and a second window was refused it. Four commands the frontend does not call (`session_get`, `db_export`, `highlight_profile_get`, `login_sequence_get`) are still registered and granted, as before. Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit audit of the Telnet dead-session fix.
- A stored command bar that held anything but buttons could keep the app from starting. The startup check of the settings in localStorage read each stored button's `id` before checking that the entry was an object, so a list holding `null` made the check fail part way. The list then kept the `null`, and the checks that come after it (terminal font, terminal colors, the update-check setting) did not run. The command bar reads every entry to draw it and failed on the `null`; nothing catches such a failure, so the window stays empty at every start until the stored settings are removed. The same went for a value stored in place of the list (`null`, a string, a number, an object), which the check left as it was. Every entry is now checked as a whole before anything is read from it, and anything stored in place of the list becomes an empty one. Adding a button and importing buttons use the same check, so there is one definition of a valid button instead of three. A stored button, and one added in the app, now keeps only its four own fields (id, name, command, color), as an imported one already did, so nothing else that sits in storage with it is carried along. A stored button is still not put through the import's hidden-character check, which is for buttons from someone else's file: a button made in the app may hold such a character and must not vanish at the next start. The app never writes such a list itself: it takes storage that was damaged or edited from outside. That the command bar fails to render on such a list was checked once by hand in a test; the empty window was not watched in a running app. Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit audit of the import direction fix.
- The pre-commit audit of that fix found seven more problems in the settings store, `src/stores/settingsStore.ts`. All were pre-existing and are fixed with it:
  - **Duplicating a button** ignored the 1000-button limit, and named the copy after the button with " (copy)" added, whatever the length. For a name of 26 characters or more the copy's name passed the 32-character limit, and the startup check dropped the copy at the next start without a word. A copy is no longer made when the bar is full, and the name is shortened first so that the copy's name fits, cut between characters and never through one. No part of the UI calls `duplicateCommandButton` today, so no button has been lost this way.
  - **On/off settings, the theme, the terminal font size and a language that is no text were not checked at startup.** A stored on/off setting that is no boolean counts as on wherever it is read as a condition, since any text does, `"false"` included: a damaged entry could switch on paste on right-click or copy on selection. A value that is no boolean now puts the setting on its careful side, the same side an import may move it to: the three confirmations (multi-line paste, quitting, closing a tab) and the update check on; paste on right-click, copy on selection and closing a tab on disconnect off. The two settings without such a side, opening a local shell at startup and closing toasts by themselves, get their default. For the multi-line paste confirmation the careful side is not the default, which is off. A theme other than dark, light or system becomes system, a font size outside 6–72 becomes 14, and a language that is no text becomes "follow the system". A UI scale that is no number becomes 100 %; it used to become 125 %, the largest. A new on/off setting does not compile until it is on the list the check goes through.
  - **A stored value could replace one of the store's actions.** The store keeps its settings and the functions that change them in one object, and everything in storage was laid over it. A value stored under a function's name replaced the function, so that setting could not be changed again until storage was cleared, and a value under any other name stayed in the store and was written back. Only values stored under the names of the store's own settings are read now.
  - **Two stored buttons with one id** were both kept. The command bar then drew two buttons under one key, and changing or removing either acted on the first. The startup check now keeps the first and drops the others, in either letter case, as the import does. Adding a button compares ids the same way.
  - **`updateCommandButton` took an `id` among its changes** if a caller passed one. Its type forbids that, but nothing checked it at run time, so one button could end up with another's id. The id now stays, and nothing but a button's four own fields is kept.
  - **`setUiScale` stored `NaN`,** which passed both of its range comparisons. A value that is no finite number is now ignored. That includes infinity, which used to become 125 %; neither the Settings dialog nor an import passes one.
  - **The hidden-character check for imported buttons** missed U+17B4 and U+17B5, the two Khmer inherent vowels, which show nothing. Both are now refused in a name and in a command. So is every other character Unicode defines as showing nothing (`Default_Ignorable_Code_Point`), which adds the code points set aside for such characters but not assigned yet, such as U+2065, found by the audit of this fix. Variation selectors are the exception, as before: a name may hold them, as emoji and East Asian and Mongolian text do, and a command may not.
  - **An imported button could have a blank label.** A name of nothing but spaces, or of nothing but the joiner that emoji sequences use, passed the check, and the button showed no label over a command that one click sends. Such a button is now refused; a name with spaces around what it shows is kept as it is. Found by the audit of the fixes above.

  None of the startup checks rewrites storage: a corrected value is written with the next change to any setting. A list with more than 1000 entries is still cut to the first 1000 before any entry is checked, which bounds the work, so a button after 1000 others or after 1000 entries that are no buttons is not kept. Both fixes are covered by 102 new tests in `src/stores/settingsStore.test.ts`, 56 of which fail against the code as it was; the others pin what already held, such as `addCommandButton` and `updateCommandButton` refusing a malformed button, which had no tests of their own. One test starts the store afresh from damaged storage: at startup the check runs while the store is being created, which the others, reading storage again later, do not show.
- **A mistyped `ssl_mode` in the config file connected to PostgreSQL as `prefer`,** which falls back to an unencrypted connection when the server offers no TLS, or when someone on the network path says it does not. Any value other than `disable` or `require` was read that way, so a hand-edited `required`, `Require` or `verify-full` asked for encryption and got the one mode that may go without. The Settings dialog only saves valid values, so this took a hand-edited file. An unknown value now loads as `require`, with a warning in the application log, and never as `prefer`. Whitespace around a value and its letter case are dropped first, so `Require` is `require`. A hand-edited `disable` or `prefer` is used as written, and a file without an `ssl_mode` still gets the default, `prefer`. Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit audit of the session log fix.
  - **What changes for a file that meant no encryption.** A `disabled` or `off` is unknown as well, so it now connects as `require` and fails against a server without TLS, where it used to connect. The app starts as it does for any failed PostgreSQL connection, and Settings shows **Require**, where the mode can be set.
  - `require` encrypts but does not check the server's certificate, and the app has no `verify-ca` or `verify-full`; a file that asks for one of them gets `require`, which is less than it asked for.
  - `connect_options` no longer has a catch-all that gives `prefer`, so a value that reaches it some other way gets `require` too. The "Connecting to PostgreSQL…" log line reports the mode in effect. The corrected value is written to the file with the next save of any setting the backend keeps.
  - An `ssl_mode` that is no string (`null`, a number), or one given twice, makes the file one that does not parse, as before: the app starts with the defaults, on SQLite, and keeps a copy of the file.
  - Covered by seven tests in `config.rs`, one of which checks that the warning quotes what the file said escaped.
- **What the config loader had to say at startup never reached the log.** The config is loaded before the application log is set up, since the log is set up from the config, and a log call made before then writes nothing, to the log file or to stdout. So none of these were ever written: that the config file does not parse and the defaults are used, and where its copy was kept; that the file cannot be read; that connection settings outside their range, or a session log filename format the app no longer accepts, were replaced by their defaults; and that the PostgreSQL password came back empty from the keychain or could not be read from it, with the keychain's own error. The warning about an unknown `ssl_mode` (above) would have gone the same way.
  - **How they are written now.** `load_config` returns its lines, each with its level, and startup logs them once the log exists, after "ShellStation starting". The keychain read moved out of `load_config` into `load_postgres_password`, which startup calls after that, so that it and the keychain code log as they always meant to.
  - **What comes from the config file goes into such a line escaped,** so that a line break in it cannot start a new line in the log, which could pass for an entry of its own. This matters for a file that does not parse: the error quotes an unknown `db_backend` as the file has it.
  - **The two startup log lines that name the PostgreSQL server** ("ShellStation starting" and "Connecting to PostgreSQL…") wrote the host, and the second the database name, as the config file holds them, line breaks included. Both are now written escaped, in quotes. So is the error in the line for a custom SQLite path that cannot be opened, which can quote that path.
  - "Loaded config from …" no longer repeats the backend, host and port, which "ShellStation starting" gives. The line for a password read from the keychain no longer gives the password's length; it was never written, and now would have been.
  - Covered by two new tests in `config.rs` (a damaged file's content cannot start a log line; settings out of range are named), and by checks added to four existing ones for the lines a missing, damaged or unreadable file and a refused filename format give. Logging the returned lines at startup, the keychain read and the two escaped log lines need a running app and have no test of their own. Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit review of the `ssl_mode` fix.
- **On Windows a session log was only as private as the log directory.** A log file, and a log directory the logger had to create, got their access list from the directory above. In a directory the user chose outside their profile, such as one under `C:\`, that list often lets every user of the machine read. They could then read what a session showed, passwords echoed by a device included. Both now get an access list of their own as part of the create, as they get their mode on Unix: full control for the user the app runs as, and nothing inherited from the directory above. Missing parents of the log directory are created the same way. It is the list `known_hosts` already got from `icacls`, but with no moment in between. A network share is the exception, where both are created as before.
  - **How.** The standard library cannot hand a security descriptor to a create, so on Windows `private_fs.rs` now calls `CreateFileW` and `CreateDirectoryW` itself, in the new `private_fs/windows.rs`: a few lines of FFI, as in `ssh/windows_security.rs`, rather than the `windows-sys` crate. The user's SID is read from the process's own token. Otherwise the creates do what the standard library's did: the same access, sharing and flags, a taken name refused and a link there not followed, and a path too long for a plain create passed in the `\\?\` form.
  - **On a network share nothing changes.** The list names the user by SID, which a server knows only when the account belongs to a domain that both machines are in. A server reached with another account, such as one of its own with the same name and password, would hold the list against the user: a log could not be opened once written, and a log directory the logger created could not be written into, so sessions would go unlogged. What is created on a share is therefore created the plain way, as before, and gets its access list from the directory above, as the share has it (see **Known issues**). A share is told by the directory that a file or directory is created in, as the system resolves it: one given by a share's name, one on a drive letter mapped to a share and one reached through a link that leads to a share all count. A directory that cannot be resolved counts as local, so that nothing is left open for not knowing. When the session log directory is on a share, the application log says so for each session, at `info`.
  - **What else it changes.** Everything created through `private_fs` gets such a list, not only session logs. That is `config.json`, `known_hosts` and the stored revocations of a system-wide `known_hosts`, each on every save, since a save replaces the file with a new one; and the config directory, its `logs` directory, `.ssh` and the directory of the stored revocations, when the app has to create them. All of these are under the user's profile by default, where the difference is that SYSTEM and the administrators are no longer on the list. A profile kept on a share is a share here too, and nothing changes in it. An administrator can still take ownership of a file. `known_hosts` is still restricted with `icacls` after each save, which now changes nothing for the file.
  - **What is unchanged.** A directory that is already there keeps its access list: the log directory of an existing install, or one the user made. Others who can list it still see a log's name and time, but cannot open a log created from now on. A log from before keeps its list too. The application log file is opened by the logging library and gets its list from its directory, as before. A file system without access lists, such as FAT or exFAT, ignores the list. When the app runs elevated, the Administrators group owns what it creates, as before; the list still names the user alone.
  - Covered by six new tests in `private_fs/windows.rs` (the SID read from the token; a file that another program creates in such a directory inherits the user's entry and no other; a directory tree and a log at a path of more than 300 characters; the `\\?\` form for long drive and share paths, from the first length that needs it; a share told from a local directory by its resolved name; a file in the way of a directory refused) and a new one in `session_logger.rs` (a log in a directory that was already there). The append test in `private_fs.rs` now also checks that a log's handle cannot cut the file short on Windows. The tests of `private_fs.rs` and the logger's test for a new log and its directory, which checked modes on Unix only, now check the access list on Windows as well, read back through the file's handle and compared whole, so an inherited entry fails them. Creating the file without the list makes six tests fail, creating the directory without it four, leaving the list open to inheriting seven, and taking every directory for a share nine. In a directory that lets every user of the machine read, `icacls` showed the user's entry alone on a log, and on a log directory the logger created with a parent, where a file created the plain way showed the inherited five. No test creates anything on a share, as a test has none to use, and the line the application log gets there has none either. Tried by hand against the WSL file system, which Windows reaches as a share: by its name and through a mapped drive letter, a log directory and a log in it were created the plain way, written and read back, and a taken name was refused. A share on a Windows or Samba server was not tried, nor one reached with another account. The full Rust suite passes on Windows, and clippy and rustfmt are clean. The two source files were type-checked for Linux on their own, since the whole crate does not cross-check from Windows; the Unix code is unchanged but for its tests' shared assertion, and has not run. A file system without access lists and an elevated process were not tried, and the app itself was not started. Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit audit of the session log fix.
- **A session whose log could not be created went on without one, and only the application log said so.** The session itself works as usual, so a user who counts on the log found out only when looking for the file. Someone able to write to the log directory can bring this about by taking every name a log could get (see the session log entry above). The session's tab now shows a toast: _Session "name" is not being logged: its log file could not be created. The application log has the details._ (It now ends _could not be created or written to_: see the entry on the lock that every session's output waited on, below.) It is a warning that stays until it is dismissed, whatever the auto-dismiss setting says, so that it is not missed while typing or with toasts set to close after a second. Translated into all 14 UI languages.
  - **When.** With the session's first output, which is when the log is created. The backend holds a session's output back until its tab is listening, so the notice does not arrive before the tab can show it. A session that never prints anything gets no notice, and has nothing to log.
  - **A log that could not be prepared at connect is reported too.** When the log directory cannot be created, or the filename is refused, the session was connected without a log and with one line in the application log. It now gets the same toast, at its first output.
  - **How.** The logger calls a notifier once when it gives a log up, and startup has that send the event `terminal-log-failed-<id>` to the window. The logger calls it under the lock that the output of every session waits for, or, since each log has a writer of its own (see below), from that writer's thread or from the reader of a session that waits for its log, so the event is sent from a task of its own. The event carries nothing: the reason, which can name paths, stays in the application log. `Terminal.tsx` listens for it next to the session's output and exit events, and the new `src/sessionLogNotice.ts` shows the toast with the tab's title.
  - **The name in the toast.** A tab's title is a session name, which can be 255 characters long and, typed in or coming from a shared database, can hold anything. The toast quotes at most 64 characters of it, with `…` where it was cut, and leaves out the characters the import already drops from a name: control characters, the bidi embedding, override and isolate controls, line and paragraph separators, and the invisible characters that make a name look blank or like another. So a name can neither reorder the warning nor push it out of view. Directional marks and joiners stay. A name can still hold a quotation mark and words of its own, as it can in its tab; the warning always follows it. Tab titles themselves are shown as before.
  - **A long word in a toast no longer pushes its close button out of the window.** A toast's text did not wrap inside a word, so a message with a long word and no spaces, such as a session name, grew past the toast's width and took the close button with it. That mattered little while toasts closed by themselves, and matters for one that only goes when closed. Toast text now wraps anywhere. This goes for every toast.
  - **How many.** The same toast is shown once however often it is asked for, so a session reconnected under the same name does not stack them. For the same reason a second tab with the same name, or with a name that reads the same once cut and cleaned, gets no toast of its own while the first is still up. Sessions with different names get one each: a log directory that is gone gives a toast for every session opened until it is back.
  - **What is unchanged.** The cause is not dealt with: the session still goes on without a log. A log that can no longer be written to later on, such as on a full disk, was still reported in the application log only after this fix; a later entry has it given up, with this toast. Should a tab not report itself as listening within the time the backend waits for it (5 s, 10 s for a local shell), the output starts anyway and the notice can be lost as that output is.
  - Covered by six new tests in `session_logger.rs` (a logged session and one with logging off not reported, a log that cannot be prepared reported at the first output and not before, none for a session closed before any output, a log given up with no notifier set, nothing kept for a log once it is reported, and the event's name) and checks added to the two tests for a log given up, that it is reported once and for that session alone; by `src/sessionLogNotice.test.ts` (9 tests: the event's name, the English text with the session's name, a name with `<` and `&` shown as it is, a name with such characters in the toast, every character left out, listed as the import's sanitizer lists them, with those kept on either side of each range, the cut at 64 characters, one toast for a reconnected session, one per session name, and none for a tab that is gone); and by two tests in `Terminal.test.tsx` (the toast on the event, and none after unmount), whose listener tests now expect three listeners. Sending the event from the backend to the window needs a running app and has no automated test, and the wrapping of toast text has none either, as the test environment lays nothing out; the app itself was not started. Listed under **Known issues** until now; found by the pre-commit audit of the session log fix, the name and the wrapping by the audit of this change.
- **A session log kept a DEL byte and the C1 control characters (U+0080–U+009F) a remote host sent,** while escape sequences that start with ESC were removed. A terminal that acts on C1 controls reads U+009B as `ESC [`, so such a log, shown there with `cat`, could move the cursor, clear the screen or change what is displayed. A log now holds no control character but CR, LF and tab, whatever the host sends and wherever a read splits it. The review and audit of this fix found more that a log kept and should not; it is fixed here as well, as the points below say.
  - **How.** The stripper went through the output byte by byte. A C1 control is two bytes in UTF-8, and its second byte is one that ordinary characters end in as well (U+009B is C2 9B, `ě` is C4 9B), so it cannot be told from text one byte at a time. The output is now read as UTF-8 first, as the terminal reads it, and stripped character by character. A character that a read ends in the middle of is finished by the next read.
  - **C1 controls are taken as the terminal takes them,** each as ESC and the character after it. U+009B opens a CSI sequence, and U+009D, U+0090, U+0098, U+009E and U+009F open a control string (OSC, DCS, SOS, PM, APC); the sequence goes with them, so no `31m` is left behind. U+009C ends a string, as `ESC \` does. U+0084 and U+0085 (IND, NEL) are written as a line feed (see below). Any C1 control ends a sequence that is under way. The others are dropped, and so is DEL; a sequence that DEL stands in goes on past it.
  - **Bytes that are not UTF-8 are written as U+FFFD,** the replacement character the terminal shows for them. They used to go into the log as they came. That covers a C1 control sent as a single byte (0x9B), which a terminal that does not read UTF-8 acts on, and one written longer than UTF-8 allows. It also means that a device that sends another encoding, such as Latin-1, no longer has its letters beyond ASCII in the log as it sent them, where a viewer set to that encoding could read them: the log shows what the terminal showed. What the stripper hands to the file is now valid UTF-8 throughout; only a write that fails can leave part of a character in the file (see below).
  - **Escape sequences that left their text in the log are removed whole.** An escape with a character between ESC and its last one, or one that ends in a digit or one of `: ; < = > ?`, left those characters behind: `(B` for `ESC ( B`, which many programs send to reset the character set, `7` and `8` for saving and restoring the cursor, `=` and `>` for the keypad modes. The strings DCS, SOS, PM and APC (`ESC P`, `ESC X`, `ESC ^`, `ESC _`) left their text. Both are now read to their end, a string as an OSC sequence is: up to `ESC \`, U+009C or BEL, or a line break or 4096 characters when it has no end. An escape other than `ESC \` that ends a string is read as the escape it is; `ESC ] … ESC [ 31 m` left `31m`. CAN and SUB abort a sequence, as they do in the terminal, where the stripper went on to the sequence's end and took text with it.
  - **What the terminal moves to the next line for is a line feed in the log:** VT, FF, and IND and NEL as `ESC D`, `ESC E`, U+0084 and U+0085. They were dropped, which joined two lines of the session into one; U+0084 and U+0085 went into the log as they came. A control string takes VT and FF with it, as it takes the rest of its text.
  - **A carriage return stays only before a line feed.** In a terminal a CR puts the cursor back at the start of the line, and what follows is written over what the line held, so a host could have a log shown with `cat` display something other than what it holds. A CR that an LF follows is the line break CR LF and is written as such, also with an escape sequence in between. Several CRs in a row count as one, so CR CR LF, which some devices send, is CR LF in the log. A CR before anything else is written as a line feed, so what followed it stands on a line of its own; at the start of a line, where there is nothing to write over, it is dropped. Output that redraws a line, such as a progress bar or a prompt that is edited, therefore takes a line per update in the log, where it used to show as the last state when printed and as one long line in an editor. A pager that clears its prompt with spaces leaves a line of spaces. This was so before the fix and was not part of the issue; it is changed here by decision.
  - **The characters that most often reorder or hide text are dropped,** although they are no control characters: the ones the import drops from a name (the bidi embedding, override and isolate controls, the line and paragraph separators, zero-width space, BOM, soft hyphen, word joiner and invisible operators, interlinear annotation marks and the standalone Hangul fillers), and the tag characters (U+E0000–U+E007F), which can spell out text that is not shown. With an override a host could have a log show a file name or a command as another. Directional marks and joiners stay, as they do in a name: right-to-left and Indic text and emoji sequences need them. A flag emoji made of tag characters loses them. The logger uses the import's own list (`is_display_spoofing_char`). Also so before the fix, and changed here by decision.
  - **What else changes.** A backspace removes a whole character (one code point) from the log; it removed one byte, which left a broken character behind after a letter beyond ASCII. It no longer removes a line break, nor anything after a CR, as the terminal's cursor stops at the start of a line. A control character right after ESC is taken as it is anywhere: a line break there is kept, and a second ESC starts a sequence. A sequence that never ends gives way after 4096 characters rather than bytes, and the application log is told so once for a session, not each time, which a host could repeat at will. After a write to a log that failed, as on a full disk, the next one starts with a line feed: part of the failed one may be in the file, ending in a CR without its LF, which what comes next would be written over, or in the middle of a character. A later entry has the first write that fails end the log, so that nothing comes next and the line feed is gone. If no write follows, the file ends there. A failed write and a failed flush give one warning between them now, not one each.
  - **What is unchanged.** A log holds nothing that a terminal that reads UTF-8 acts on but line breaks and tabs, so showing it there cannot move the cursor back or up, clear the screen or set the title. What it shows can still mislead: a host can always write text that looks like a prompt or like other output, and the list of dropped characters is fixed and not complete. A log is text from a remote host and stays that: stripping it does not make it safe to hand to something that reads markup or runs what it is given. Directional marks, joiners, variation selectors, the combining grapheme joiner and a few fillers that scripts need stay, as they do in a name. A terminal set to another encoding, as a Windows console on a legacy code page is, takes some bytes of ordinary characters for C1 controls (`ě` holds 0x9B). A host can choose such characters, and the stripper cannot take them out without taking text out, so a log shown there can still act on the terminal: show a log in a terminal that reads UTF-8 (on Windows, after `chcp 65001`), or open it in an editor. A backspace still removes only what the same output of the session added, since what an earlier one added is in the file already. A character that the session ends in the middle of, and a CR that nothing follows, are not written. BEL ends a DCS, SOS, PM or APC string here, where a terminal reads it as part of the string; what follows is then kept in the log. A sequence without an end still takes up to 4096 characters with it, which the terminal does not show either; sequences that each end in the start of the next count as one for this, until the limit is reached and the count starts again. The limit keeps a sequence that was cut short from taking the rest of a session with it. It is no limit on what a host can keep out of a log: whatever a host puts inside escape sequences, with an end or without, is not in the log, as it is not on the screen. A line break ends a string without an end, but not a CSI sequence, which goes on after it as it does in the terminal. Logs written before keep what they hold.
