  - Covered by 19 new tests in `session_logger.rs`: DEL dropped, also after ESC and inside a sequence; each of the 32 C1 controls; a C1 CSI and OSC with their sequences, and each way an OSC ends; text beyond ASCII kept; characters and C1 controls split at every byte; bytes that are not UTF-8; backspace after a letter beyond ASCII, at a line break, after a CR, on a line it has emptied and across two outputs; the 4096 limit counted in characters, the same when the output is split at every byte, kept up across sequences that each end in the start of the next, and its report marked as made once (the line in the application log itself has no test); escapes with a character in between or ending in a digit or sign; each of the four other strings, opened and ended each way; CAN and SUB in each kind of sequence; the six forms of a move to the next line, and VT and FF inside a CSI sequence; a CR before text, before an LF, at the start of a line, at the end and across outputs; the characters that reorder or hide text, also inside a sequence and after ESC, and those that stay; a write that fails between a CR and its LF, in the middle of a character, and twice in a row, against a log that fails once when it is full; and the bytes of a log file. One test splits seven inputs in two at every byte and compares each log with the one the unsplit input gives. It does the same for 2000 inputs, each put together from 40 pieces picked out of 42 by a fixed pseudo-random sequence and cut into reads of up to four bytes by the same sequence; no input in it holds a backspace, which reaches back only within one read. Another test puts backspaces among such pieces and checks only what the log may hold. Another goes through every pair of bytes and every pair of control characters after 16 starts, which leave the stripper in each of its states or in the middle of a character. All three check that what would be written holds no control character but CR, LF and tab, no CR without an LF after it and none of the dropped characters. The 12 tests the stripper already had pass with the same expected text. The full Rust suite passes on Windows, and clippy and rustfmt are clean; the change has nothing platform-specific in it, but was not run elsewhere, and the app itself was not started. Listed under **Known issues** until now; found by the pre-commit audit of the application log directory change. The escapes that left their text were found while fixing it, and CAN and SUB, the joined lines, the carriage return, the characters that reorder or hide text, the repeated warning and the failed write by the pre-commit review and audit of this change.
- **The output of every session waited on one lock while a session log was prepared, created, written, flushed or closed.** A log directory that was slow, such as one on a network share that did not answer, therefore held up the output of all sessions for as long as a write took, those without a log included. A connect waited as well, since it prepares its log under the same lock, and so did closing a tab. The SSH and Telnet readers are tasks of the async runtime, so each one that waited there also kept one of the runtime's threads from everything else. Each log is now written by a thread of its own, and nothing is done to the file system under the lock.
  - **How.** Under the lock, a session's output is stripped as before and handed to the log's writer, which takes no file. The writer makes sure the log directory is there when the session connects, creates the file on the session's first output, and writes: the outputs that are waiting go into one write together, as far as they fit in 64 KiB, and a single output whatever its size. Closing a tab closes its log without waiting; the writer writes what is left, closes the file and ends.
  - **A session waits for its own log, and for no other.** Text that waits for a writer is kept in memory, so it has a limit: 1 MiB for a log, what the writer is at included. A session whose log is further behind than that waits for the writer before it goes on, without the lock, until the log is no more than 1 MiB behind. A slow log directory therefore still slows a session that puts out megabytes, as it did, and nothing is lost; a session that puts out less never waits, and neither does any other session. An SSH or Telnet reader waits on a blocking thread, so the runtime's own threads stay free. What can wait for one log is 1 MiB of text and one output of the session, in buffers that can be up to twice as large as the text; outputs are joined as they come, so that a host that sends a byte at a time does not have each byte take a buffer of its own. While a session waits, its output stands still, as it did during a slow write. A Telnet session sends no keys and no change of window size in that time either, since one task of it reads and sends, and an SSH session no change of window size; keys typed into an SSH session or a local shell are sent. A login sequence waits for the log as the session does, and the wait counts against the time its step has. A session held to the pace of a slow log is not told so: it shows in the output.
  - **A log that takes nothing is given up.** When nothing at all is written for 5 s while a session waits for its log, the log is given up: the application log says so, the session's tab shows the toast for a session that is not logged, once, and the session goes on. A log directory that stops answering thus holds up a session logged there once, for 5 s, and only one with more than 1 MiB of output that the log has not taken. The writer still writes what was waiting, should the directory take it again, and the log ends there, at the end of an output. A write that is slow but gets somewhere is not taken for this: one write in 5 s is enough, so a directory that takes writes that slowly holds its session to that pace for as long as it does. The logs of other sessions are not given up with it; each waits for itself. Closing a tab ends its session's wait at once. A wait whose reader was stopped first, as at a quit, can go on for up to those 5 s and then report a tab that is gone, which the window ignores.
  - **The toast says so.** It read _its log file could not be created_, which is not what happened to a log that is given up later. It now reads: _Session "name" is not being logged: its log file could not be created or written to. The application log has the details._ Changed in all 14 UI languages. The event is the same (`terminal-log-failed-<id>`); for a log given up later it is sent then, not with the first output.
  - **Quitting waits for the logs.** A writer is a thread that the exit stops wherever it is, so the app's own quit now closes every log and waits until the writers have written what they had. That comes after the sessions have closed, and within the 4 s the quit has in all: after an SSH close that took its 3 s, the logs have the second that is left. Most are written by then, since a log is closed as soon as its session is. What a log directory has not taken when the 4 s are up is lost.
  - **At most 512 writers run at a time.** A writer stuck in a write that does not return stays, with the text it has, until the write returns: its session can be closed, but the thread cannot be stopped. Sessions opened one after the other against a log directory that never answers could otherwise leave any number of them behind. No more than 300 sessions can be open at a time, so the limit is reached only by writers that have outlived their sessions. A session that connects then gets no log, and its tab says so with its first output. With every one of them stuck and holding all that may wait for a log, that is somewhat over half a gigabyte of text, in buffers of up to twice that.
  - **A writer that panics gives its log up.** Nothing would take the session's text any more, so the writer says on its way out that the session is no longer logged, as for any log that is given up.
  - **What else changes.** A log directory that cannot be created is found out by the writer and no longer at connect: the application log has `Failed to prepare session log` for it where it had `Failed to open session log`, which a filename that is refused and the limit on writers still give, and the toast comes with the first output as before. A filename that is refused, which startup and saving the settings both prevent, no longer has the log directory created first. The application log's lines for a log that is created or closed are written when the writer has done it, shortly after the output or the close. A log that can no longer be written to still gave a warning for each write that fails after this fix (a later entry has it given up at the first), and such a write now loses up to 64 KiB of outputs, or the one output if it is larger, where it lost one output. When the logger's lock is poisoned by a panic elsewhere, the readers now hand their output to the log all the same, where they skipped it; closing the log at the end of a session still skips it.
  - **What is unchanged.** What a log holds, how its file and its directory are created, and, for a log that cannot be created, when its session is told: with its first output. The lock is still there, and every session's output still passes it: what is done under it is stripping the output, which touches no file. A log in a directory that does not answer is not written for as long as that lasts, as before. What a writer holds is lost when the process ends without the app's own quit: on a crash, or a quit from outside the app (see **Known issues** for those quits); an update install was among them until it got an entry of its own, above. Before, every output was in the file when the reader went on, so only a write under way could be cut short; now the text handed over and not yet written, up to 1 MiB and an output, can be lost with it. Creating the writer's thread at connect is still under the lock; it takes no file. A log still had no size limit after this fix; the next entry gives it one.
  - Covered by 14 new tests in `session_logger.rs`. In nine of them a write takes for as long as the test holds a lock that only the test build has. One has output taken meanwhile, for the session with the slow log and for another that connects, puts out text and closes, and finds nothing missing from either log afterwards. One puts a log more than 1 MiB behind and checks that its session waits while another session's output is taken, that the wait ends when the writes go through, and that the log goes on whole. One does the same with nothing written for the timeout, cut to 100 ms: the log is given up, reported once, the other session's log left alone, and the file then holds exactly what was waiting. One closes the log while its session waits, which ends the wait without a report. One hands on more without waiting, which gives the log up. Two go through what the readers call: a reader on a thread waits without the shared lock while another session's output is taken, and a reader that is a task waits, until its log is given up after a timeout cut to 300 ms, while a runtime with a single thread goes on with other work. One closes every log and checks that the wait for the writers does not return while a write is under way, and does once it is through, with a log that never had any output left without a file. The others, the third of which holds a write up as well: a wait that is told of a little written every 250 ms goes on past a timeout of 1.5 s and gives the log up only when nothing follows, with the test in the writer's place; outputs that wait are taken for a write together up to 64 KiB, in their order, a larger one alone, and counted as waiting until they are written; 400 outputs of more than six writes' worth, with characters of two bytes, are joined to seven buffers while a write is held up, none larger than a write, and are in the file whole afterwards, with nothing left waiting; a writer that ends in a panic, as if in the middle of a write, reports its log once, drops what waited and no longer counts as running; a log directory that cannot be created is reported with the first output and not before; and a session that connects while 512 writers run gets no log and says so, and the next one, with one writer fewer, gets its log. The ten earlier tests of the logger that read a file wait for the writer where they read what it wrote, and pass with the same expected contents; the four for a filename that is refused now expect no log directory. Run eight times over, the logger's tests passed each time. The full Rust suite passes on Windows, and clippy with the pedantic lints and rustfmt are clean. The change has nothing platform-specific in it, but was not run on Linux or macOS. A log directory on a real share, slow or gone, was not tried, and the app itself was not started: that the window gets the event for a log given up later, that an SSH or Telnet session goes on after its wait, and that quitting waits for the logs, have no automated test beyond the logger's own. A write that fails while a session waits for its log has no test either. Listed under **Known issues** until now; found by the pre-commit audit of the DEL and C1 fix. The wait for the log, the 1 MiB limit, the writes of 64 KiB and the writer that panics come from the audit of this change.
- **A session log had no size limit and was not rotated, although `docs/DESIGN.md` §5.5 listed rotation.** A log holds what the remote host sends and has no end of its own, so a host could fill the volume the log directory is on with a session's output, at the pace the log took it. Writes then failed, for that log and for whatever else writes there. A log file now has a largest size, and a session's log a largest number of files.
  - **How.** A file that is full is closed, and the log goes on in a new one, named after the first with `.part0002`, `.part0003`, … before the extension: `router.log`, then `router.part0002.log`. The numbers only go up, so a name is not used again once its file is deleted, and the names sort in the order the files were written up to `.part9999`; the numbers after it are longer and sort before it. The new file is created as the first one is: by a call that fails when anything holds the name, a symbolic link included, and for its owner alone. A name that is taken is passed over for the next number. With 1000 names in a row taken, or a file that cannot be created for another reason, the log is given up, and the session's tab shows the toast for a session that is not logged. A file is filled up to its limit and no further. What is written to it ends neither inside a character nor between a CR and its LF, since a file that ended in a CR would have whatever a terminal shows next written over its last line; a write that fails part of the way can still leave a file ending there, as it could before. The size is counted from the bytes the file took, those of a write that then failed included, and not asked of the file system. The next file is created only when there is text for it, so a file that is exactly full is not followed by an empty one.
  - **The oldest files are deleted.** Once a log has more files than it may keep, the oldest go. One session's log therefore takes no more than the two limits multiplied, or up to twice that with files that cannot be deleted (see below), and holds the session's latest output: of a long session, the start is gone. Only files that the log's own writer created are deleted, by the names it gave them, so neither another session's log nor a file someone else put at a name like these is touched. As with the create, that protects the name and not the log directory: someone able to replace that directory, or one above it, with a link decides where a name leads.
  - **A file that cannot be deleted** stays, and is tried again when the next file is full: on Windows a file that another program holds open is such a file, and may be free by then. A file that is gone already counts as deleted. When more than twice the number of files kept would be left, the log is given up, with the toast: a log whose oldest files do not go has no limit, and with such files it can take up to twice the two limits multiplied. The application log has a warning for a file the first time it cannot be deleted, and not for each time it is tried again, since a host that puts out text fast enough fills a file many times a second. Someone able to write to the log directory can end a log this way, by putting something that cannot be deleted where its files were, as they could before by taking every name a log could get.
  - **The settings.** **Settings → Session Logging** has two new fields, **Maximum log file size (MiB)** and **Log files kept per session**: 10 MiB and 10 files by default, so 100 MiB for one session's log, within 1–256 MiB and 1–50 files, so 12.5 GiB at the most. Neither limit can be switched off, and the ranges end there so that the webview cannot raise a log's limit to more than a volume is likely to have free. The webview saves these settings, so `logging_save_config` accepts only values within the ranges, and values outside them in the config file (`logging.max_size_mb`, `logging.max_files`) are replaced by the defaults at startup, with a warning. A value there that is no whole number (`-1`, `1.5`, `"10"`) makes the whole file unreadable, as any other number in it does: the file is set aside as `config.json.bad` and the defaults are used for everything. Within the ranges the webview can set either limit, down to one file of 1 MiB, which keeps only the last of a session's output; it could always switch session logging off, which keeps none. A config file from an earlier version gets the defaults. The two fields keep what is typed until they lose focus or the settings are saved, and then show the whole number within the range that is saved. A log keeps the limits that were set when its session connected; saved settings are for the sessions that connect after. The labels and hints are translated into all 14 UI languages.
  - **What changes for a log that was large before.** A session that puts out more than 100 MiB used to have all of it in one file. With the defaults it now has its last 90 to 100 MiB, in ten files. Whoever needs more raises the limits, up to 50 files of 256 MiB; a session that needs more than those 12.5 GiB kept is not served by this.
  - **What is unchanged.** What a log holds, how its first file is named and created, and how a log is handed to its writer: creating and deleting files is done by the log's writer thread, never under the lock all sessions share. The application log says at `info` when a log goes on in a new file and when a file is deleted. The limits are for each log by itself; nothing limits the log directory (see **Known issues**).
  - Covered by 15 new tests in `session_logger.rs` (text spread over files at the byte the limit gives, with each output in a write of its own and with outputs joined while the writer was held up; no empty file after one that is exactly full; the oldest files deleted while another session's log and a file at a name the log would have taken stay; one file kept; a character of two bytes and a CR LF at the limit; a limit smaller than a character; the room in a file after a failed write and after writes; bytes of a failed write counted; a file, a directory and a symbolic link at the next name passed over and not deleted; every name taken; a file that cannot be deleted tried again; too many of them; limits kept from connect; the limits the config gives) and 5 in `config.rs` (a config without the limits, limits outside their ranges, a limit of the wrong type, the ranges, and the limits being set only when all that are given are in range, which is what `logging_save_config` calls). The full Rust suite passes on Windows, and clippy, rustfmt, `tsc`, eslint, prettier and the frontend tests are clean. The command `logging_save_config` itself and the two fields in the Settings dialog have no test, nor has a write that fails at a file's limit or the warning being given once, nothing was built or run on Linux or macOS, the test for a symbolic link at the next name skipped itself here, as this Windows account may not create links, deleting a file that another program holds open was not tried, the translations were not checked by native speakers, and the app itself was not started.
- **A session log that could no longer be written to filled the application log with warnings, and its session's tab was not told.** On a full disk, or on a share that went away, every write to the log failed, and each failure put a warning into the application log: up to one for each output of the session, for as long as the session lasted. The tab showed nothing, so the session looked logged while its log had stopped. A log is now given up at the first write that fails.
  - **What happens.** The application log gets one warning with the reason (`Failed to write session log; this session is no longer logged`), the session's tab shows the toast for a session that is not logged, once, and the session goes on without a log. The toast's text already covered this case (_could not be created or written to_), and the event is the same (`terminal-log-failed-<id>`), sent when the write fails. A session that was waiting for its log, because the log was more than 1 MiB behind, goes on at once.
  - **No write is tried again.** That goes also when the disk has room again later, or the share is back: the log ends at the failed write, and a reconnect begins a new one. A log that went on would have a gap that only the application log told of, under a toast that said the session was not logged. It is also how every other log that is given up is treated: one whose file cannot be created, one that cannot go on in a new file, and one that takes nothing for 5 s.
  - **What is lost.** What the file took before stays. The output that was in the failed write, up to 64 KiB or the one output, is lost as before, and what waited behind it, up to 1 MiB, is now dropped where it was tried write by write. Part of the failed write may be in the file, which can then end in a CR without its LF or in part of a character, as it could before.
  - **What goes with it.** A write after a failed one used to start with a line feed, so that nothing followed a CR without its LF or part of a character. No write follows a failed one any more, so that line feed is gone, with the byte of room a file kept for it.
  - **What is unchanged.** A failed write is not told from one that might work again: any error ends the log. A share that does not answer at all, where a write neither fails nor returns, is still the case of the log that takes nothing for 5 s; should the write fail after those 5 s, the application log has a second warning for it, once, and the tab no second toast. Nothing limits the log directory as a whole (see **Known issues**), so a full volume still ends the logs written there, each with its toast.
  - Covered by six tests in `session_logger.rs`, in five of which writes to the log files fail for as long as the test says, by a switch that only the test build has: a log given up at a write that fails, reported once and for that session alone, with no write tried once writes would go through again, what the file held left in place and another session's log going on; a first write that fails, which leaves the empty file; 256 KiB waiting behind a write that fails, dropped without another write; a session waiting for its log, which goes on when the write fails and not after the stall timeout; and a write that fails in a log's second file, which leaves both files as they were and starts no third. The sixth, which replaces the test for the line feed, checks what a failed write leaves behind, cut between a CR and its LF and in the middle of a character. The tests for the line feed's byte of room and for counting the bytes of a failed write are gone with what they tested. That the application log gets one warning has no test of its own, a full disk and a share that goes away were not tried, the switch stands in for the file's own error, and the app itself was not started: that the window gets the event has no automated test beyond the logger's own. Listed under **Known issues** until now.
- **A stack of toasts taller than the window ran off its lower edge,** where the newest toasts could not be read or closed until older ones were closed, and nothing limited how many toasts there were. Toasts that stay until closed made this likelier: a log directory that is gone gives one for every session opened.
  - **The stack scrolls.** It is never taller than the window: its toasts are in lists that get a scroll bar instead, so every toast can be read and closed.
  - **Toasts that stay until closed are a group of their own,** above the others, whatever came when. The group takes up to half the window's height and keeps it however many other toasts there are; with no other toasts it has the whole height. Its list is never scrolled for the user: a new toast goes to its end, and one already in view stays in view. The other toasts get the rest of the height, and their list scrolls to a new toast; one that closes leaves the list where the user has it. A pile of ordinary toasts, such as an import's warnings, therefore cannot push a toast that stays out of sight.
  - **Close all.** A group with more than one toast has a **Close all (n)** button above its list, which does not scroll with it and closes the toasts of that group alone. Clearing an import's warnings leaves a toast that stays where it is, and a log directory that is gone no longer takes a click for every session. The notice that the private-address check is off is closed by its own button only: the button of its group, which is there for a pile of notices of another kind, neither counts nor closes it. Translated into all 14 UI languages.
  - **A limit.** At most 100 toasts are up at a time, not counting those that stay until closed whatever the settings say. One more takes the place of the oldest of them. An import shows a toast for each of its warnings, so a file with a warning for every entry could put up thousands, each with a timer of its own.
  - **What the stack takes of the window.** The first toast is 16px from the window's top and right edges, as before, or under the button of its group where there is one, and the two groups are 24px apart. The stack itself now reaches to those edges and keeps the distance by padding: a list that scrolls cuts off what leaves it, and the toasts' shadows need the room. So that this room takes nothing from what is under it, such as the tab bar or a terminal's scroll bar, only the toasts and the buttons take the pointer; the gaps between toasts used to take it as well. A list that is too tall for its room is the exception, since its scroll bar could not be used otherwise: it takes the pointer over its whole area, up to 452px wide and as high as the group, which is 32px wider than what a stack that tall took before. The toasts, the buttons and a list that scrolls lie over the tab bar, which drags the window, and are now marked as no part of that area.
  - **What is left.** An import with more than 100 warnings shows the last 100 of them, and the toast with its result, which comes before them, is gone as well. Toasts that stay until closed are not limited and never make room: each says something that must not be missed, and each is shown once, so there are as many as there are things to say, one for every session name that is not logged. Once they fill half the window, a new one is below what is in view, and only the scroll bar and the count on the button show that it came. The two **Close all** buttons differ only in their place and their count, for a screen reader as well. Closing a toast from the keyboard, or a group, leaves the focus on the page as a whole, as closing a single toast always did. That toasts lay above dialogs has an entry of its own below.
  - Covered by six new tests in `src/stores/toastStore.test.ts` (the limit dropping the oldest, a toast that stays neither counted nor dropped, any number of those kept, ids that count up, which the scrolling relies on, closing the toasts that stay or the others, never both, and a toast that is closed alone left by both) and seven in `src/components/ToastContainer.test.tsx` (the toasts that stay above the others whatever came when; a button only for a group with more than one toast; a button closing its own group alone, with its real English text and outside the list; a toast that is closed alone neither counted nor closed by the button, and closed by its own; the list scrolled to a new toast, and not when one goes; no list scrolled for a toast that stays, new or asked for again; a list taking the pointer only while it is too tall, measured when the toasts change and when the window does). `src/privateIpCheckNotice.test.ts` now checks that its notice is closed alone, and `src/sessionLogNotice.test.ts` that its notice is not. `tsc`, eslint, prettier and the frontend tests are clean. The test environment lays nothing out, so the layout was looked at in Chrome, on a static page with the app's stylesheet, 12 toasts that stay and 30 others in a window 826px high: the stack was as tall as the window, the upper group took half of it, both lists scrolled by wheel and by dragging the scroll bar, the buttons stayed in place, and clicks beside a button and beside a single toast reached a button placed under the stack. A list that lets the pointer through could not be scrolled by its scroll bar there, which is why one that is too tall takes it. The app itself was not started, nothing was tried in WebView2 or WebKit, the lines for the area that drags the window have no test and Chrome does not apply them, and the translations were not checked by native speakers. Listed under **Known issues** until now; the group of its own, the button for each group, the notice that is closed alone and the pointer were found by the pre-commit review and audit of this change.
- **PostgreSQL admins: this version has a database migration, so start it once with the role that owns the tables, normally `shellstation_admin`, before the users do. That start also applies the fix below. All users then need this version: an older one still connects with the mappings it has and can clear one, but can no longer save one.** In PostgreSQL mode, any user could read and rewrite every other user's mappings of sessions to their own credential profiles and login sequences. The two tables that hold them, `session_credentials` and `session_login_sequences`, had no row-level security, and a row was keyed only by the **User Identity** from Settings, a name each client chooses for itself. A mapping holds only the ID of a profile or sequence, which stays in its user's local database. But that ID decides which of the user's secrets a connection sends, and the table gave the IDs away. Someone with a database account could therefore point another user's mapping for a session at another of that user's profiles, and so have that user's password for one host sent to a different one, such as a shared session whose host is theirs. Setting their own User Identity to someone else's had the app do it for them.
  - **A mapping now belongs to the database role that wrote it.** A new migration adds an `owner` column to both tables. The app writes `current_user` there and names it in every statement, and `setup_postgres_rls` enables row-level security on both tables, with `FORCE` as on the others, and one policy for each, `session_credentials_owner_all` and `session_login_sequences_owner_all`. A role reads, changes and deletes its own rows, and of other rows only the placeholders described below. A row it writes must be its own and for a session it can see. The foreign key is checked without row-level security, so without that last rule its answer would tell any role whether a session with a given ID exists. The app's command for a single session now takes the session from `sessions` as well, like the one for a folder, and says `not found` for a session the role cannot see.
  - **User Identity stays, under the role.** People who share one database account are still told apart by it, as before. It no longer has to be unique across the team, since two roles' rows never collide: the primary key is now `(session_id, owner, user_ident)`. With the old `(session_id, user_ident)`, a row written under someone else's name would have kept that user from mapping the session at all. The migrations are shared with SQLite, which cannot drop a primary key, so the migration adds a unique index and `setup_postgres_rls` makes it the primary key.
  - **Existing mappings carry over.** They get the placeholder owner `'local'`, and this version does not connect with a placeholder. Each client claims the ones under its own User Identity at its next start, and when that identity is set or changed in Settings, which takes no more than a regular user's privileges. Where the user has mapped a session again in the meantime, that mapping stays and the placeholder is deleted. Any role may read, claim or delete a placeholder, but only for a session it can see, so the mappings for a personal session are left to that session's owner. A placeholder cannot be changed and stay one, and none can be added. A role named `local` gets no more of them than any other role, and can save no mapping of its own.
  - **What that leaves.** See **Known issues** for the placeholders of shared sessions, which are claimed by name alone. Under permission model Option B, users hold `TRIGGER` and `TRUNCATE` on these tables as on the others, and both reach past row-level security.
  - **Session IDs as sent.** The mapping commands bound a session, folder, profile or sequence ID to their statements as the webview sent it, after checking only that it reads as a UUID. One in upper case, in braces or without hyphens passed the check but matched no stored ID, and `session_connect` found such a session while missing its mapping, so the connection fell back to the session's own profile. The commands and the connect now bind the ID as `Uuid` prints it. The app itself only ever sent IDs in that form.
  - `docs/ADMIN_GUIDE.md` §4 removed a user's mappings by `user_ident` and left their login sequence mappings; it now removes both by `owner`. §5 has queries to check that the protection is applied. `docs/DESIGN.md` §4.5.1 describes the rule.
  - Covered by a new ignored test, `per_user_mappings_are_private`, run against a throwaway server like `shared_rows_keep_their_owner`, whose setup it now shares. It starts from mappings written as the previous version wrote them and checks what another role can see of them, that placeholders can be neither rewritten nor added, the claim, that a row under someone else's name gets in nobody's way, that a role can neither read, change nor delete another's rows nor give its own away, that it gets no mapping for a session it cannot see and the same answer for one that does not exist, that a role which does not own the tables cannot apply the setup, and the statements of the mapping commands, which moved out of the commands into functions the test can call.

  Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit audit of the shared-row fix.
- `docs/ADMIN_GUIDE.md` §6 took its backup as `shellstation_admin`. Row-level security is forced for the role that owns the tables too, so `pg_dump` stopped with `query would be affected by row-level security policy` instead of writing a dump. The guide now takes the backup as a superuser. Pre-existing; found while fixing the per-user mappings.
- In PostgreSQL mode, a lock held in the database could make every other user's ShellStation wait without end. PostgreSQL keeps a lock until its transaction ends and lets a statement wait for it that long, and none of the app's connections set a limit. Any user could take such locks, then on every shared item with `SELECT … FOR UPDATE` (the entry below leaves a row's lock to its owner), and can on a whole table with `LOCK TABLE`; so does a transaction left open in another tool, or a client that stopped in the middle of a change. An edit that met one never returned and kept one of the app's ten connections, and with a table locked the app did not get as far as showing its window, since the startup setup waited too.
  - **The app's statements now give up.** Every connection the app keeps its data through sets `lock_timeout` to 5 s, `statement_timeout` to 60 s and `idle_in_transaction_session_timeout` to 30 s before it is used. A change that cannot get a lock fails after 5 s and can be tried again, a start goes on without the setup, and the server ends a ShellStation session that has left a transaction open, with its locks. A value the server already sets for the database or the role is kept, unless it is 0, which reads the same as not set. They are set by a statement after connecting, not as startup parameters, which a connection pooler may refuse; being settings of the session, they do not hold through a pooler that hands each transaction to another session. The connection test and **Create Database** in Settings use connections of their own, which touch none of the app's tables.
  - **The migrations and the row-level security setup run under the same limits.** A migration that waits more than 5 s for a lock, or has a statement that takes more than 60 s, now fails and is rolled back, where it used to wait, and the next start tries again. The database error the app shows at startup says which of the two it was; before, its text for any error with `timeout` in it was that of a connection that timed out, which these would have been taken for. An admin who needs more for the owning role sets it on the server (`ALTER ROLE shellstation_admin SET lock_timeout = '30s'` for the wait, `statement_timeout` for a long statement), which is kept.
  - **Creating or moving a folder or session waits for fewer locks.** These lock the siblings of the new place so that two changes do not give two items the same position. They locked every sibling the user could see, which at the top level includes every other user's shared folders: a lock on one of those kept everyone else from creating a top-level folder of their own. They now lock only the user's own siblings.
  - **A start of the role that owns the tables no longer locks them.** The row-level security setup runs at every start, and its statements locked each table before finding nothing to do: `ALTER TABLE` and `CREATE POLICY` against every other use, `CREATE TRIGGER` against writers. A lock against every other use waits for every open transaction that has touched the table, and everything else that wants the table waits behind it, plain reads included, so a single transaction left open stopped every user for as long as that start waited. Each step now looks in the catalog first and changes only what is missing. The setup used to end at its first `ALTER TABLE` for a role that does not own the tables; it now checks that outright, before any step that changes the schema, so that no other role gets to the trigger functions.
  - **A setup that is interrupted leaves no table half protected.** A step can now fail at a held lock, which anyone can time to the owning role's start. The setup used to switch row-level security on for all tables and create the policies afterwards; stopped in between, a table refuses everything to everyone. The setup now goes a table at a time, after replacing the trigger functions. Each table's policies and the switch happen in one transaction, so a table has both or is as it was, and they are the table's last step, after its constraint or primary key and its triggers. A step that gives up on a lock, or as a statement that took too long, ends the steps of its table, and the setup goes on with the next table; the next start goes on from there. A step that fails for another reason, such as a constraint that existing rows break, no longer keeps the steps after it from the database at every start; only a function's triggers are left alone when that function could not be replaced. A step that fails at the connection, and not at the database, ends the setup, since every further step would meet the same. What failed is reported together. The application log tells a setup that is not this role's to apply, as at every regular user's start, from one that did not finish, which is an error.
  - **What that leaves.** A user can still hold a lock; see **Known issues**. `docs/ADMIN_GUIDE.md` has a new §7 on finding and ending the session that holds one and on limits set on the server, and §5 says that a second instance migrating at the same time waits 5 s, not without end. `docs/DESIGN.md` §4.5.3 describes the rules.
  - Covered by a new ignored test, `held_locks_do_not_hang_others`, run against a throwaway server like `shared_rows_keep_their_owner`. It checks that a connection made as the app makes its own has the three limits set and keeps a value from the server, that another user's lock on a shared top-level folder of his does not keep a user from creating and moving a folder of their own, that a change to a table locked against writers and a read of a table locked against everything fail at the lock instead of waiting, and that the owning role's setup runs while a row lock is held on the table and fails at the lock while the table is locked. The same for sessions: another user's session in one's own folder, as one may be from before the entry below, does not keep the folder's owner from creating and moving sessions in it. It checks that the setup puts back a missing policy and row-level security that is not forced or not on, that a setup interrupted at a lock on one table leaves that table without the missing policy and not forced, and still puts back a missing policy on another, that a constraint which cannot be added does not keep a missing trigger from being put back, and who the setup takes for the owner: a member of the owning role that inherits its privileges, and not one that does not. The lock limit is shortened to 300 ms for this, and what the other two limits do is PostgreSQL's and not tried. The test fails without the sibling change, for folders and for sessions, and without the catalog checks. The startup error's two new texts are checked in the unit test of its text for a connection that timed out.

  Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit audit of the shared-row fix.
- **PostgreSQL admins: start this version once with the role that owns the tables, normally `shellstation_admin`, for this fix to take effect.** In PostgreSQL mode, any user could rewrite another user's shared folders and sessions with a statement of their own. The `*_shared_update` policies let every role update every shared row, and the triggers of the earlier fix (above) kept only its owner and its place from being changed. Everything else was open: the host, port, user name and jump host of a shared session, which decide where everyone who opens it sends their credentials, its switch for legacy algorithms, its name and its tags. The app never did this, since its commands change only the user's own items, so it took direct access to the database. This is taken to be the gap that the known-issues entry on shared rows referred to; its details were kept outside the repository.
  - **A shared item is now its owner's alone to change.** `setup_postgres_rls` drops the two `*_shared_update` policies, in the same statement as the policies it creates. Another user's update of a shared row finds nothing to update, whatever it sets, and reading it works as before. The same policies were what let any user take a row lock on a shared item, so that is gone with them (see the lock entry above, and **Known issues** for what is left).
  - **An item goes only into a folder of its owner's.** A new row had only its owner checked, and the foreign key to its folder is checked without row-level security, so anyone could put a session of their own into another user's shared folder, where it showed among that user's, or move one there. A third trigger function, `shellstation_own_folder` (as `folders_own_folder` and `sessions_own_folder`), refuses both, for new rows and for moves. It looks at every folder above as well, so a folder of one's own that already sits in another user's is no way in either; such a folder can still be changed where it is, and moved out. The same look up the tree refuses to put a folder into itself or below itself. The app's own move command refused that, but the database accepted it from anyone's own statement, and in such a ring the app's queries down a folder's tree did not come to an end; they now stop at the statement limit of the lock entry above. The app's move command also looks up the tree, and no longer runs without end when a ring is already there. The table owner, its members and superusers get past the function, as past the other two.
  - **A session row's own credential profile and login sequence count for its owner only.** In PostgreSQL mode the session dialog leaves those two columns empty and keeps each user's choice in the per-user mappings, but Bulk Edit writes a login sequence into the rows of the user's own sessions, anyone's own statement can write either, and `session_connect` used them for whoever connected without a mapping. They are IDs in the connecting user's local database, so the owner of a shared session, who also chooses its host, could name one of another user's profiles and have its secret sent there. Another user's session is now connected to by the user's own mapping, and without one by no profile and no login sequence; a jump host that is another user's session needs the user's own mapping (a later entry leaves a session only jump hosts of its owner's). The two messages for a connection that has no profile to go by, for a session that has no user name either and for a jump host, say so. A row with the placeholder owner `'local'` is no one's own for this. The app now warns in its log when it cannot ask the database which role it runs as; until the next start it then takes no session for the user's own.
  - **What it does not undo.** What was changed, or put into someone's folder, before the owning role's first start of this version stays. A change to a shared item cannot be told from its owner's own, so owners should look over what they share; `docs/ADMIN_GUIDE.md` §5 has queries for items in another user's folder and for checking that the policies are gone. The admin role is held to its own rows now, like everyone: reassigning a removed user's items always took a superuser for the personal ones (§4), and now takes one for the shared ones too.
  - `docs/DESIGN.md` §4.5.1 describes the rules, and §4.5.3 and `docs/ADMIN_GUIDE.md` §7 no longer say that a user can lock every shared item.
  - Covered by the ignored test `shared_rows_keep_their_owner`, run against a throwaway server. It first puts the two policies back, as on a database whose owning role has not started this version, and checks that the triggers keep a shared row's owner and place there while its host can be rewritten. After the setup has dropped them, it checks that another user's updates of a shared row change nothing, whatever they set, that the row can be read but not locked, that neither a new item nor a move puts something into another user's folder, or into a folder of the user's own that is in another user's, seen or not, while the user's own folders and the top level work and such a folder can still be renamed and moved out, that a folder goes neither into itself nor below itself and that a ring which is already there neither stops the check nor keeps a folder from being taken out of it, that the owner still changes and moves her item, and that the admin role reaches none of it while a superuser does. It fails without the dropped policies and without the folder check. `held_locks_do_not_hang_others` now checks that the other user's row locks reach only his own rows, and has him hold the table for the change that gives up. A unit test in `commands/session.rs` covers whose session a row's profile counts for.

  Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit audit of the shared-row fix.
- **PostgreSQL admins: start this version once with the role that owns the tables, normally `shellstation_admin`, for this fix to take effect. It needs `CREATE` on the database, which `docs/ADMIN_GUIDE.md` §1 grants it.** In PostgreSQL mode, the trigger functions of the row-level security setup could be put in place by someone other than the owning role. The setup created them without a schema, which put them into `public`, and `CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION` keeps a function's owner. A role that may create functions there (every role before PostgreSQL 15, and the users of permission model Option B) could create one under the setup's name before the owning role's first start of the version that brings it. An owning role that is no superuser then failed at that function and created none of that function's triggers; one that is a superuser replaced the body of a function that stayed the other role's to change again, with the triggers calling it and running as whoever changed a row.
  - **The functions are in a schema of the owning role's.** `setup_postgres_rls` creates the schema `shellstation_rls` for the role that owns the tables and the trigger functions in it. No regular role can create anything there, or name what is there: a new schema grants nothing to anyone, making one takes `CREATE` on the database, which regular users do not have, and whatever anyone else has been granted on the schema is revoked at each start of the owning role. Whoever fires a trigger needs no privilege on the schema. Each function is made the owning role's in the statement that replaces it, also when a member of that role or a superuser runs the setup. A schema of the name that belongs to another role is not used: the setup reports it and leaves functions and triggers as they are. A start that is allowed to hands it to the owning role: a superuser's, or one of a role that has the privileges of the schema's owner.
  - **A trigger is replaced unless it is the setup's.** A trigger of one of the setup's names was left as it was. It is now left only if it calls the setup's function, is enabled, fires on the events and for the column it should, and has the argument and a condition, or none, as it should. Naming a function takes `USAGE` on its schema, so a trigger that calls the setup's function was made with the owning role's privileges. That moves the triggers of earlier versions to the new functions, puts back a trigger that was switched off, and replaces one that a user holding `TRIGGER` on the table (Option B) made under the name. The function in `public` that a replaced trigger called is dropped with the last trigger that called it, if it has the name of that trigger's function and is the starting role's to drop. The table is locked for this only when there is something to replace.
  - **The setup's own comparisons could be answered by someone else.** Its statements run with a search path that has `public` in it. `pg_catalog` is searched first, but PostgreSQL prefers an operator elsewhere on the path whose argument types match exactly to one in `pg_catalog` that needs a cast. A role that may create things in `public` could define an `=` for an `oid` and a `regclass`, and comparisons such as `tgrelid = 'folders'::regclass`, which the setup made at every start, ran it as the owning role and took its answer; the check of what a trigger calls would have been one of them. The setup now makes no comparison for which `pg_catalog` lacks an operator of exactly its two types: a name of a table, function or schema is cast on to `oid`, and a number compared with an `oid` is written as one.
  - **What that leaves.** What a trigger's condition says is not compared, and a user who holds `TRIGGER` on the tables can add triggers of their own under other names, which is why Option B is said to reach past row-level security (`docs/DESIGN.md` §4.5.1). A function in `public` under one of the setup's names stays there when no trigger of the setup's called it at that start, when that start did not get to the last trigger that called it, or when it is another role's and the starting role is no superuser; nothing of the setup's calls it once its triggers are moved, and `docs/ADMIN_GUIDE.md` §5 has the query to find it. See **Known issues** for what a role that may create in `public` can still do to other statements. If the owning role lacks `CREATE` on the database, its start says so in the application log and leaves the triggers as they were, and §5 has the statement for a superuser to create the schema.
  - Covered by a new ignored test, `trigger_functions_are_the_owning_roles`, run against a throwaway server like the others. It checks whose the schema and the functions are and that the triggers call functions in it, that a second start leaves every trigger as it is, the move from a function in `public`, which is dropped, or stays while another trigger calls it, a trigger for the wrong column, one with the wrong argument, one without its condition and one that was switched off, a function and a trigger that another role put under the setup's names first, with what that let through before the owning role's start and that it is refused after, that this role can neither create a function in the schema nor have a trigger call one, that the schema and functions are the owning role's when a member of it runs the setup, a schema that is another role's, for the owning role and for a superuser, that grants on the schema are taken back, and that the setup gets through with operators in `public` for each kind of comparison it used to make, which fail when called. It fails when a trigger of the name is accepted whatever it calls, when the setup's own triggers do not pass its comparison, as with a wrong value for the events, and without the casts to `oid`, where the planted operator then runs as the owning role. The owning role without `CREATE` on the database is not covered by it.

  Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit audit of the lock fix.
- **PostgreSQL admins: start this version once with the role that owns the tables, normally `shellstation_admin`, for the database's part of this fix to take effect.** In PostgreSQL mode, a session could name any session as its jump host, another user's included. The app offered every SSH session the user could see, and for a statement of a user's own the ID was enough, shared or not: the policies check a row's owner and nothing it refers to, and the foreign key is checked without row-level security. Three things followed. The owner of a shared session chose which session the people who opened it went through, and whoever had a profile for that jump host, as its owner has, went through it: their own bastion opened a connection to the host and port the other user's session names. The owner of a jump host could delete it, and the foreign key then left the session that named it without one, to connect directly. And the foreign key's answer alone told whether a session with a given ID exists.
  - **A session's jump host is now a session of its owner's.** A fourth trigger function, `shellstation_own_jump_host` (as `sessions_own_jump_host`), refuses any other, for a new session and for a change of the jump host. It answers before the foreign key does, and the same for a session that is not there as for one that is not the role's. A chain of jump hosts named under this rule thus has one owner, who alone changes any part of it; deleting a jump host is then its owner's change to their own sessions. Taking a jump host away is always allowed, and the trigger is not called for it, so the foreign key still clears a deleted one. The table owner, its members and superusers get past the function, as past the other three.
  - **The app's own statements refuse it as well,** with a message of their own (_A jump host has to be one of your own sessions_), when a session is created, changed or bulk-edited. That holds on a database whose owning role has not started this version. The session dialog and Bulk Edit now offer only the user's own sessions in PostgreSQL mode; a jump host that is set stays shown in the session dialog. A copy of a session (Clone) starts without a jump host that is another user's, which it would be refused. A data import whose file gives a new session a jump host that turns out to be another user's session, one already there in a shared folder of the same name, leaves that session without a jump host, goes on, and says in its summary how many there were. The other imports already reported a jump host they could not set as a warning.
  - **No connection is opened through another user's jump host.** `session_connect` goes through the chain link by link and stops at a jump host whose owner is not the owner of the session that names it, with a message that names both, before that jump host's profile is looked up and before anything is connected to. This is for what was named before, and for a database where the trigger is not in place or does not hold a user (Option B). It goes by the owners of the two rows, so it holds as far as a row's owner does: under Option B a user can get past the rule that keeps `owner` as well.
  - **What changes for users.** Another user's shared session could be picked as a jump host until now. Through the app such a connection only ever worked for the jump host's owner: the app had no way to assign one's own profile to another user's session (it has one now, see the next entry), which only a statement of one's own could. A session that names one is now refused at connecting until its owner picks a jump host of their own; it can still be edited, and saving it with the jump host unchanged is not refused. For a bastion that several users go through, each keeps a session of their own.
  - **What it does not undo.** A session that named another user's jump host before the owning role's first start of this version keeps it. It is not connected through, but the jump host's owner can still delete it, and the session that named it then connects directly, until the session's owner has picked a jump host of their own. This is listed under **Known issues**; `docs/ADMIN_GUIDE.md` §5 has the query that lists these sessions and what to tell their owners. Nor does it keep a role from learning whether a session ID exists by every way: the primary key still tells, and so does the foreign key of the `credentials` table (see **Known issues**).
  - Where the trigger is in place and holds the user, a regular role's row can no longer come to refer to another user's session through `jump_host_id`, so it no longer takes the foreign key's lock on that session either. `docs/DESIGN.md` §4.5.3, `docs/ADMIN_GUIDE.md` §7 and the lock entry under **Known issues** now list it only for the cases that are left: a session that named one before, a database whose owning role has not started this version, and Option B. `docs/DESIGN.md` §4.5.1 describes the rule, and §4 and §5 of the admin guide what it means for removing a user and for the update.
  - Covered by a new ignored test, `jump_hosts_stay_with_their_owner`, run against a throwaway server like the others. It checks that a user names a session of her own as a jump host and none of another user's, shared, personal or not there at all, the last with the trigger's refusal and not the foreign key's; that the app's create, update and bulk edit refuse the same with their own message, and that create and update take a jump host of the user's; that a session with another user's jump host from before can still be changed by a statement and by the app, but not be given another of his; that deleting a jump host clears it from the sessions that named it; that without the trigger the statement goes through while the app still refuses, and that the owning role's start puts the trigger back; and that the table owner gets past the function for its own rows only, for a new row and for a change. `trigger_functions_are_the_owning_roles` now counts four functions and seven triggers, and checks that a trigger of the new name which calls the right function but is not for new rows, is for another column, has no condition or has an argument is replaced. Two unit tests in `commands/session.rs` cover connecting: the rule it goes by, and the walk along a chain, on an in-memory database, which fails at the first jump host of another owner, whether that is the first link or a later one, names the two sessions, and gets there before that jump host's profile is looked up. `src/stores/sessionStore.test.ts` covers which sessions the dialogs offer, that a jump host which is set stays listed, and what a copy starts with. Not tested: a connection through a chain, which would need hosts to connect to; the dialogs themselves; and a data import that meets another user's session, of which only the sentence in the summary is tested. The app was not started.

  Pre-existing; listed under **Known issues** since the pre-commit audit of the shared-row gap.
- In PostgreSQL mode, a user could not assign a credential profile or login sequence of their own to another user's shared session. The session dialog and the folder commands were for the user's own items. Such a session was therefore connected to with the user name its row has and a typed password, and one with a jump host not at all: a jump host is its owner's session as well, and is not connected to without a profile. The per-user mappings that `session_connect` goes by were there for it, with nothing in the app to write them.
  - **My Login.** The context menu of another user's shared session has **My Login…** where a session of the user's own has **Edit**, and F2 opens it. The dialog names the session's owner, shows its protocol, host, port and user name and, for SSH, its switch for legacy algorithms, and takes a credential profile (for SSH) and a login sequence. It writes the user's own mappings with the commands the session dialog already uses; no statement and nothing in the shared database changed. While the role the app connected as is not known, every session would count as another user's, so the entry is not offered then.
  - **Jump hosts.** The dialog lists the session's jump hosts, each with its host and port and a profile to pick, and says that a jump host is not connected to without one. It follows the chain by the rule the backend connects by, and says so where a connect would be refused: at a jump host its owner has not shared, or one of another owner than the session that names it. A shared jump host has **My Login…** in its own menu as well.
  - **What the owner wrote is shown as written.** The owner chose the name, protocol, host, port and user name that the user decides by. Protocol, host, port and user name stand in fields of their own, apart from the dialog's sentences: left to right, wrapped, and whole, in a box that does not scroll: it is the end of a host that decides where a connection goes, and the rest of the dialog, with **Save**, is reached only past it. A host that can be confirmed takes 255 characters at the most as it is shown. A jump host's port has a field of its own too, so that a host cannot bring a port along. A host is shown in printable ASCII, and every other character in it is written out as its code point: `\u{0430}` for a Cyrillic a where a Latin one is expected, `\u{202E}` for a right-to-left override. A host in another script can look like one the user trusts, letter for letter, and marks and right-to-left letters can make two hosts look alike or change the order one reads in; a host that is in another script in earnest is shown the same way. A user name is shown like a host, with a backslash of its own doubled, since a domain's user name has one. A name and the owner's role name can be in any script, so there only the characters are written out that change how text displays: control characters, bidi overrides and isolates, and invisible characters, the set the import drops from a name. Dropping them would show something other than what is there. A name and the owner's role name are shown up to 64 characters, with an ellipsis after that, and a host and a user name up to 255, which is as long as one that can be confirmed is.
  - **Nothing is cleared unread, and nothing is lost in a save.** The dialog takes no choice and saves nothing until it has read what is assigned, writes only what was changed, and shows a failure to read or to save, such as a user identity that is not set. While a save is under way it takes no input and does not close, since its writes cannot be taken back; after a failure the focus is back on **Save**, and saving again writes only what is left.
  - **The messages at connecting** no longer tell a user to edit a session that is not theirs. Where no profile is found for a session or jump host whose own does not count for the user, they point to **My Login** in its context menu, and say that the User Identity has to be set and that the session's owner chooses the host. Where an assigned profile is gone, they name **My Login** as the place to pick another.
  - **`\w` in a login sequence is a password and nothing else.** With a key profile it typed the path of the key file at the remote host's prompt, which told that host where the key is kept and under which user name. It now stands for the password of a password profile, and for nothing with any other profile. Pre-existing, for the user's own sessions as well.
  - **The folder commands stay with a folder's owner.** **Apply Credential Profile…** and **Apply Login Sequence…** are not offered for another user's shared folder; see **Known issues** for why.
  - **What is assigned is used only for what was confirmed.** A mapping is for a session, and the session's owner can change what the session names afterwards: its host and port, its protocol, its user name, its jump host, its switch for legacy algorithms. A password would then go to a host of the owner's choosing, and a login sequence in clear text to whatever answers on Telnet, which checks no host key. A save in **My Login** therefore also confirms what the dialog shows. The backend reads the session and each jump host again, refuses the save if one is no longer what was shown, and otherwise remembers what each names, before anything is assigned. `session_connect` then uses a profile or login sequence that the user assigned to a session that is not theirs only while the session still names that. Otherwise it refuses, before that session's profile or sequence is read and before anything is connected to, and says what has changed; it does so for the session connected to and for each jump host on the way. Taking a jump host away counts as a change: the host is then looked up and connected to from the user's own machine.
  - **A session is confirmed for the way it was shown.** Of the session it is for, the dialog shows everything a connection goes by, and all of that is confirmed: protocol, host, port, user name, switch and jump host. Of a jump host it shows the host, the port and, as the next entry, the jump host it goes on to, and those are what is confirmed of it, kept apart from the other. A jump host is connected to over SSH, with the user's own profile and the switch of the session that goes through it, so its own protocol, user name and switch are not used; and what the user was not shown must not count as confirmed when the same session is later connected to by itself. A session confirmed as the one connected to passes as a jump host as well. Since it is that session's switch a jump host is connected to with, a profile assigned to a jump host is used only through a session the user has confirmed, also where nothing is assigned to that session itself: another session of the same owner through the same jump host, or the same one with its switch turned on since, is refused. The switch and the jump host count for SSH only; a Telnet session has no use for either.
  - **What cannot be shown for what it is is not confirmed.** The dialog has to show what the user confirms so that no two values look alike and none shows in part. That does not hold for everything a row can name. A statement of the owner's own can write a protocol that is neither SSH nor Telnet, which a connect takes for SSH, a port outside 1 to 65535, which a connect cut down to another port than the row shows, and a host longer than 255 characters, which would not show whole; the app's own commands refuse all three. They take the rest, though no session needs any of it: no host at all, or one with a backslash in it, which reads like a character written out, or with a space or a control character in it, or with any other character that no host name has and an SSH connect refuses, an invisible one among them, which a Telnet connect does not ask about; a host that takes more than 255 characters written out, as one of more than 30 letters of another script does, whose end would be far from its start; a user name longer than 255 characters, or with a control character in it, which a login sequence would type at the host as a key, or with a space at its start or end, which does not show. Such a session cannot be confirmed, the save says which one and why, and nothing the user assigned is used for it. A connect now refuses a port outside the range for any session, the user's own included. And the dialog takes whatever is not Telnet for SSH, as a connect does, so that what is assigned to such a session shows in it.
  - **The dialog says what has changed.** Where something is assigned and the session was never confirmed, or names something else than was confirmed, the dialog says so; it does so for a session whose jump host alone has a profile as well. What has changed is marked in red, a field by its border and a label also by its weight. A protocol, host, port or user name that was confirmed before is crossed out under what the session names now. So is the jump host a session went on to before, by the host and port the user confirmed of it and not by what its row names now, or by its ID where the user confirmed nothing of it. Its text now says that what is assigned is used only for what is shown when the user saves.
  - **Where it is kept.** In a file of the user's own, `session-logins.json` in the app's config directory, which is where the local database with the profiles is unless the user keeps that elsewhere. It is not in the shared database: there it would take new columns and a start of the owning role, and a role with the privileges for it could write what another user has confirmed. The file is written for its owner alone and replaced whole. One that is not what the app writes counts as nothing confirmed. One that is there and cannot be read, as while another program holds it, counts as nothing confirmed for the moment and is not written over: a save says so, and the file is read again the next time.
  - **What changes for users.** A profile or login sequence that was assigned to another user's shared session before this version is not used until its user has opened **My Login** for that session and saved; the refused connect says so. The session dialog offered such assignments until 0.11.3 kept it to the user's own sessions, and a statement of one's own could always make one. The same goes on a machine or account that does not have the file yet. And while the app does not know which database role it connected as, which the application log says at startup, it takes no session for the user's own, uses nothing the user has assigned to any, and says to restart.
  - **What that leaves.** What is confirmed is what a session names, not what answers under that name; see **Known issues**.
  - Covered by `SessionLoginDialog.test.tsx` (40 tests). What it shows: the owner named and protocol, host and port each by itself; the user name, with a backslash of its own doubled; whatever is not Telnet taken for SSH; a name, host and owner with such characters written out; a host in ASCII and nothing longer than it may be; the owner's switch for SSH; the notice for a jump host that cannot be used; a Telnet session with a login sequence only; the session kept that the dialog was opened for. What it saves: no choice taken before the read; what is assigned shown, confirmed and not written again; what is shown confirmed before anything is assigned, the session and its jump host each as shown; nothing assigned where the backend refuses to confirm; the choice for the session and for a jump host written and nothing else; an assignment cleared without confirming anything; nothing saved when the read fails, also by submitting the form; no input, no second start and no closing while a save is under way; Escape closing otherwise; only the rest written after a save that failed, with the focus back on **Save**. What it says of a change: the notice where something assigned was never confirmed; what was confirmed before crossed out and what changed marked; a changed user name and switch marked and the jump host that was shown by what was confirmed of it, also where there is none now; each jump host of a chain of two marked for what has changed of it; the notice gone once a save has confirmed, though assigning then failed, for the session and for a jump host; no notice where the session names what was confirmed, a jump host's own protocol, user name and switch aside; the notice for another host, port, user name, switch, protocol or jump host, each by itself; the notice where only a jump host has a profile and the session was never confirmed, and where that jump host was never confirmed, names another host or goes on to another one, and none where both are confirmed; none for the switch or the jump host of a Telnet session; none where nothing is assigned. `displayText.test.ts` has 15 tests, for a name, a host and a user name, and `sessionStore.test.ts` 7 new ones for the chain (its order, a jump host that is not visible, one of another owner, a loop, the ten the backend connects through, Telnet). Breaking the dialog one place at a time fails a test each, in 15 places: the check against a second submit, the lock on the selects, Escape during a save, the writing out of the host, the confirming before anything is assigned, the comparison of the host, of the port and of the user name with what was confirmed, the exception for a jump host, the two for a Telnet session, the rule for what is SSH, the notice being cleared by a save, the session being held where only its jump host has a profile, and the jump host that was being shown by what was confirmed of it. Without the check that the read has finished, the test run fails with an error it does not catch. The colour and weight of a mark are in the stylesheet and under no test, which only tells that an element is marked. In the backend, `login_pins.rs` has 16 tests: which sessions need confirming; a session that names what was confirmed, one never confirmed, and each change of protocol, host, port, user name, switch and jump host, with the message naming it; the switch counting for SSH only, and a Telnet session not held to a jump host; what a jump host is held to; that what was confirmed of a jump host does not confirm the session, and that a confirmed session passes as a jump host; what cannot be confirmed; the message without a known role; another user's name, host and user name written out and cut in a message, with a backslash of their own doubled; what was confirmed kept across a restart and replaced by confirming again; a file the app did not write, a file too large among them; a file that cannot be read for now, which is not written over, is said to be so, and is read again; a pin that cannot be written down, with what was confirmed before kept. `commands/session_login.rs` has 6: a session confirmed as shown; one changed since refused, in each field that is shown of it; what cannot be confirmed, with the session named; the sessions confirmed together being a chain; a shown session read as the dialog sends it; what is sent back of a session and of a jump host. In `commands/session.rs` the test of a chain of jump hosts now goes through another user's jump host with a profile assigned to it: refused while the session is not confirmed, and while its jump host is not; resolved once both are; refused through another session of the same owner that was never confirmed, and through the confirmed one with its switch turned on; refused again when the jump host's host is another; refused without a known role; and not asked about where nothing is assigned. That is the check in the walk along the jump hosts; the two in `session_connect` itself are not under test, as `session_connect` as a whole is not. The test of the connect messages checks the new advice, a new one what `\w` stands for, and the test of the registered commands covers the two new ones. The full Rust suite (434 tests, 6 ignored as before) and the frontend suite (505 tests) pass, and eslint, prettier, `tsc`, clippy and rustfmt are clean. The menu entry and F2 have no test, nothing was tried against a PostgreSQL server, the translations were not checked by native speakers, and the app itself was not started. The timing of the dialog's controls, the display of what the owner wrote, the role that is not known, the folder commands, `\w` and what the owner can change afterwards were found by the pre-commit review and audit of this change. So were three faults of earlier versions of what is confirmed, before any was committed: that a jump host's confirmation held what the dialog had not shown, that a profile for a jump host was used through a session that was not held to anything, and that the end of a long host could be scrolled out of sight.

  Pre-existing; was listed under **Known issues** since the review of the shared-row gap.

- In PostgreSQL mode, **Apply Credential Profile…** and **Apply Login Sequence…** on a folder of the user's own also assigned to other users' shared sessions that were in it or below it, without showing them. Such sessions can be in a user's folder from before a folder took only its owner's items (an earlier entry), or where the trigger of that fix does not hold a user (Option B). What a user assigns to another user's session is used only for what the user confirmed the session names in **My Login** (the entry before this one), but which profile or sequence is not part of that: one that these commands assigned to a session the user had confirmed was used from then on without the user being asked. The statements behind the two commands also took another user's folder, which only the menu kept from them, and they walked down the folders without a guard against a loop of them, which the trigger keeps a regular user from making: with one, the statement ran until its limit of 60 seconds and failed.

  - **The two commands are for the user's own sessions in the user's own folders.** Their four statements, which set and clear the two mappings, take the folder only where it is the user's own, go down only through folders of the user's own, and cover only sessions of the user's own, as Bulk Edit and a change of a folder's visibility already did. What a user has assigned to another user's session in such a folder stays as it is, when the folder is assigned to and when it is cleared, and is changed in **My Login**. For another user's folder the statements change nothing and count no session.
