  - **The walk ends on a loop of folders.** A folder that the walk has been through is not gone through again.
  - **What changes for users.** Whoever assigned to other users' sessions in a folder of their own with these commands does so in **My Login** from now on, one session at a time. A session of the user's own that is below another user's folder inside the user's folder, which can be there from before as well, is no longer covered by a command on the folder above; its own dialog assigns to it. The number the commands report is of the user's own sessions: those assigned to, or, when clearing, those that had something assigned. Nothing changes in the database, so no start of the owning role is needed.
  - Covered by the ignored test `per_user_mappings_are_private`, run against the PostgreSQL 18 server of `ssh-testlab`. In a folder of one user's it puts a shared session of another user's and a shared folder of his with a session of hers in it, as a superuser, the way rows from before the folder rule are there, and gives both sessions a mapping of hers by itself. Assigning to the folder and clearing it then cover her two own sessions there and leave those two mappings as they were; her command on his folder inside hers, and his on her folder, change nothing; and on two folders of hers that name each other as their parent, each command covers the one session there and returns, from either folder. Each of the four conditions (the folder asked for, the folders below it, the sessions, and the end of the walk) taken out of each of the four statements, sixteen breaks, fails the test. Not tested: the two commands from the folder's menu. The app was not started.

  Pre-existing; was listed under **Known issues** since the pre-commit audit of **My Login**.

- **Bulk Edit** on a folder and a change of a folder's visibility, in PostgreSQL mode, walked down the user's own folders without a guard against a loop of them, as did the two statements that write a credential profile or a login sequence into the session rows of a folder, which the app's window does not use in that mode. `shellstation_own_folder` keeps a regular user from making such a loop; with one, from before that trigger or under Option B, a statement that had to walk to the end ran until its limit of 60 seconds and failed. Whether it has to is for PostgreSQL to decide: it may read the whole walk first, and where it does not, it still has to as soon as the user has a session or a folder that is not below the folder asked for. The walk is through the user's own folders only, so a loop held up no one but its owner. **Sort Alphabetically** on a folder, which sorts the folders below it as well, went down the folders in the app itself and without a guard too, in either mode: on a folder of such a loop it did not come to an end, sent the database one statement after another and took more and more memory, until the app was closed.

  - **Each walk ends on a loop of folders now**, as the ones of **Apply Credential Profile…** and **Apply Login Sequence…** do since the entry before this one: a folder that the walk has been through is not gone through again. Nothing else about the five statements changes, and nothing in the database.
  - **Sort Alphabetically goes into a folder once.** It also reads the folders once for the whole sort, where it read them again for every folder it went into.
  - Covered by a new ignored test, `folder_walks_end_on_a_loop`, run against the PostgreSQL 18 server of `ssh-testlab`. A superuser makes two folders of a user's name each other as their parent, which the trigger refuses the user herself, with a third folder below one of them and a session in the first and in the third, and a folder of hers apart from those with a session of its own. That last one is there to make a statement walk to the end however PostgreSQL goes about it: the way it did in this test, it looked for each of the user's sessions or folders among the folders of the walk and stopped at the first it found, and the test passed without the guard until there was a session to look for in vain. From either folder of the loop, each statement then returns and covers the two sessions there once, and not the one apart: the two for the session rows, Bulk Edit in both of its passes, the change of visibility for the three folders and the two sessions, and the four statements of the entry before this one. The visibility command's statements moved into a function of their own for this, `set_item_visibility`, which the command calls. With the guard taken out of any one of the five statements, the test fails. The sort has a unit test on an in-memory SQLite database, `a_recursive_sort_ends_on_folders_that_name_each_other`: from either folder of a loop it ends and has sorted the folders of the loop and those below it, it ends on a folder that names itself as its parent, and without the guard it fails after its 10 seconds. Not tested: Bulk Edit, the change of visibility and the sort from the menu. The app was not started.

  Pre-existing; the statements were listed under **Known issues** since the pre-commit review and audit of the entry before this one, and the sort was found by the audit of this fix.

- In PostgreSQL mode, the app did not check that the row-level security setup is in the database. Only the role that owns the tables can apply the setup, at a start of it that gets through. Where that start's setup stopped part of the way, as at a lock someone held, or a release's change to the setup had not been applied yet, every other user's ShellStation worked against the database as it was, and all there was to see was an error in the application log of the owning role's start.

  - **Every start asks the catalog for what its version's setup applies**, as whatever role it is, and after the setup has run, been skipped or stopped: row-level security and `FORCE` on the four tables, each policy and that the two obsolete ones are gone, each trigger with the function it calls and its shape, the four trigger functions, their schema and that both are the owning role's, the constraint on `visibility`, and the primary key of the two mapping tables. It asks what the setup's steps ask before they change anything, and about the two things the setup does at every start without looking: it compares each trigger function with the one this version creates, in its body, the search path it is pinned to and that it runs as its caller, and it asks whether anyone but the owning role holds a privilege on the functions' schema.
  - **The window says what it finds.** Where something is missing or different, a notice names the tables concerned, and the trigger functions where it is those, says that other users of the database may be able to see or change more than they should, that the role that owns the tables has to start this version once, or that ShellStation is to be updated if a newer version set up the database, and to tell the database administrator. It stays until it is dismissed, is not closed along with a pile of other notices, and is back at every start while it applies, and whenever the window's page is loaded again. The application log has every part as an error. A check that fails is told as that, in a notice of its own, and is not taken for a setup in place. The notice is there in all 14 languages.
  - **The check waits for no lock.** It is one statement that reads the catalog and nothing else, so a lock on a table, which makes the setup give up on that table, does not hold it up, and the start that could not apply the setup still says what is missing.
  - The setup's lists of trigger functions, triggers and policies moved out of `setup_postgres_rls` into functions of their own, and what a trigger has to be into another, so that the setup and the check go by the same lists. The setup's statements are the same but for white space.
  - Covered by a new ignored test, `rls_setup_check_finds_what_is_missing`, run against the PostgreSQL 18 server of `ssh-testlab`. As a regular user, the check names all 28 parts on a database that the setup was never applied to, and nothing after the setup. For each of 26 changes a superuser makes, it names that part and no other: row-level security not forced and switched off, a policy dropped, an obsolete policy put back, the constraint dropped, a primary key under another name and a constraint of its name that is no primary key, a trigger dropped, disabled, calling a function elsewhere, made for another column or without the insert, and with an argument or a condition it should not have or without the one it should, a function with another body, without its search path, with another one and running as its owner, a function and the schema given to another role, a function dropped with its trigger, and a privilege on the schema granted to everyone and to one role. With all of `folders` locked against every other use it still answers, within 2 seconds, and a check on a closed connection is reported as not checked. Each of 26 conditions taken out of the check or its report, one at a time, fails the test, those of a trigger's shape and of a function's attributes at the case made for them. The notice has unit tests in the frontend, among them that every language has a text of its own for it. Not tested: the notice in the running app. The app was not started.

  Pre-existing; was listed under **Known issues** since the pre-commit audit of the per-user mapping fix.

- **PostgreSQL admins: the permission model "Option B" is withdrawn. If your database was set up with it, take its grants back as `docs/ADMIN_GUIDE.md` §2.3 says.** The admin guide and `docs/DESIGN.md` §4.5.2 offered it beside the model with an admin role and DML-only users, for teams that wanted every user to apply migrations. It promised what PostgreSQL does not allow, and what it did grant reaches past row-level security. Nothing changes in the app.

  - **It never applied a migration.** PostgreSQL leaves altering a table, adding an index to it and creating a policy on it to the table's owner, which no grant confers. Five of the six migrations do one of these to an existing table, and the first is the admin role's in any case. An Option B user who started a new version first therefore did not apply its migration: their ShellStation started in offline mode until the admin role had started that version, as a regular user's does.
  - **What it did grant.** All privileges on the tables include `TRIGGER` and `TRUNCATE`: a trigger of a user's own runs when other users write, as those users, the admin role among them, and `TRUNCATE` empties a table whatever its policies say. `CREATE` on the schema `public` lets a user put tables there, and operators and functions that other users' statements may run in place of the built-in ones. The docs said so already, as a trade-off for small teams, beside a list of rights (`ALTER TABLE`, `CREATE POLICY`) that the grants do not give.
  - **Why it is withdrawn and not repaired.** A user who is to apply any migration needs the owner's rights over the tables, as a member of the owning role has, and whoever has them can drop the policies and replace the trigger functions that run when other users write. PostgreSQL has no privilege for applying a release's schema changes and nothing else. What the one model costs is one start of the admin role for each release that changes the schema or the row-level security setup, and a user who starts such a release before that is told in the window (the entry before this one, and the offline banner where a migration is waiting).
  - **The docs.** `docs/ADMIN_GUIDE.md` §2 has the one set of grants, with `GRANT ALL PRIVILEGES` and `CREATE` on the schema among the things to avoid, and §5 the one update procedure. Its new §2.3 is for a database that was set up with Option B, as steps to take before the admin role connects again, since a user's trigger runs as the admin role too. They are, with the users kept out and no row changed meanwhile: the statements that take the grants back and leave what a regular user gets, from everyone as well, who before PostgreSQL 15 may create objects in `public`; queries that show whether any role still holds more, on a table, a sequence, a column or for later tables, or as the owner of the database, which no revoke takes from it; queries for what was made with the grants and stays, which are tables, views and schemas that are not the admin role's, triggers that call nothing of the setup's, functions, operators and types outside the setup's schema, members of the admin role, roles that bypass row-level security or create roles, members of the predefined roles, settings stored for a role or the database, and the rows of the table that says which migrations are applied; how to remove it, which begins with switching row-level security on and dropping every trigger and policy of the tables, the setup's own included, since the check at each start does not read what a policy says, and the admin role's next start makes the setup's again; and what cannot be undone or told afterwards, which is what a trigger read or changed in any user's rows, and what it ran with the rights of the admin role or of a superuser. §5's update procedure also no longer says to grant on new tables again, which the default privileges cover. `docs/DESIGN.md` §4.5.2 describes the one model and says why the other went. Both still say "under Option A" and "a user of Option B", as the code's comments and this changelog do: the first now means a database whose regular roles hold no more than the model gives them, the second a role that still holds the withdrawn grants.
  - Tried in a throwaway database on the PostgreSQL 18 server of `ssh-testlab`: with Option B's grants a role that does not own a table is refused `ALTER TABLE`, `CREATE INDEX` and `CREATE POLICY` on it, and can add a trigger to it, truncate it and create a table; after the statements of §2.3 it is refused the trigger, the truncating and the table as well, and still reads and writes rows. In a second one, with a table, a trigger and its function as the setup leaves them, the queries of §2.3 return nothing; with `CREATE` on `public` for everyone, a role granted directly, a schema, a trigger, a view, a type, a function and an operator of that role's, a member of the admin role and a role that bypasses row-level security, each query lists what it is for, and after the statements for the group alone the first two still list the role that was granted directly. The checks for a sequence, a column, a predefined role and a stored setting each list one that was planted, and nothing before. On a database that ShellStation's own migrations and setup made, in the harness of the ignored tests, the queries of step 2 and the first of step 1 return nothing either; and after the block of step 3 the check at a start names the eight policies and seven triggers, a regular user sees no folder, and a run of the setup as the admin role leaves nothing missing. The steps were also run from first to last there, for a role that was given Option B directly and had made a trigger with its function on `sessions` and a table: the checks of step 1 come back empty, step 2 lists the three, after step 3 it lists nothing, the setup then runs through with nothing missing, and the role is refused `TRUNCATE`, a trigger and a table and still saves a folder. Not tried: a database that was really run under Option B, and a server before PostgreSQL 15.

  Pre-existing; was listed under **Known issues** since the review of the shared-row fix.

- **PostgreSQL admins: start this version once with the role that owns the tables, normally `shellstation_admin`, for this fix to take effect, and update the users' ShellStation before or together with that start.** In PostgreSQL mode, regular users could write to `_sqlx_migrations`, the table by which a start decides whether it has migrations to apply. `docs/ADMIN_GUIDE.md` §2 grants `SELECT`, `INSERT`, `UPDATE` and `DELETE` on all tables of the schema, this one included, and it has no row-level security. A user who took a row away there, or marked one as failed, sent every start into the migrations: regular users then started in offline mode, and the admin role's start failed, at a migration that was applied already or at the row marked as failed, before the row-level security setup ran, until the table was put right by hand. A user who added a row for a migration of a later release, whose file and therefore whose checksum is public, had that migration taken for applied when the release came. It let a user keep everyone out of the database and hold back a schema change; it showed no user another's items.

  - **The table is the owning role's to write.** The row-level security setup, which runs at every start of the owning role, takes back whatever anyone but the table's owner holds beyond `SELECT`, on the table or on a single column of it, from a role, from everyone, and with what a role passed on. Privileges are revoked by the names the server gives them, so one that a later PostgreSQL adds goes as well. A start that finds nothing to revoke takes no lock on the table. A `REVOKE` locks it as a reader does: it holds up no one and waits only for a lock against every other use, on which it gives up like any other step of the setup.
  - **Rows for migrations the build does not carry are logged.** What the table already holds is not undone. A start writes a warning to the application log where the table has rows for migrations its own build does not carry. A later version leaves such rows; so would a user who added the row of a coming migration while the table was theirs to write, and until that release comes its number is one that no build carries.
  - **A start writes there only where it migrates.** Every start used to put right the checksums that differ from its build's by line endings alone, as a Windows build's do against a database set up from Linux or macOS, with an `UPDATE`, which a regular user is now refused. That is done only where migrations are about to run, which is what compares the checksums, and so by the owning role. A start that finds every migration applied reads the table and nothing more.
  - **The check at each start asks about it** (two entries up): where anyone but the table's owner holds more on it than reading, or the table is not the owning role's, the window names `_sqlx_migrations` in its notice, and the application log says which of the two.
  - **Older versions.** A version from before this one still writes to the table at its start where the stored checksums have other line endings than its build's. Refused that, it starts in offline mode. So the users update before, or together with, the owning role's start of this version.
  - **The docs.** The grants in `docs/ADMIN_GUIDE.md` §2.1, §2.2 and §2.3 take the three privileges back from a regular role, so that the notice does not show after a new role has been granted to, and §5 says what the release changes, with the statement and a query to look by hand. `docs/DESIGN.md` §4.5.2 says why the table is the owning role's, and no longer says that `sqlx::migrate!()` runs at a regular user's start, which it has not since the app's own look at the table.
  - Covered in the ignored test `rls_setup_check_finds_what_is_missing`, run against the PostgreSQL 18 server of `ssh-testlab`. Before the setup, with the grants as the guide had them, the check names the privileges and a regular user marks a migration as failed, after which the app's look finds migrations to apply. After the setup the check names nothing, and for a grant to one role, to everyone, on all tables again, on one column, and one passed on by a role that was allowed to, and for the table given to another role, it names that part and no other. A regular user is then refused an `UPDATE`, a `DELETE` and an `INSERT` there and a lock on the whole table, and still reads it; with a checksum that differs by line endings, a regular user's look at the migrations passes and leaves it, and the owning role puts it right; the setup revokes while a writer's lock is held on the table, and under a lock against every other use it gives up at that step, goes on, and the check names what is left; and a row for a migration that the build does not carry is found, and counts for nothing in the look at the migrations. Each of 11 conditions taken out, one at a time, fails the test. Tried by hand on the same server as well: a `REVOKE` goes through under a lock that lets only reads by. Not tested: a start of the app against a database with the table tampered with, and an older version against a database after this one's setup. The app was not started.

  Pre-existing; was listed under **Known issues** since the pre-commit audit of the withdrawal of Option B.

- **Toasts lay above dialogs,** so a stack of them could cover part of a dialog and take the clicks meant for it. In a window less than about 1350px wide, a dialog reaches under the toasts' column with its right part, where its buttons are, and the host key prompt is such a dialog. A toast can hold text from a file or a remote host, and a file from someone else can put up a tall stack with an import's warnings.
  - **Dialogs lie above the toasts.** The toasts show dimmed beside a dialog, and none covers it or takes a click meant for it. A menu that is open lies above them as well.
  - **Under a dialog a toast waits.** The stack takes no input there: the pointer does not reach it through the dialog, and it is inert, so that its buttons take no focus from the keyboard either. A toast's time does not run under a dialog, which may hide it, and starts, in full, once no dialog lies over it.
  - **What a dialog raises itself is shown with it.** Three dialogs raise toasts of their own while they stay open: Settings, for an error and for an import's result and warnings, and the Credential Manager and the Login Sequence Manager, for an error. Under the dialog such a toast would be dimmed and partly hidden behind the dialog that has just raised it. It is shown in a layer of the dialog's own instead, under every dialog on top of it or opened from it, such as the host key prompt or a confirmation. With this change the layer still lay over the dialog itself; it is a strip on top of the dialog's box since the entry on that below. Only what the dialog itself reports is in its layer. A toast from anywhere else, such as a tab's failed connect, is under the dialog like any other, also when it comes while the dialog is open. When the dialog closes, the toasts it still shows go to the window's stack, with their time in full, and what its work reports after that goes there too. **Close all** closes the toasts of the stack it is in. The update dialog is no fourth: the toast for a check that failed comes with its error dialog, which says the same, and is under it until it is closed.
  - **One limit, and one of each toast that stays.** The limit of 100 toasts is for the window's stack and the dialogs' layers together: the oldest goes, in whichever it is. A toast that stays until closed is still shown once. Where a dialog asks for one that is already in the window's stack, it moves to that dialog's layer.
  - **The progress of an import and the notice while the app quits** lie over the toasts as every dialog does, also over those of Settings: under them a toast waits.
  - **A dialog's own toasts lay over that dialog** as this change left them. The entry on that below puts them into a strip on top of the dialog's box.
  - Covered by tests in `ToastContainer.test.tsx` and `toastStore.test.ts`: a toast's time under a dialog and after it; a stack that is inert under a dialog, on top of it or opened in its overlay, and not under its own or one below; what a dialog raises in its layer and nothing else there; a closing dialog's toasts in the window's stack, and what it reports afterwards; a click on a dialog's toast, which a dialog that closes on a click beside it does not take for one; **Close all** for one stack; a dialog rendered into the body, as a confirmation is; a dialog mounted twice, as in development; the limit across stacks; and a toast that stays, asked for by a dialog. What is painted on top was checked in Chromium with the app's stylesheet and components, in a window 876px wide: with 26 toasts up, the buttons of a dialog take the pointer over their whole area, a dialog's own toast takes it over that dialog and no longer under a second one, and a button of a toast under a dialog refuses the focus.

  Pre-existing; was listed under **Known issues** since the pre-commit audit of the toast stack fix.

- **A dialog under another could take Enter or Escape,** when the dialog on top claimed that key not at all. A key went to the topmost of the dialogs that claimed it, and not every dialog claims both. The host key prompt accepts a first connection's key on Enter. The keyboard-interactive prompt claims no Enter, which its form is for, and is on top of a host key prompt that is open at the same time, as with two tabs connecting at once: the Enter after a password typed there accepted the other host's key, unseen, and did not send the password. The update dialog, which appears by itself, and Settings opened from the database banner claim no Enter either, and can lie over a host key prompt that comes up behind them.
  - **A claim counts only while no dialog lies over it,** whatever that dialog claims. The key is then left alone, for the dialog on top: a form there is submitted, a focused button pressed.
  - **A dialog that another lies over takes no input.** No dialog keeps the focus to itself, so a button of the lower one, Accept of a host key prompt, could still have the focus when another dialog appeared on top, and Enter or Space typed there pressed it, unseen. The lower dialog's box is now inert for as long as another lies over it: its buttons lose the focus and cannot get it.
  - **A held Enter is one Enter.** A dialog that closes on Enter left the key's repeats to the dialog under it, so the Enter that confirmed a paste or a deletion could go on to accept a first connection's host key. A repeat of the key is now taken and does nothing.
  - **Every dialog says that it is there.** The prompt for the user identity, the progress of an import and the notice while the app quits claimed neither key and so had no marker, by which the dialogs' order is known. They have one now. Escape during an import therefore no longer closes Settings under the progress.
  - Covered by four tests in `DialogKeys.test.tsx`: Enter typed into a field of a dialog that claims Escape alone, over one that accepts on Enter, and both keys under a dialog rendered into the body that claims neither, both of which fail without the change; a repeat of Enter, which no dialog gets and no button; and the box of a dialog under another, beside it, inside its overlay or rendered into the body, which is inert until that one is gone. What a webview does with an inert box is not covered there: the test environment only has the attribute.

  Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit audit of the fix above.

- **An open menu lay above a dialog that appeared while it was open.** A context menu is painted above the dialogs, and the host key prompt comes when the host answers, whatever is open then: the menu of a tab, of the session tree or of a command button stayed over it until a click beside it.
  - **A menu closes when a dialog opens,** also one that opens together with it. So does the open list of a select, which is painted above a dialog opened inside the same overlay. A menu opened in a dialog that is already there stays, and a dialog that closes leaves it alone.
  - **No menu is opened from behind a dialog.** The pointer does not get past a dialog, but what had the focus when it appeared still has it, a tab, a command button or an item of the session tree, and the menu key or Shift+F10 there opened its context menu above the dialog. The request is now dropped where it is for something outside the dialog on top.
  - **A dragged item's preview is under the dialogs.** The drag-and-drop library puts it at a `z-index` of 999, above a prompt that appears while an item of the session tree is dragged; it is 95 now, above the toasts.
  - Covered by six tests in the new `ContextMenu.test.tsx`: a menu and a select's list, each closed by a dialog that opens; a menu and a dialog that open together; a context menu asked for behind a dialog, inside it and with none open; a menu that stays open in a dialog that was there before it and when that dialog closes; and a select's list that stays open while its dialog renders again. The preview's `z-index` has no test.

  Pre-existing; was listed under **Known issues** since the pre-commit audit of the fix for toasts above dialogs.

- **What was typed while a dialog was open went to whatever had the focus behind it.** A dialog covers the window, so no click gets past it, but it does not take the focus: a terminal that had it when a prompt appeared kept it, and what was typed went on to its remote host, unseen behind the prompt. Tab left the dialog for the window behind it, and a shortcut of that window, such as Ctrl+D for a copy of the selected session, worked from inside a dialog.
  - **Nothing typed at something behind the dialog on top reaches it.** That goes for keys, for text put in by a paste, a cut or an input method, and for the window's shortcuts. Ctrl+Q still asks to quit. A key typed there does nothing in the webview either, such as one that reloads the page. With the focus on nothing, as after a click on text in the dialog, a key is for the page and goes on as before: the keys that copy a selected fingerprint or scroll the dialog work.
  - **Escape and Enter typed there still answer the dialog,** as before, so that a prompt can be answered without a click into it: after a double click on a session, the focus is on the session tree. The exception is an Enter typed into a terminal, which ends a line that was being typed there and is no answer to a prompt that appeared meanwhile. It was left to the terminal before, and is dropped now. Escape went to a terminal where the dialog on top claims none; it is dropped as well.
  - **Tab stays in the dialog.** It goes from the dialog's last stop to its first and back, from anything behind the dialog into it, and from something in the dialog that is no stop itself, such as text that was clicked, to the next stop of the dialog. What the key was typed at in the dialog still gets it: a select closes its open list.
  - **The focus is left where it is.** A prompt that took it from a terminal would get the Enter of the line being typed there, which accepts a first connection's host key.
  - Covered by twelve tests in `DialogKeys.test.tsx`, for a window with a terminal, a field and a tab behind a dialog: keys and their release; a paste, text put in and text composed; Escape and Enter from a tab, which the dialog gets; Enter from the terminal, which neither gets; Escape and Enter with no claim; Tab into the dialog, around at its ends and left alone between two of its stops; keys with the focus on nothing; Enter in a field of the dialog that takes lines; Tab from something in the dialog that is no stop, past stops that are disabled, hidden or not shown; a stop that sees the Tab that takes the focus around; a dialog under another, which is behind it; and whether a dialog is open. The test environment lays nothing out, so what is shown is a stub's answer there. Tried in Chromium with a terminal of the app's own library behind a dialog of the app's stylesheet and components: what is typed reaches the terminal before the dialog opens and not while it is open, Enter there answers nothing, Escape answers the dialog, and Tab goes into the dialog and around in it. The check for Ctrl+D has no test, and nothing here was tried with an input method.

  Pre-existing; was listed under **Known issues** since the pre-commit audit of the fix for toasts above dialogs.

- **A toast that a dialog raised itself lay over that dialog,** where it could cover part of it and take the clicks meant for it. Settings shows an error that way, and an import's result and warnings, whose text is the file's and of which a file from someone else can make a tall stack; the Credential Manager and the Login Sequence Manager show an error. In a window less than about 1390px wide these dialogs reached under the toasts' column with their right part, where their buttons are.
  - **The three dialogs show their messages in a strip on top of the dialog's box.** The strip is part of the dialog's layout and covers nothing: the box moves down and gives up height as the strip grows, and the two are never taller than the window together. It takes up to about a third of the window's height, where its lists scroll, as they do in the window's stack. No toast floats over a dialog any more.
  - **What the dialog holds moves** when a message comes, and back when it is closed. So that it does not move by itself, a message in the strip has no time that runs out: it stays until it is closed, by its own button or by **Close all**, or until the dialog is. It then goes to the window's stack, as before, with its time in full.
  - **The three dialogs are 520px wide,** as wide as a dialog of their kind gets, so that the strip and the box are one width whatever the box holds. With little in it, such a dialog was as narrow as 420px. Where the window has less, they are as wide as the window allows, and no longer at least 420px, which at a large UI scale is more than a small window has.
  - **The box takes no click for half a second after the strip has changed.** A control of the dialog comes to lie where the pointer is when the box moves: under the button that closed the last message, for the second click of a double click, or under a click that was on its way when a message came. The click is refused at the box and goes nowhere, not to the overlay behind it either, where a click closes the two managers. The keyboard is not held up.
  - The rest is as before: only what the dialog itself reports is in its strip, a toast from anywhere else waits under the dialog, and a dialog on top of the three or opened from them covers the strip as it covers the box.
  - Covered by tests in `ToastContainer.test.tsx`: a dialog's toast in a strip before its box, and not in the stack that floats; a toast that stays for ten minutes, is closed by its button, and has its time once the dialog has closed; and a click in the box, refused for half a second after a message came and after the last one was closed, and taken as before when a toast comes in the window's stack. The layout was tried in Chromium with the app's stylesheet and components, in a window 876px wide: the strip ends where the box begins and is as wide, with one message and with forty, where it stops at 34% of the window's height and the list scrolls; a box with little in it and one that fills the window both stay within the window; a field of the box takes the pointer; and at a UI scale of 150% the two are still one width. In a window 656px wide, close to the smallest the app allows, the two are 520px wide at a scale of 100% and as wide as the window allows at 150% and 200%, with nothing to scroll to sideways; and a click on a button of the box does nothing right after a message came or the last one was closed, does not reach the overlay, and is taken again 600ms later. The three dialogs themselves were not tried, which do not run outside the app.

  Pre-existing; was listed under **Known issues** since the pre-commit audit of the toast stack fix, narrowed by the fix for toasts above dialogs.

- **In PostgreSQL mode, any user could change or delete every user's highlight profiles, and with one pattern bring other users' windows to a standstill.** The profiles were kept in the shared database, in a table with no owner and no row-level security, and a session row named its profile there. A profile's patterns run in the window of whoever opens a session with it, in the webview's regular expression engine, and the check that refuses patterns prone to catastrophic backtracking goes by a pattern's shape and does not catch every one. A rule can also show text in the background's colour. Whether profiles should be per-user or shared with an owner was listed under **Known issues** as undecided. They are now **per-user**: in PostgreSQL mode each user's profiles are in their own local database, where credential profiles and login sequences already are, and so is the choice of a profile for a session.
  - **Why not shared with an owner.** An owner would keep one user from changing another's profile. It would not keep the owner of a shared profile, or of a shared session that names one, from choosing what runs in every other user's window, so the backtracking check would still be all that stands between two users. Kept by each user, the only patterns a window runs are ones its user made or imported. The price is that a team has no profile in one place: a profile is passed on as a file, with **Data Export / Import**.
  - **Sessions.** Which profile a session is shown with is the user's own choice, kept in the local database by session (`db/session_highlights.rs`), for the user's own sessions and for other users' shared ones. The session dialog and Bulk Edit make it for the user's own sessions as before. Another user's shared session, which is not the user's to edit, has **My Highlighting…** in its context menu, with a dialog that offers the user's profiles; it shows the session's name written out, as **My Login** does. The new `session_set_highlight_profile` command is behind it. `session_list_all`, `session_get` and `session_search` hand the window each session with the user's choice in place of what its row names, so a terminal follows it as it followed the row. The row's `highlight_profile_id` is neither read nor written in PostgreSQL mode, a profile that is not in the user's local database is refused as a choice, and where the choices cannot be read a session is shown without a profile, never with the row's. Bulk Edit chooses the profile for the sessions the rest of it covers, the user's own. A choice is dropped when its profile is deleted, and when the user deletes its session or a folder above it.
  - **What was there is taken over, once.** At its first start in PostgreSQL mode with this version, ShellStation copies the profiles of the shared table into the local database, with the profile that each of the user's own sessions names as the choice for it, and remembers that it has; so an update leaves no one without their profiles. Another user's shared session starts without a profile: the one its row names was its owner's choice, and the user makes their own with **My Highlighting**. A profile that is there already, under its ID or with the same name and rules (as after a move from SQLite to PostgreSQL, which left the local profiles behind), is not copied again, and a session with a choice keeps it. The copy and the note of it are one transaction; a take-over that fails is logged and tried again at the next start. Afterwards nothing is read from the shared table.
  - **Within limits.** Every role can still write the shared table, and a start reads it before there is a window. The rows are therefore checked, and taken within limits: a profile with a name of at most 255 bytes and rules of at most 1 MiB that read as a list of at most 500 rules, as many as a profile saved in the app has (the limit moved to `highlight.rs` as `MAX_RULES_PER_PROFILE`); at most 1000 profiles; and at most 16 MiB of rules in all. No statement of the take-over runs longer than 10 seconds, where the app's others have a minute: the limit is set for the transaction that reads, unless a shorter one is in effect, and ends with it. The profiles that a session of the user's own names are read first, then the others in the order the app showed them, so that no other role can fill the limits before the profiles the user's sessions use. What is past a limit, or is no profile the app could have saved, stays where it is, and the application log has a warning that says how many profiles that was.
  - **Nothing changes in the shared database.** The two local tables (`local_session_highlights`, `local_flags`) are made by the app at its start in PostgreSQL mode, not by a migration: the migrations are shared with PostgreSQL, where one would put the tables into the shared database, unused, and take a start of the owning role. There is no migration and no change to the row-level security setup, so no start of the admin role is needed. `docs/ADMIN_GUIDE.md` §5 has what an admin does: look the shared profiles over before the users update, and empty the table and revoke writing to it once they all have.
  - **Elsewhere.** The data export writes, and the data import and the SecureCRT highlight import add to, the profiles where the app keeps them, which in PostgreSQL mode is the local database; an export's sessions carry the user's choice. **Settings → Keyword Highlighting** says in PostgreSQL mode that the profiles are kept on this computer and are the user's alone. SQLite mode is unchanged: one database, and a session row names its profile. The new strings are in all 14 UI languages.
  - Covered by nine tests in `db/session_highlights.rs` (choices set, replaced and cleared, a deleted profile forgotten, the overlay replacing what a row names, a take-over copying once and nothing that is written to the shared table later, keeping what is there, leaving out what is no profile or has one rule more than the app saves, and the walk below a folder, which ends on folders that name each other); six in `commands/session.rs`, with one SQLite database standing for the shared one and a second for the local one (a new session, an update, a single choice and a Bulk Edit each leaving the row's column alone and refusing a profile that is not the user's, an update of the profile alone refused for a session that is not there, SQLite mode writing the row, and the sessions that go with a deleted folder); nine in `SessionHighlightDialog.test.tsx` (the profiles offered, the start from what is chosen, none, a profile that is gone, profiles that are loaded only after the dialog opened, the name written out and cut, cancelling, and the store's action); and by `central_highlight_profiles_are_taken_over_once` in `lib.rs`, one of the tests that need a PostgreSQL server, which was run against PostgreSQL 18 with the others. There a user's first start takes the profiles, and a choice for the user's own session but not for another user's shared one; an oversized profile is left where it is and counted; nothing that another user writes to the shared table afterwards arrives; and a first start on another machine, after that user has put sixteen profiles of the longest rules before all others, still reads the profile the user's own session names, fifteen of the sixteen, and no more than 16 MiB; and the time limit of the take-over's statements is gone after them. The full Rust and frontend suites pass on Windows, with clippy, rustfmt, eslint, prettier and `tsc` clean. The app itself was not started, so **My Highlighting**, the Settings hint and a terminal following a changed choice were not tried in a window, and the translations are the author's own and were not checked by native speakers. The limit on all that a take-over reads and on a profile's rules, the order it reads in, the time limit of its statements, choices for the user's own sessions only, the update of a profile alone for a session that is not there, a Bulk Edit that listed its sessions only after changing them, and the session tree taking keys behind the new dialog were found by the pre-commit review and audit of this change. Was listed under **Known issues** since the per-user mapping fix.
- **The application log could be written into a file someone else had put in its place, and a start of the app could hang on one.** The logging library's rolling writer opened whatever held the day's name (`shellstation.YYYY-MM-DD.log`). It followed a symbolic link there, so someone able to write to the log directory could have the log's lines appended to a file of the user's. It waited on a FIFO there: at the start of the app, before there was a window, or at midnight in the thread that writes the log. It created a missing file with the mode the umask left it on Unix, and with the access list of its directory on Windows, and it created a log directory that had been removed while the app ran with the umask's mode. All of this mattered only in a log directory that others can write to or read: on Unix one that could not be restricted to its owner, and on Windows one that was already there and lets others in. The app now opens the files itself, with a writer of its own (`app_log.rs`) in place of the library's; the library still provides the thread that writes the log.
  - **A new day's file is created for its owner alone,** as a session log is: by a call that fails when anything holds the name, as 0600 on Unix, and on Windows with an access list for the user alone, outside a network share.
  - **A file that is already there is appended to only if it is a plain file of the user's own.** A second start on the same day goes on in the day's file, so a file that is there has to be opened, which a session log never does. It is opened without following a link at its name and without waiting, and judged through the open handle, so that it is that file that is judged whatever the name holds a moment later. Refused are a symbolic link (on Windows any reparse point), a FIFO, with a reader or without one, a directory, a file that belongs to another user, and a file with a second name. A second name, because where a user can link another user's file, a file of the user's linked to the day's name would pass for the user's own. On Windows the owner may also be the administrators, who own what an elevated process creates; on a network share the owner is not looked at (see **Known issues**).
  - **A file that others could open is not appended to either,** on Unix. The day's file that an earlier version left with the umask's mode, usually 0644, is such a file at the first start of this one. Restricting it would not be enough: whoever has it open goes on reading what is appended, whatever its mode is by then. It is made read-only for its owner alone (0400), which takes it from everyone else, and a file its owner may not write to is refused, so it stays refused at the next start; the log of that day goes on under the next name. This is only done where the file system keeps modes. Where it has none, a mode says nothing of who can open a file: the change is refused or not kept as asked, what the system made of it is put back, and the file is appended to.
  - **A name that is refused is passed over.** The log then goes into `shellstation.YYYY-MM-DD_1.log`, and so on up to `_999`, each by the same rules, so that the next start on that day comes to the same file. A warning in the application log names the file that is written to and the one that could not be used, with the reason; at a start it is on stderr as well. A name counts as refused only when something holds it. An error that is not about the name ends the search at once: a directory that is missing, or cannot be searched or written to, a full disk. So does a search that has taken more than 2 s by the time it would go on to another name, since whoever holds a name can make its open take long, on Windows with a lock of theirs on a file of their own. A start that ends up without a file goes on without a log file, as before, with the reason on stderr.
  - **Two instances of the app** write to the same file of the day, as before. Where one creates it in the moment after the other found the name free, the other opens it in a second round, and does not pass the name over.
  - **At the change of day** the first write opens the new day's file, by the same rules. The log directory is treated as at a start: one that is gone by then is created again for its owner alone, and one that is there is restricted to its owner on Unix, without following a link at its name; one that cannot be restricted is still used, with a warning. Where the new day's file cannot be opened, the log stays in the file it has, so that no line is lost, says so once in a warning, and tries again the day after. The day only goes forward: a clock that is set back does not bring an earlier day's file back.
  - **The warning for a directory that could not be restricted** said that others may be able to read the application log. It now says that they may be able to list the directory, and to read the log where the file system has no Unix modes.
  - **What is unchanged.** The files' names, which are still dated in UTC, and that the app deletes none of them. On Windows a file that is already there keeps its access list: the day's file of the update in a directory that lets others in, and every file on a network share. Someone able to write to the log directory can still keep a day from having a log file by taking its names, and can delete or rename a file where the directory lets them; where it does, they can also move another file of the user's from a directory they can write to onto a day's name, which is then appended to, or made read-only if others could open it. Someone able to replace the log directory, or one above it, with a link still decides where the log goes. The open of the day's own name is still waited for, as the library's was: for a directory that does not answer, and on Windows for a lock on the file there, which the system breaks after about half a minute. An access control list can grant what a mode does not show. And where the file system gives every file to another user, each start begins a new file.
  - Covered by fourteen tests in `app_log.rs`: the names; a new file that is its owner's alone, by mode on Unix and by access list on Windows; a second start appending to the day's file; the first write of a new day going to a new file, and a clock set back not bringing the earlier one back; a name held by a directory passed over at the start and at the change of day, with the note of it, and the next start coming to the same file; a day with all thousand names taken, at the start and at the change of day, where the log stays in its file and the day is not tried again; a missing directory ending the search at the first name; a search that is out of time ending after the first name; a removed directory created again for its owner alone; a file with a hard link, a link to a file and a dangling link not written through; and, on Unix only, a directory that cannot be searched ending the search, a FIFO with a reader and one without neither waited on nor written to, and a file left at 0644 not appended to at two starts in a row and made 0400. In `lib.rs` the test for a chosen directory now also checks that the day's file is its owner's alone, and the test for a day's name taken by a directory now expects the name to be passed over where it expected no log file. The full Rust suite passes on Windows, where the test with symbolic links says that it cannot create one and ends early, and clippy, with the pedantic lints as well, and rustfmt are clean. The whole crate does not build on Linux from here, so `app_log.rs` and `private_fs.rs` were built in WSL in a crate of their own: all 22 of their tests pass there, with clippy and its pedantic lints clean. Taking out the flag that keeps a link from being followed, the check for a second name, the check for a plain file or the check for a file others could open makes the test for it fail, and an open that waits hangs the FIFO test. On a file system without modes, the Windows drive as WSL mounts it, a second start appended to the day's file and left it writable. On Windows, a file that belongs to another user, a reparse point and a file on a network share have no test, and neither have two instances that start in the same moment or a lock that makes an open take long; nothing was tried on macOS or on a file system that turns a mode into a read-only flag; the change of day was tested with a clock of the tests' own, not at a real midnight; and the app itself was not started. `just lint` also failed at a missing `;` in a test of `commands/session_login.rs`, which is added. Was listed under **Known issues** since the application log directory change. The file that others could open, the time limit of the search and the errors that end it, the second round for another instance, the directory at the change of day, the day going forward only, and a link never passing for a free name were found by the pre-commit review and audit of this change, as was what the new entry under **Known issues** has.
- **A right-click on the sidebar's search field opened no menu, so text could not be pasted there with the mouse.** The window suppresses the webview's own context menu, since it is a web page's (on Windows with Back, Refresh, Save as and Print, and a refresh closes every session; a development build adds the inspector), and a text field's usual menu went with it. That stays as it is. The search field now has a menu of the app's own, like the one of a session or a tab, with **Copy** and **Paste**, in all 14 languages.
  - **Copy** takes the field's selection and is greyed out without one. **Paste** puts the clipboard's text where the caret is, or over the selection, and the caret behind it. The text goes in as one line: line breaks around it, which a copied line brings along, are dropped, and each run of them inside becomes a space. A tab and the other control characters count as line breaks, and so does half of a character without its other half (an unpaired surrogate), so none of them gets into a query from the menu.
  - **The clipboard** is read and written through the clipboard plugin, as the terminal does, so the window is granted nothing new. It is read only on a click on Paste, not when the menu opens. A clipboard that cannot be read or written leaves the field as it is, without a message.
  - **The field takes 200 characters,** typed or pasted, counted as the webview counts them, in UTF-16 units. A clipboard can hold megabytes, and the backend searches by the first 200 bytes of a query and always has, so a longer one showed text that was not searched for. For a query in ASCII the two limits are the same. One with other characters in it, which take two to four bytes each, is still cut short by the backend before the field is full, as before. A paste from the menu puts in what there is room for, and reads no more of the clipboard than that takes.
  - **Dialogs.** The menu closes when a dialog opens, as the app's other menus do, and a paste whose clipboard answers after a dialog has opened is dropped, as text typed behind a dialog is. A menu asked for from the keyboard on something behind a dialog was stopped before the app saw it, but not refused, and the listener that suppresses the webview's menu, which is on the document for the bubble phase, was among what it no longer reached, so the webview may have shown its own there. It is now refused as well.
  - **What is left.** Every other text field is as before: no menu on a right-click, and a paste from the keyboard. A paste from the menu is not in the field's undo history, so Ctrl+Z does not take it back.
  - The field moved from `SessionSidebar.tsx` into the new `src/components/SearchField.tsx`, covered by `SearchField.test.tsx` (20 tests: the helper that makes one line of the clipboard's text, with line breaks, other control characters and unpaired surrogates, a long run of line breaks, the room there is, and a character of two units at its end; the menu and its two entries; Copy with and without a selection and with a clipboard that cannot be written; Paste at the caret, over a selection, into an empty field, as one line, up to the field's limit and not with half a character; an empty or unreadable clipboard; a dialog that opens before the clipboard answers or while the menu is open; a field that is gone by then; no menu from behind a dialog; Escape; and typing with the menu open). The test for a menu asked for from behind a dialog in `ContextMenu.test.tsx` now also checks the refusal, and that nothing is refused in the dialog on top or without one. The tests run under jsdom with the clipboard plugin mocked; no automated test runs the menu in the app itself. Pre-existing; found while using the app. The limit, the control characters, the helper for line breaks, which first used a pattern that took time by the square of a run's length, and several tests were found by the pre-commit review and audit of this change.
- **A session search with a backslash in it found the wrong sessions, and one with a NUL character failed or searched for something else.** The search matches a query anywhere in a session's name, host, user name and tags with `LIKE`, and writes the pattern's wildcards `%` and `_` with the escape character `\` before them, but did not write the escape character itself that way. A backslash in a query was therefore read as an escape of the character after it: a search for `CORP\admin`, as a user name with a domain is written, found a session of `CORPadmin` and not the one searched for, and a query ending in a backslash searched for a literal `%`. A backslash before a `%` or `_` did the opposite: it escaped the backslash the search had put there and left the wildcard a wildcard, so a query could have wildcards of its own, which the escaping is there to prevent. The pattern now takes all three characters as written, and has no wildcards but the two the search puts around the query.
  - **A NUL.** PostgreSQL refuses a parameter with a NUL in it, so such a search failed there, and the sidebar, which shows no error for a search, kept the results of the search before. SQLite reads a pattern up to its first NUL, so it searched for the part of the query before it. No text in the database has a NUL that a search could find, so a query with one now finds nothing, on both backends. A keyboard hardly types one; a clipboard can hold one.
  - The pattern is built in one place for both backends, the new `search_pattern` in `db/mod.rs`. Covered by three tests in `db/sqlite.rs`, the first tests there, against an in-memory SQLite database: the pattern itself, a search for each of the three characters and for a backslash before a wildcard, and a query with a NUL. All three fail with the escaping as it was. PostgreSQL has no automated test for this: the patterns before and after were tried by hand against PostgreSQL 18, with `ILIKE … ESCAPE` as the search statement has it, and behave as on SQLite; the refused NUL was not tried there, since no query with one reaches the server any more. Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit audit of the search field's menu.

### Changed

- **The release builds restore the crate cache only for their own `Cargo.lock`.** The four build jobs cache the crates cargo has downloaded (`~/.cargo/registry`, `~/.cargo/git`) under a key made of the platform and the hash of `Cargo.lock`, and fell back to the newest cache of the platform when there was none for that hash (`restore-keys`). What a build finds in its cargo home is compiled into the app that the job then signs, and cargo checks a crate against the lock file when it downloads it, not when it is there already. A cache written for another lock file, by whatever run wrote it last, was thus taken into a signed build, and saved again under the new key, so that it was carried on from lock file to lock file. The fallback is gone: a build restores the cache written for exactly its `Cargo.lock`, or none.
  - **What it costs:** a release whose `Cargo.lock` changed downloads its crates anew, once for each platform, unless the runner still has them. Nothing is compiled anew for it that was not before, since the cache holds what was downloaded and not what was built.
  - **What is left** is more than this takes away, and is listed under **Known issues**: the runners keep their cargo home between jobs, and a cache is still taken as its first run wrote it.
  - That cargo takes what is in its cargo home unchecked was tried with cargo 1.99 in a cargo home of its own: with `--locked --offline`, it compiled a crate whose unpacked source had been changed, and one unpacked from a `.crate` file that had been replaced, without a word about the checksum in the lock file. The workflow was not run with the change. Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit audit of the pinned actions.
- **The release workflow's checkouts no longer leave their token in the checkout.** The `checkout` action by default writes the token it fetched the repository with into the checkout's git config, so that git commands later in the job can use it. Everything a job runs after the checkout could read it there: `npm ci` with the install scripts of the packages, and the build with the build scripts of the crates. No job of the workflow uses git after its checkout, and the repository has no submodules and no dependency fetched by git, so all seven checkouts now have `persist-credentials: false`. It is the job's own token that this keeps out of the git config, not the signing key, which the build step still has in its environment, nor the release tokens. Whether that token is out of a step's reach altogether is not settled by it (see **Known issues**). The workflow was not run with the change. Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit audit of the pinned actions.
- **The release workflow no longer pastes the tag and the file names into what it sends to Forgejo, and keeps its token and the server's answers to itself.** The step that publishes the Forgejo release built the JSON body of the request that creates the release, and two URLs, by putting the tag and each file's name into a string. A tag with a quote in it gave a body that is no JSON, and an `&`, `#` or `?` in a tag or a file name ended the value in the URL where it stood, so the request asked for something else than was meant. The body is now written by `json.dumps` and the values in the URLs are percent-encoded, both by the `python3` the step before already uses, started with `-I` so that it loads nothing from the checkout.
  - **A file's path is handed to `curl` in quotes,** with a backslash before a quote or a backslash in it. In the file name of a form field, `curl` reads a `;` or a `,` as its own syntax, so a file with either in its name was not uploaded, or not as what it is.
  - **The token is no longer among `curl`'s arguments,** where other users of a runner can read them while a request runs. The step writes the `Authorization` header to a file and has `curl` read it from there, which `curl` can since 7.55. The token is still in the environment of the step and of what it starts, as before.
  - **What the step keeps on disk is in a directory of its own,** made by `mktemp -d` for its user alone, with a `umask` that keeps the files in it to that user as well, and removed when the step ends: that header and the server's answers. A step that is killed outright does not get to remove it, and leaves the header in that directory. The answer to an upload, which is printed when the upload fails, went to the fixed name `/tmp/upload-body`, where someone else on a shared runner could have put a link.
  - Only a maintainer pushes a tag, and the file names are the build's own, so the pasted values made for a release that fails in an odd way at worst, not a way in for anyone. A tag that is the app's version, as the pipeline now requires of every tag but a throwaway one, has none of these characters.
  - **What is left:** the step still finds an asset that is there already by looking for its name in the server's JSON as text, which counts on that JSON being compact and misses a name with a quote, a backslash or a character outside ASCII in it; the upload then fails for the name being taken. A `/` in a tag is sent encoded, which a proxy in front of Forgejo may refuse. No file the build names and no tag that is a version has any of this. The step that publishes the GitHub release passes the tag to `gh` as an argument of its own and was not changed.
  - The step's script was run as it stands in the workflow file, with the real `curl` against a stand-in for the Forgejo API that records what it receives, on Linux (bash 5.3, `curl` 8.18, under WSL) and on Windows (Git Bash, `curl` 8.22). The runs: a plain release; tags with a quote and a backslash, with `&`, `=`, `#`, `?` and a space, and with a `/`; file names with `&`, `=`, `#`, `+`, `%` and a space, with `;`, `,` and `=`, with a quote and backslashes, one that ends in a backslash, and one written to leave the quotes and add an option (`x";type=text;filename=y`); a release and a file that are there already; and an upload the server refuses. Each time the server was asked for exactly that tag and those names, got each file whole, and was sent the token, which was in no argument of `curl` and in no output, and nothing was left on disk. On Linux all eleven runs pass, and the step as it was before fails checks in every one of them. On Windows the three file names with a quote or a backslash cannot be tried, since the file system takes none. Neither Forgejo nor the pipeline was run. Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit audit of the release tag check, and the token, the form field and the checkout's Python by the audit of this change.
- **The release workflow names its actions by commit.** `.forgejo/workflows/release.yml` used `checkout@v4`, `cache@v4`, `upload-artifact@v3` and `download-artifact@v3`. Such a tag can be moved to other code by whoever controls the action's repository, and that code would then run in the build jobs, which hold the key the updates are signed with, and in the publish job, which holds the release tokens. Each use now names the commit its tag pointed to when this was written (`checkout` v4.4.0, `cache` v4.3.0, `upload-artifact` v3.2.1, `download-artifact` v3.0.2), with the release in a comment, so the jobs run the code they ran before. It says which code runs, not that it is sound, and it covers the actions alone: not the npm packages and crates a build runs, nor the tools the runner brings, and whoever can push a `v*` tag still runs that commit's workflow file with the secrets; `docs/DESIGN.md` §4.7.6 lists these. `docs/DEVELOPMENT.md` has how to move to a newer release, which nothing does by itself any more. The commits were looked up in the actions' repositories, where each is the commit of the release named and of the moving tag; the workflow was not run with them, and that Forgejo's runner takes a full commit in place of a tag was not tried here, so the next tag pushed, best a throwaway one, is the first run. Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit audit of the release tag check.
- **The updater now refuses an update whose signature does not name the version it was signed for** (`plugins.updater.requireSignedVersion` in `tauri.conf.json`). The update manifest is not signed, only the artifact is, so someone able to replace `latest.json` could announce a higher version number with the download and signature of an older release, which is genuine and validly signed, and so put an outdated build back onto a machine. The Tauri CLI this project builds with writes the app version into each signature, where the signature covers it, and the updater plugin already refused an artifact whose signed version is not the announced one. A signature that names no version, as the ones of releases signed before the CLI recorded it, passed for any version until now.
  - **For a release:** the version in `tauri.conf.json`, which the build signs for, must be the version of the tag, which the manifest announces; a release where they differ is one no installed app updates to. `docs/RELEASING.md` says so in the step that bumps the version, and `docs/DEVELOPMENT.md` where it has a throwaway tag pushed to try the pipeline, whose manifest no app would take. An artifact signed by hand needs `tauri signer sign --app-version`.
  - **What it does not change:** an app from before this version does not ask for a signed version, and updates to this one as before; it is open to such a manifest until it has. The check is the plugin's own, at the download, and fails like an invalid signature: nothing is installed, and the dialog says that the update failed. An older release offered under its own version is still an update to an app older than it, so a replaced manifest can hold such an app at a release that has since been superseded, though not take it back below the one it runs. Rolling a release back by making an earlier one "Latest" works as before: an app with this setting is only offered releases newer than itself, which the build has signed with their version.
  - **The release pipeline now refuses a tag that is not the app's version.** A new step of the `preflight` job in `.forgejo/workflows/release.yml` compares the tag, without a leading `v`, with the version in `tauri.conf.json`, and fails the release before anything is built if they are not the same string, or if no single version can be read there. A release tagged without the version bump used to publish normally and be one no installed app would update to, with every app showing only that the update failed. A tag that ends in `-test`, or in `-test` and a number, is not compared, so the throwaway tags that `docs/DEVELOPMENT.md` has pushed to try the pipeline still work; no app takes such a release as an update, and `docs/DEVELOPMENT.md` now says not to leave one published, since the installed apps find no update while GitHub serves it as the latest. The comparison is made once, for the commit the tag names when `preflight` runs; a tag moved to another commit before the build jobs check it out is not compared again, and the app refuses what comes of that. The step's script was run locally, as it stands in the workflow file, against a matching tag, a forgotten bump, a tag that is a prefix of the version or has a suffix, three throwaway tags, four tags with `test` in them that are none, a tag with a command substitution in it, and configs with CRLF line endings, a pre-release version, no version, a version that is none and two version lines. The pipeline itself was not run. Recommended by the pre-commit review and audit of the setting, which also found the first exemption, any tag with `-test` in it, too wide.
  - Not tried against a published release. That `tauri build` signs with the version was read in the source of the CLI version the lock file pins (2.12.4); the signatures of the releases published so far were not looked at, and no app with this setting has updated yet. Pre-existing; found by the pre-commit audit of the update install fix.
- Command bar buttons now read three-digit octal codes for control characters, `\000` to `\037` and `\177`, as SecureCRT's send strings do: `\003` sends Ctrl+C, `\033` ESC. This lets imported SecureCRT buttons keep their commands as written (see **Added**), and gives the command bar every control character, which it could not send before.
  - Codes for printable characters, `\040` to `\176`, are not read and are sent as written. A command spelled in them would show as digits while typing text no one reviewed: `\162\155` would type `rm`.
  - A code above `\177`, an escaped backslash before digits (`\\033`) and fewer than three digits are sent as written too.
  - The free-form input bar reads the codes as a button does, as it already read the other escapes.
  - **A button that sent a backslash and a control character's octal code as text,** such as `printf '\033[1m'` meant for the remote shell's own `printf`, now sends the character instead. Write `\\033` to keep the text.
  - The escape list in the button dialog has a new line for the codes, in all 14 languages.
- The minimum Rust version is now declared: 1.94, as `rust-version` in `src-tauri/Cargo.toml`. It is what sqlx 0.9 needs, the highest minimum the locked dependencies declare, so it has been the real minimum since 0.30.0. Cargo already refused an older toolchain through sqlx's own requirement, and now names ShellStation's. Clippy now knows the minimum too: it flags a standard library API newer than it, and no longer suggests one. `docs/DEVELOPMENT.md` says so as well. How long a path chosen in a backend dialog stays good for saving is written as `Duration::from_mins(10)` again, which needs 1.91. Two lints that clippy 1.95 reports under `just lint` and 1.99 does not are fixed, so that the lint passes on both: the decline cooldown's cap is `Duration::from_hours(1)`, and a doc comment names `SQLite` in backticks. `docs/DESIGN.md` §8 lists sqlx as 0.9+.
- `quick-xml` 0.41.0 → 0.42.0. It now hands over names, text and entity references as strings rather than bytes, so the tag comparisons in `import/mremoteng.rs` and `import/securecrt.rs` compare strings, and reading text no longer has a decoding step that could fail. Both importers already parse from a string, so the new up-front UTF-8 check changes nothing for them. The release's panic and resource-limit fixes are in namespace handling, the serde deserializer and `Attributes::new`, none of which the importers use. `plist`, through Tauri, still uses 0.41.0, so both versions are in the tree. The existing importer tests pass unchanged.
- `base64` 0.22 → 0.23, the version Tauri 2.12 and its updater plugin already use (see **Fixed**), so no new copy is added. The standard engine the app encodes with is the scalar one, as before. 0.23 also brings SIMD engines, which the updater plugin's default features compile in; the app does not use them.
- `dirs` 6 → 7. Its one change, `preference_dir` on Windows, is a function the app does not call: it uses `config_dir` and `home_dir`, so the database and config stay where they are. Tauri 2.12 already uses 7, so `dirs` 6 drops out.
- Within their existing version ranges: `serde_json` 1.0.151 → 1.0.154, `sha2` 0.11.0 → 0.11.1, `uuid` 1.24.0 → 1.29.0 and `zeroize` 1.9.0 → 1.9.1.
- The SecureCRT and mRemoteNG import commands now log at `info` when they start (with the payload size) and finish (folder, session, skipped and warning counts), and at `warn` when parsing fails — as a fixed message, because quick-xml errors can quote fragments of the file (tag names, an unrecognized entity name inside an attribute value); the full error still reaches the UI. Previously an import left no trace in the app log at any level, so an import that never reached the backend was indistinguishable from one that did. No hostnames, usernames or session names are logged.
- The SecureCRT and mRemoteNG imports now reject a file with more than 10,000 folders or 50,000 sessions, the limits the JSON database import already enforced. The 100 MB file cap alone allowed hundreds of thousands of entries, each a database write. The check runs after parsing and before anything is written, so a rejected file leaves no partial import. The two constants in `commands/database.rs` are now `pub(crate)` and shared rather than duplicated.
- Both XML parsers now stop as soon as a file passes 10,000 folders, 50,000 sessions, 50,000 warnings or 50,000 dropped sessions, instead of building every entry before the count check sees them. A 100 MB file holds millions of minimal entries (`<Node Type="Container" Name="f"/>`), and every dropped session or malformed element (a SecureCRT `<string>` without a `name`) collected a warning string, with no limit; a crafted file could make the parse hold several times its own size in memory. Warnings share the session limit because each session dropped on its own produces exactly one. The list of dropped sessions kept for jump host resolution shares it as well, as a count of its own: one warning covers a whole dropped subtree, however many sessions it held.
- The app log level from the config file is now validated before it goes into the log filter. Only ShellStation's own targets get the configured level, so third-party crates such as `sqlx` (which logs SQL statements) and `russh` stay off at every level — but the value was spliced into the filter string unchecked, and a hand-edited `"level": "trace,sqlx=trace"` would have switched them on. It is now lowercased (a hand-edited `"DEBUG"` keeps working, as it always did) and checked against `error`/`warn`/`info`/`debug`/`trace`, falling back to `info` with a message on stderr. The "file logging enabled" startup line now reports the level in effect rather than the raw config value. `RUST_LOG` still overrides the config unvalidated, as a deliberate developer setting; `docs/DEVELOPMENT.md` gained a **Verbose logging** section that says what turning on third-party targets writes to the log file.
- The application log now treats a log directory the user chose as the session logger treats its own, and as it treated the default one already. On Linux and macOS a chosen directory that is already there is restricted to its owner (0700) at each start with application logging on, and a missing one is created as 0700, with any directories above it that are missing too. It used to be created with the default umask, and one already there was left alone. The logging library creates the log file itself, with the mode the umask leaves it, which usually lets every user of the machine read it, so the directory is what keeps the application log from them.
  - **A directory that cannot be restricted** is still used: one that belongs to another user, one on a file system without Unix modes, one the user cannot read, or one with a symbolic link at its name (see below). That is reported on stderr, as it was for the default directory, and now as a warning in the application log itself as well, since stderr is seldom where a user looks; both add that others may be able to read the application log. The log file in such a directory was as open as the umask made it, unlike a session log, which on Unix is created for its owner alone wherever it is (see **Fixed**); the entry on the application log's files under **Fixed** closes that, and rewords the warning. The session logger, which said nothing when it could not restrict its directory, now says so for each session, at `info`: others may see the names of the logs there, but cannot open them. That line needs application logging to be on, at `info` or a more verbose level; with it off, nothing records it.
  - **A link at the directory's name is not followed.** Restricting a directory by its path follows a symbolic link there. A log directory can sit where others can write. Someone able to put a link at its name, or to swap one in between the create and the restriction, could have had a directory of the user's elsewhere restricted to 0700, with its setgid bit dropped. Both logs therefore open their directory first, by a call that refuses a link at its name and anything that is not a directory, and restrict it through that handle (`restrict_dir_to_owner_nofollow` in `private_fs.rs`). A separator at the end of the path, which a directory from the config file can have, is dropped first: with one, the system follows a link at the name whatever the call asks. A directory that belongs to another user is refused there as well, which matters when the app runs as root, who could change its mode. This goes for the session logger too, which restricted by path until now, and for the default directory of both logs: only the user can put a link there, but only as long as the config directory around it could be restricted itself.
